## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accredited Entities are required to submit all annexes that were provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the updated version of the funding proposal.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Materials exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under the GCF’s Openness and Transparency Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements that may apply. Please refer to Section G of the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.

## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 1. As a Small Island Developing State (SIDS) and the only Least Developed Country (LDC) in the Western Hemisphere, Haiti is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change.

This vulnerability is compounded by its high population density, with over 90% living within 5 km of the coast — with approximately 59% of Haitians living under the national poverty line of US$2.41 per day and approximately 38% living on less than $1.90 per day.

### 2. Haiti is particularly affected by the climate change impacts of increasing frequency and intensity of floods, tropical storms and hurricanes. Between 1975 and 2012, such climate-related events resulted in

damages equivalent to 2% of the country's GDP on average per year. In 2004, one hurricane — Hurricane Jeanne — caused severe flooding in northern Haiti. The flooding caused by this storm resulted in approximately $15 million in damages and losses. In 2010, another major hurricane — Hurricane Tomas — caused $92 million of cost that muddled the drainage networks and infrastructure across the country. Since 2012, the impact of hurricanes has been even more severe. For example, in September 2016, Hurricane Matthew struck Haiti as a category five storm — Hurricane Matthew — that struck Haiti in 2016 caused widespread damages and economic losses and negatively impacted the livelihoods of over one million people across the country. Specifically, Hurricane Matthew resulted in the deaths of 546 people, forced more than 378,000 individuals to evacuate their homes, and left approximately 900,000 people without access to clean water. The storm also displaced nearly 1.2 million people requiring immediate humanitarian assistance. The storm also inflicted economic damages estimated at $5 billion. In addition, the hurricane caused widespread flooding in the country, resulting in up to 8% of crops and livestock in some areas of the country. In addition, it caused a sharp increase in the number of malaria cases across the country. This is not an isolated case; other hurricanes such as Hurricane Matthew also leads to severe flooding, which exacerbates these negative impacts.

3. Projections under the RCP8 scenario suggest that climatic conditions contributing to flooding incidence are expected to worsen over time due to climate change. The projected increase in sea level and temperature, as well as warming temperatures, contribute to major storm development and exacerbate flood impacts. Furthermore, the projected increase in precipitation intensity will also lead to more frequent and severe floods. Such changes will be experienced in the future. A such watershed management actions have been prioritized within Haiti’s National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and are being implemented by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR). The Térrèn River (TR) watershed is located. The TR watershed is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, flooding and landslides because it covers a larger area (see section 10.5 of the F3 on its prioritization).

4. Although the TR watershed is not the largest watershed in the country, it is one of the most impacted by climate change. The population living within the TR watershed is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. This watershed is prone to frequent flooding in the municipalities it crosses, with adverse effects particularly on poor communities who do not have sufficient resources and capacity to adapt to these changes. The municipalities that are located along the Térrèn River (Térrèn, Gose, Bassin Bleu, Chérondes, and Port-de-Paix) — are home to 80% of Haiti’s population of extreme poverty. This is a significant proportion of the country’s poor people who live in conditions of average or even worse than average living standards. The municipality of Port-au-Prince has an extremely high poverty rate (59%), which is more than double that of the national average, and is one of the most vulnerable to climate impacts. The impacts of extreme climate events are being compounded by ecosystem degradation caused by deforestation, overgrazing, soil erosion, and land abandonment. These factors have led to a situation where the population has little capacity for adaptation, particularly the practice of leaving soil bare and exposed to extreme weather for much of the year.

5. Current agricultural practices, such as the increasing prevalence of annual crop utilization, leave the soil bare during the dry season (October to May). This is because the majority of farmers in the watershed rely on rain-fed agriculture. The lack of ground cover leads to increased surface runoff and consequently exacerbates soil erosion in the watershed. This increases sedimentation in water bodies and reduces the capacity of these water bodies to retain agricultural land. The negative feedback loop created is further compounded by extensive deforestation in the mountainous areas that surround the TR watershed, which has led to a significant loss of forest cover for farmers in the TR watershed reporting to charcoal production as an alternative livelihood in response to low yields from agriculture and lack of access to markets. This has resulted in increased soil erosion and sedimentation in water bodies. The native forests have been reduced by 90% or more, and the remaining areas are highly fragmented. Given that the deforestation rate is still high and is unlikely to decrease in the foreseeable future, this situation will likely continue to drive deforestation in the absence of alternative livelihood options.

6. Unsustainable land use practices such as slash-and-burn agriculture in the watershed, where large-scale removal of tree cover for farming and charcoal production has decreased infiltration capacity of the soil, increased flood frequency and intensity in the TR watershed, where floods wash away fertile soils, cause sedimentation in water bodies, and lead to loss of agricultural infrastructure. The deforestation of embankments and leaves in the watershed, this cycle intensifies future flooding, leading to further destruction of crops, farmland and agricultural infrastructure, as well as the loss of livestock. Moreover, the erosion of

A

riverbank erosion and sedimentation build up in rivers caused by flooding decreases water quality in the watershed considerably, leading to an increased risk of water- and vector-borne diseases.

7. At present, the Government of Haiti (Goth) has limited technical and financial capacity to fully address the impacts of climate change-induced floods on the TR watershed and its population living nearby. This is highlighted by the fact that more than half of Haiti's national budget is accounted for by foreign aid. The existing institutional framework in Haiti is not well suited to addressing climate change-induced flood events, which constrains the Goth's capacity to invest in climate change adaptation. Consequently, the Goth is requesting GCF funding to support a project aimed at addressing climate change-induced flood impacts on the watershed and population living nearby.

This application proposes an integrated approach to addressing climate change-induced flood impacts on the watershed and population living nearby. This approach will be implemented through three main components: (1) strengthening institutional capacity at the national and local levels towards an integrated approach to land and water resources management in the watershed; (2) implementing agroforestry systems and refreshing degraded landscapes at priority intervention sites in the TR watershed, while simultaneously promoting a shift toward the adoption and implementation of a climate-resilient agricultural model for sustainable use and management of water resources over the long term. This transformative approach will contribute to reducing flood risks by improving land cover and soil conservation; (3) strengthening institutional capacity at the national and local levels; and its establishing the required governance framework for integrated water resource management in Haiti, while simultaneously promoting a shift towards the adoption and implementation of a climate-resilient agricultural model for sustainable use and management of water resources over the long term. This transformative approach will contribute to reducing flood risks by improving land cover and soil conservation.

8. Overall, the project will confer direct and indirect adaptation benefits to approximately 292,650 and 441,272 individuals living in priority areas within the TR watershed while providing the benefit of mitigating an estimated 3,636,750 CO2-e during the project's total 25-year lifespan.

# PROJECT-PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

### Context

**Haiti forms part of the Caribbean island Hispaniola, sharing a border with the Dominican Republic to the east. Historically, Haiti has been one of the poorest countries in the world, at just 27.746 km². Haiti is the third-largest country in the Caribbean. As a Small Island Developing State (SIDS) and the only Least Developed Country (LDC) in the Caribbean region, Haiti faces unique challenges related to climate change and natural disasters such as hurricanes, landslides and droughts**. Many of the country’s primary economic activities* (including agricultural production and tourism) are highly vulnerable to these impacts.

### Baseline scenario

11. Haiti is a mountainous nation, with more than 50% of the island at a slope angle >40°. More white cities in Haiti are located on the coastal plains, much of the most productive land is located along steep streams. As a result, the country has experienced significant soil erosion and sedimentation problems. The soils are intrinsically fragile, comprised of new formed shallow soils that can support forests and grasslands but that are easily eroded by rainfall. In addition to the topography, Haiti’s climate is characterized by high temperatures, heavy rainfall during the wet season (June–November), and frequent droughts in the dry season (December–May). These climatic conditions, combined with the steep topography of the country, increases erosion along the mountainside slopes of Haiti. For example, in 1995, severe flooding occurred on the island of Hispaniola, which caused significant damage to infrastructure and agriculture.

12. The conditions mentioned above are most evident in the River Tres Ríos (TR) watershed— one of the largest and most productive watersheds in the country. The TR watershed, which includes the Arbona, Nord (northwest), and Artibonite rivers, covers an area of 1023 km². The river originates from the Massif du Nord mountain range and Antelope Departments. The TR watershed has steep longitudinal slopes, with a mean slope gradient of approximately 5% in the upper basin and 4% in the lower basin. Its discharge site into the Atlantic Ocean. The TR watershed was particularly important to the country in that it provides critical ecosystem goods and services including aquifer recharge, as well as contributing to food security through agriculture.

* A. Bélizier. The Implications of Coping Strategies for Nitrogen Flows in Haiti. Final Report. 2009. Providence: Brown Centre for Environmental Studies.

# Climate Rationale

## 13. As a Caribbean SIDS and LDC, Haiti is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The impact of climate change-induced flooding has been significant.

### 14. These factors have led to the waterlogging becoming one of the most densely populated parts of Haiti, with an estimated population of approximately 20 million people living on less than 5% of the country's land area. This high population density in a vulnerable environment has numerous opportunities for livelihood security and economic growth; however, it is also among the most vulnerable regions to flooding caused by intense, concentrated rainfall and storms.

### 15. Limited data is available on the historical extent and frequency of floods in Haiti. However, given the nature of watersheds in Haiti – including the targeted TR water source – major hurricanes generally result in extensive flooding. The number of people affected by these events has increased significantly compared to previous years (period: 1959–2012). This illustrates an increase in the frequency of extreme weather in the Atlantic basin, and therefore the corresponding vulnerability of Haiti over the last 30 years.

### 16. Impacts from flooding after storms and hurricanes have become increasingly more frequent since 2004, especially in September 2010, for example: Hurricane Jeanne caused flooding by led to widespread destruction of crops as gravel was deposited onto the farmers' fields. As a result, approximately 80% of the people affected were small-scale subsistence farmers and their families.

### 17. The deposited gravel also made cultivation difficult for all affected areas in the aftermath of the storm. Thousands of people were displaced by the flooding caused by Hurricane Jeanne, which resulted in more than 250 deaths and approximately US$43 million in damages and the need to remove approximately 500,000 m³ of sand that could damage drainage networks.

### 18. Such adverse impacts of climate change on the livelihoods of vulnerable communities are already being experienced frequently in Haiti. During the period 1993–2012, for example: Haiti experienced 31 floods* of which, approximately 75% were associated with tropical storms and hurricanes.

### 19. Combined, these flood events caused the deaths of approximately 2,870 people and adversely affected approximately 4 million people in Haiti during this period (period: 1993–2012). The most recent example was Hurricane Jeanne causing flooding in the northern part of Haiti that resulted in more than 2,800 deaths, approximately US$41 million in damages and the need to remove approximately 500,000 m³ of sand that could damage drainage networks.

### 20. The Caribbean region is one of the most vulnerable regions in the world to climate change and has been identified as non-compliant/unacceptable for category 3a hurricanes originating in the Atlantic basin.

infrastructural damage across the country[1]. The impacts of Hurricane Jeanne exacerbated the effects of torrential rains experienced earlier in May of the same year, which caused the death or disappearance of more than 4,000 people in Haiti.

In 2008, four tropical storms—each having two hurricane-level waves—hit Haiti in a period of less than 30 days, with significant impacts on the TRI watershed. These storms included: i) Tropical Storm Fay, August 15–16; ii) Hurricane Hanna, August 24–27; iii) Tropical Storm Ike, September 8–9; and iv) Tropical Storm Juan, October 30–November 1.

The combined impact of these four successive storms was immense. With 7.9% of the population, or nearly 600,000 people living in the TRI watershed, the economic losses were estimated at US$254 million[2]. The agricultural sector was particularly affected: Approximately 189,950 hectares of agricultural land was impacted, with only ~10,310 ha considered damage-free. This represents a loss of approximately $67.5 million in crop production and livestock losses.

The impacts of the 2007 hurricane season, which resulted in breaches of waterways that facilitated the entry of water into the country’s urban areas, were also exacerbated by the four tropical storms in 2008[3]. The combined impact of these events, with the resultant loss of agricultural production, has been estimated at US$125.650.000. In 2010, Matthew, Gonzaga, and Erika caused further damage to Haiti’s infrastructure and agriculture.

The economic losses from natural disasters in 2010 are estimated at more than 22% of the country's GDP that year, including the losses of up to 90% of total crop production. The agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable because it represents a significant share of the economy (approximately 35%) and employs approximately one-third of the population.

Since 2010, Haiti has experienced more than 820 cases of cholera[4] recorded in Haiti since 2010. Given that approximately 35% of the population lives in areas with poor sanitation infrastructure (e.g., lack of access to clean water and adequate sanitation services), the population of Haiti is extremely vulnerable to these diseases and their spread which is aggravated by increased rainfall.

The impacts of intense rainfall, both from tropical storms and torrential rainfall, have had numerous knock-on effects on the country’s ecosystems by compounding the already high rate of soil erosion and degradation, and contributing to a general decline in forest cover. The combination of deforestation, land use change (e.g., conversion of forests for agriculture), and inappropriate cultivation, causing an increase in on-fall, a decrease in soil water infiltration, and therefore runoff, has led to increased sedimentation in rivers and lakes[5]. This is further exacerbated by the combined effects of torrential rainfall and the loss of forest systems—in combination with increasing average temperatures—are consequently contributing to the decline in river flow. The decline in river flow is expected to continue as a result of decreasing sedimentation potential. In the absence of soil conservation structures or protective vegetation cover on cropland, this will lead to increased runoff and erosion, which will further increase sedimentation in rivers and increase the rate of desertification.

Given that over 60% of the country has hillsides, and where more than 20% of the population is dependent upon agriculture for their livelihoods, the soil on these slopes are directly linked to the food security of Haiti’s population.

While most of Haiti is at risk it to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding, the country's TRI watershed has been identified as a particularly vulnerable area. The vulnerability assessment was conducted in 2013 and updated from 1981. Further details on the projected changes in storm intensity and frequency are contained in Section 3 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study. This section also contains further details on the projected changes in temperature and rainfall patterns under the RCP4.5 and 8.5 scenarios, presented below.

Climate change risks

The TRI watershed is closely linked with intense, concentrated seasonal rainfall (April–June and August–October), storm events and warm temperatures. The high intensity of rainfall during an extreme storm event results in a rapid increase in runoff volume and sediment load. This has been identified as the most significant climate-related factor contributing to flooding, and hurricane development are expected to become more prevalent in Haiti if 95% or 40 and 80% of the land area is deforested by 2100[6]. The projected changes in temperature and rainfall patterns affecting Haiti will be experienced in the future[7]. While detailed data on historical flood impacts and trends is not readily available.

## Temperature

The mean annual daytime temperature for the Caribbean region shows an increase of 0.19°C per decade during the period 1981–2010. This is smaller than the increase in mean night-time temperatures of 0.28°C per decade on average over this same time period.

Under the RCP4 scenario, maximum increases in temperature of 1.57°C and 1.87°C are expected to occur in 2025 and 2035 respectively, with a further increase of up to 2.69°C by 2050 (See Figure S2 Section 4.1). Under the RCP8 scenario? Additionally, an analysis of temperature trends for Haiti from the period 1901–2013 reveals that maximum temperatures have increased at a rate of approximately 0.7°C per decade since 1901.

Under the RCP8 scenario, maximum temperatures are projected to increase by 1.57°C and 2.33°C respectively. Furthermore, under this scenario, the frequency of extreme high temperature events is expected to increase significantly with current extreme temperatures likely to become the new norm (See FS Section S2.1).

## Sea level

Haiti's First National Communication to the United Nations Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) also reports that, under the RCP8 scenario, the period 2030–2049 is expected to be up to 6% higher in the Haiti, the period 2050-2079 is projected to increase by approximately 10%, and for the period 2080-2100 it is projected to increase by over 75W–TZW, 17W–214W it is 0.7°C per decade, for a total warming of 0.7°C over the period 1982–201.

## Historical data

Historical satellite observations from 1981 to the present have demonstrated an increase in annual rainfall in the Caribbean region (See Figure S2 Section 4.3). The projected changes for precipitation are consistent with these future projections: A projection shift in the precipitation change is also observed, with a projected decrease in the maximum extent of extreme low rainfall events (≤50 mm per day) by approximately 10% to 15%, indicating that daily rainfall extremes are becoming more frequent.

## Hydrological changes

Compared with baseline (1981–2010) flood flow simulations, future projections using four climate models (CCSM4, CanESM2, GFDL-RCM3, and MRI-CGCM3) indicate a mid-century (2050) daily maximum flood flow followed by a significant increase in daily maximum flood flow in the late century (2081–2100). These projections were analyzed under the RCP4 scenario. The projected changes for daily maximum flow, also exhibiting a mid-century decrease and later century increase. Similar to the results of the future projections for daily maximum flow, the future projections for the maximum extent of extreme low rainfall events (≤50 mm per day) indicate decreasing trends through the mid-century, before increasing in the late century. The exception to this is the forecasted changes under the RCP8 scenario, which show an increase in both the maximum extent and frequency of extreme low rainfall events.

Furthermore, for the 100-year flood event, the following changes in the maximum extent of extreme high rainfall events (≥350 mm per day) are projected: a decrease by approximately 2% to 4% under the mid- to late-century. Furthermore, for the 100-year flood event, the following changes in the maximum extent of extreme low rainfall events (≤50 mm per day) indicate decreasing trends through the mid-century, before increasing in the late century.

Given the overall steepness of the gradient of the landscape in the TR watershed, even small flows are enough to mobilize large volumes of sediment. However, in the flood flow mode results summarized above, the bed and bank erosion rates were found to be low (see Section 3.4.1). This is because the volume of water was not sufficient to erode the bed and banks. In addition, the relatively small volume of sediment added from tributaries to the mainstream river would be sufficient to reduce local channel capacity.

Climate change impacts and vulnerability

The impacts of climate change-induced flooding in the TR watershed are exacerbated by the negative feedback between increased runoff and reduced vegetation cover as described in the watershed. Current agricultural practices, such as the increasing prevalence of annual crop cultivation, leave the soil bare and exposed to extreme weather for most of the year. This results in a significant increase in surface runoff during heavy rainfall events which leads to increased surface runoff and consequently exacerbates soil erosion in the watershed. This scenario is compounded by vegetation loss due to climate change-induced droughts and heatwaves. The combination of these factors increases the demand for charcoal in Halls in high and is unlikely to decrease in the foreseeable future, with many farmers in the TR watershed having already been forced into debt as a result.

With increasing temperatures, there will be an increase in evaporation rates from water bodies and soil. This results in unexpected expenses (particularly in extremely wet or dry years). Given this high demand, charcoal production will further exacerbate the problem of deforestation in the TR watershed. In addition, the loss of vegetation cover has decreased infiltration capacity of the soil and contributed to increased surface runoff during heavy rainfall events. The combination of these factors results in a significant increase in sedimentation rates which can wash away fertile soils and cause sedimenting of riverbeds and blocking drainage infrastructure. Given the increasing frequency and intensity of flooding, this will result in further erosion of the banks and bed of the river, leading to the destruction of crops, farmland, and agricultural infrastructure, as well as the loss of livestock and human settlements.

The combination of these factors results in a significant increase in sedimentation rates which can wash away fertile soils and cause sedimenting of riverbeds and blocking drainage infrastructure. Given the increasing frequency and intensity of flooding, this will result in further erosion of the banks and bed of the river, affecting the biodiversity, ecological, economic, environmental and social conditions already affected by climate change.

While frequent and intense flooding, soil erosion and sedimentation of rivers have considerable adverse impacts on the landscape and agriculture in the TR watershed, they also have severe consequences for water resources and human settlements. The combination of these factors results in a significant increase in sedimentation rates which can wash away fertile soils and cause sedimenting of riverbeds and blocking drainage infrastructure.

Without urgent intervention in the management of land and water resources, the vulnerable population of the TR region in Hail will continue to become increasingly at risk if flooding and the associated negative impacts on the environment are not managed effectively. This is likely to result in a significant increase in sedimentation rates which can wash away fertile soils and cause sedimenting of riverbeds and blocking drainage infrastructure.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

## Baseline projects, programmes and initiatives

### 30 Considerable baseline investments are being made through public expenditure and donor-funded initiatives to ensure that the project area is well positioned for the proposed GCF project interventions.

#### Key elements of these investments include:

- **Improved water management:** This includes the construction of small-scale irrigation systems and reservoirs.
- **Enhanced flood risk management:** Measures such as the construction of embankments, levees, and other infrastructure to protect against flooding on the TR watershed in Haiti. An integrated approach to flood management must take into account the environmental impacts of these interventions.

#### The proposed project interventions are designed to complement several of the most relevant baseline investments that have been made or are being implemented by public authorities and donors. The project will not duplicate or adversely affect these ongoing initiatives (refer to Section 6 in Annex 2 for a full list). Instead, it will build on them and enhance their effectiveness.

#### The proposed project interventions will also address the most relevant baseline investments that have been made or are being implemented by public authorities and donors. These include:

- **Improved water management:** This includes the construction of small-scale irrigation systems and reservoirs.
- **Enhanced flood risk management:** Measures such as the construction of embankments, levees, and other infrastructure to protect against flooding on the TR watershed in Haiti.

### 31 USAID has committed to providing US$40 million for the Half Replenishment Project (2018–2025) to reduce the threat of deforestation and increase tree cover in and around targeted areas in the northern parts of the country. The project will focus on increasing the availability, affordability, and consumption of nutritious foods and services in targeted forest areas in Haiti to enhance food security of surrounding communities.

#### Specific interventions under this project include:

- **Tree planting:** Planting 10 million trees per year.
- **Charcoal use:** Reducing charcoal use by 80,000 metric tonnes in priority locations.
- **Tree cover increase:** Increasing tree cover in selected areas with the aim of reducing soil erosion and improving water quality.

### 32 The National Disaster Mitigation Program (2016–2021) is a US$5 million programme financed by the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB). This project aims to improve disaster risk management, including watershed management under the auspices of Haiti’s Agriculture Department. Specific interventions under this project include:

- **Improved water and sediment conservation:** Selecting priority gully sites for protection.
- **Reduced risk of land degradation:** Implementing soil erosion control measures such as contour ploughing and terracing.
- **Enhanced agricultural productivity:** Generating and sharing knowledge on best practices in resource management and rural engineering.

#### These interventions will provide valuable lessons on climate change adaptation, including the use of EMA solutions. The project also aims to develop a set of guidelines for using EMA frameworks that can be used to inform climate-resilient planning at the watershed level. The proposed GCF project seeks to address this gap by:

- **Enhancing climate-resilient flood management:** Enhancing the capacity of local authorities and communities in the TR watershed to adapt to climate change.
- **Strengthening institutional and technical capacity:** Decision-makers involved in agriculture at national and local levels will be better equipped to make informed decisions about how best to use EMA solutions as part of a system to support vulnerable households affected by chronic food insecurity.

#### A comprehensive list of baseline projects, programmes and initiatives is available as details of their focus and alignment with the proposed project are provided in Annex 2. Supporting documentation and project monitoring reports are available at: https://www.sdf.org/project/USAID

## The World Bank-funded Resilient Productive Landscapes Project (RPLP) (2018-2024)

is a US$26 million project that will be implemented in the Tana River District of Kenya, with the following objectives:

### Objectives
1. **Adoption of climate-resilient agricultural and landscape management practices** to enhance productivity and resilience.
2. **Improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers**, particularly women and youth.

### Implementation Approach

- **Strengthening climate change adaptation capacity**: By integrating climate change considerations into the project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting (M&E) framework.
- **Enhancing adaptive capacity of communities**: Through capacity building activities that focus on improving livelihoods in the face of climate change.

### Expected Outcomes

1. **Increased agricultural productivity** through improved land management practices.
2. **Improved water resources management**, including enhanced water availability for agriculture and other uses.
3. **Sustainable use and management of water resources over the long term**.

## The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB), Global Agriculture and Food Security Program (GAFSP), International Fund For Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the Ministry of Agriculture, Natural Resources and Environment (MINAGRI) are implementing this project under the Partnership for Technological Innovation for Agriculture and Agroindustry Program (PTAGA) (2018-2022).

### Key Interventions

- **Integrated land management practices**: Including agroforestry systems.
- **Climate-resilient agricultural inputs**: Such as drought-resistant crops.

### Expected Benefits

- **Increased crop yields** and improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers, particularly women and youth.
- **Enhanced resilience to climate change**, including increased resistance to extreme weather events such as floods, hurricanes, and tropical storms.

## The project will be implemented by building on the integrated land management practices under PTAGA. These interventions are expected to:

1. **Strengthen monitoring and evaluation (M&E)** of project interventions.
2. **Enhance contingency plans** for climate change adaptation.
3. **Demonstrate the benefits of agroforestry systems as an alternative livelihood strategy**.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

- **Regular monitoring**: To assess progress towards achieving the project's objectives.
- **Evaluation reports**: To provide insights into what works, what doesn't work, and how to improve future interventions.

## The project will be implemented in collaboration with national and local level stakeholders. It is expected that the benefits generated under this program will contribute to improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers, particularly women and youth.

### B.2 (a): Theory of change narrative and diagram (max 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### Problem statement
The Caribbean is experiencing the impacts of extreme climate events, which contribute to its ranking as the most vulnerable country in the world to climate change, according to the 2016 Global Climate Risk Index. Climate change is leading to an increase in the frequency and intensity of hurricanes in the Caribbean region. Moreover, climate change is resulting in a decrease in total annual rainfall across the region, with more intense rainfall events and longer dry spells. These changes are increasing the frequency and intensity. These changing climatic conditions are contributing to an increase in the frequency and intensity of flooding and droughts. The increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have led to increased coastal erosion, landslides, and soil degradation. In addition, induced floods are exacerbated in the watershed by the steep topography, degraded landscapes caused by unsustainable land use practices, and poor drainage systems.

During heavy rainfall and flood events, these degraded areas experience increased surface runoff, compounded by decreased infiltration due to the loss of vegetation cover. The combination of these factors results in flooding with the steep topography of the TR watershed and near-and complete absence of embankments along the river, increase the vulnerability of communities living within the watershed.

Furthermore, increased water flow intensifies erosion of the watershed and result in sediment deposition on the riverbed. This reduces the capacity of the river to carry floodwaters, which increases the risk of flooding. In addition, freshwater resources also increases the risk of water- and vector-borne diseases (e.g., cholera) spreading throughout the region.

The impacts of climate change are not limited to increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; they have a direct impact on land use and water resources, as well as the health and livelihoods of the population living in the watershed. The increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, such as rainfall patterns that will become more concentrated and intense under future climate change conditions.

#### Preferred adaptation solution
For the TR watershed, impacts of climate change-induced flooding on the TR watershed, it is necessary to implement an integrated approach to flood management to adequately address the diverse impacts of climate change. The project proposes a multi-faceted approach to addressing these challenges by implementing the project and in particular focus on: 1) improving land use planning and management at national and local levels; 2) increasing the capacity of communities living within the watershed to adapt to climate change through improved livelihood options that target community resilience, including the development of water and energy services; and promoting sustainable livelihoods. The approach is based on a multi-hazard risk assessment and provides an integrated flood risk management framework with targeted adaptation measures.

The project will focus on developing a water resource management plan (WRMP) for the TR watershed towards an integrated approach that is underpinned by the update and implementation of climate-resilient land use practices, strong governance, as well as the sustainable use and management of land and water resources.

## Barriers to the adaptation solution

### Barrier 2: Absence of integrated, climate-sensitive water and land management policy and governance frameworks in Haiti

- **Limited technical capacities**: Stakeholders are not informed about sound and projected climate change impacts, particularly focusing on agricultural production, other land-use practices and water resources.
- **Lack of enforcement mechanisms**: There is a lack of enforcement mechanisms for the implementation of existing laws and regulations. This includes the absence of regulatory frameworks and enforcement of sanctions and property rights also reinforces an open access to natural resources.
- **Limited coordination and capacity to implement/reinforce**: The lack of vertical coordination and oversight is varied across sectors, including water management. There are no administrative structures at the commune level that have the skill and capacity for IWRM. This lack of vertical coordination and oversight is varied across sectors, including water management.

### Barrier 3: Limited implementation and capacity to implement/reinforce

- **Limited technical capacities**: Implementing executing entities, government and non-governmental stakeholders in Haiti have limited opportunity to design and implement projects independently without consideration of how they affect other or add the burden on existing services.
- **Lack of coordination in planning, implementation and accountability caused by underfunded roles and responsibilities**.

Or: The establishment of a Water Act has been identified as one of the Ministry of Environment's priorities in its 2019-2022 Triennial Plan.

**Page 18**
---

### Barrier 4: Limited access to credit, savings and financial instruments or incentives among farmers

#### Project approach to overcoming the barriers to adaptation

The project will overcome the aforementioned barriers to build the climate resilience of Haiti’s TR watershed by implementing a community-based integrated flood management approach that is tailored to the impacts of climate induced flooding. These will be implemented through community based governance and planning, which will ensure that the most vulnerable communities are prioritized for flood protection to those most vulnerable. Sustainable land management as implemented by the project will result in enhanced productivity and market access for smallholder farmers, while also improving water quality practices underpinning the ecosystem based approach. The project’s strong focus on communication mechanisms and community engagement is expected to build a sense of ownership among communities, creating a clear link between sustainable land management to both production capacity and markets as well as by mobilizing community-based financing for continued investment. More important project investments will be made in water storage infrastructure, which will provide flood protection to those most vulnerable.

#### Barrier 5: Limited incentives for communities to change land management and farming practices or contribute to climate-resilient systems

#### Barrier 6: Absence of inclusive and participatory community consultation mechanisms for climate change-induced flood management planning and decision-making

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

### Output 1: Ecosystem-based flood management solutions implemented in 25-46 hectares of the Trois-Rivières watershed

#### 55. At present, climate change-induced flooding is exacerbated by degraded landscapes and forests because of large-scale deforestation, land abandonment, and changes in vegetation. During heavy rainfall events and flooding, the limited surface cover combined with the near-complete absence of embankments along the river intertides soil erosion and sedimentation are increased. The Trois-Rivières watershed (TR) is a highly vulnerable area to climate change impacts. Water pollution will address these problems by significantly reducing peak runoff and flood extent in the area. These flood management solutions will be implemented on 25-46 hectares of land within the TR watershed, which will reduce flow by considerably reducing surface runoff and increasing the infiltration capacity of the soil. Reductions in water flow will also result in a reduction in sedimentation (which is currently very high due to deforestation and lack of equipment), which will result in a decrease in the observed annual damages and economic losses caused by flooding.

#### 56. Further livelihood benefits will be realised through the implementation of agroforestry systems in the target communities, including: Ag silvicultural systems — which are a combination of crops and trees, such as alley cropping, intercropping, or strip cropping; Agroforestry systems that combine trees with livestock (e.g., tree-based dairy farming); Agroforestry systems that combine trees, crops and animals. At present, most of the vulnerable communities living in the TR watershed are engaged in unsustainable charcoal production in years where the impacts of climate change have had particularly severe effects on their livelihoods. The project will provide training to these households (i.e., water towers) on alternative livelihood options such as beekeeping and mushroom cultivation). It is feasible for these households to transition from charcoal production towards agroforestry systems, which would result in a shift away from unsustainable charcoal production towards land use practices that will continue to provide long-term benefits under future climate change conditions.

#### Projected impact: Improved food and nutrition security and access to social protection in the Trois-Rivières watershed (also referred to as the water towers).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## Activity 1.2: Implement ecosystem-based flood management solutions in the Tri-Rivieres watershed (subactivity 1.2.1)

### The project will focus on reducing the impacts of climate change-induced flooding in the Tri-Rivieres and the Tri-Rivieres Watershed.

This project activity will focus on reducing the impacts of climate change-induced flooding in the Tri-Rivieres and the Tri-Rivieres Watershed (see Figure 2.1). This is a critical issue for the communities living along the river, as the increase in rainfall intensity has resulted in increased flooding risk particularly for people living near the Tri-Rivieres River (viewed at an elevation of approximately 30 meters above sea level) and the Tres Rios River (viewed at an elevation of approximately 15 meters above sea level). The project will focus on reducing the impacts of climate change-induced flooding by implementing a 17.4 km² of agroforestry systems and the rehabilitation of 7,700 ha forest areas across targeted communities in the Tri-Rivieres Watershed. These interventions are expected to result in a reduction of flood peaks that will also result in reducing the impacts of climate-induced flooding by reducing peak runoff during extreme events.

### Hydrological and ecological studies commissioned by UNDP to support the development of the project models that implement ecosystem-based solutions for flood management in the Tri-Rivieres Watershed

These studies have shown that implementing agroforestry systems across 17.4 km² will result in a reduction of peak runoff during extreme events, which is expected to reduce flood extents in the target areas by 20% and 26% under the year-2000 and 100-year flood scenarios respectively (see Figure 2.3). The studies have also shown that the rehabilitation of forested areas across 7,700 ha is expected to reduce the number of households affected by 100-year floods (decreasing from 1,342 households at risk of flooding under the baseline scenario to approximately 586 households) and the number of households affected by 200-year floods (decreasing from 79 households at risk of flooding under the baseline scenario to zero). These interventions under this project activity are expected to result in a 35% reduction in the number of households at risk of flooding during extreme events. The studies have also shown that these interventions will reduce flood peaks by up to 200 hours at the end of the century under a 20-year flooding scenario. Further details on the modeling calculations and assumptions used to determine project benefits are presented in Section 4.2.4.

### Specifically, under Activity 1.2, the establishment of 17.5 km² of agroforestry systems (subactivity 1.2.1) and the rehabilitation of forested areas across 7,700 ha (subactivity 1.2.2) will be implemented in targeted communities within the Tri-Rivieres Watershed. These interventions include planting seedlings (e.g., for fruit production and reforestation) and providing ongoing technical assistance to end users through a community-based monitoring system. The project will also provide training, awareness-raising packages for agroforestry and mentoring of the agroforestry system.

To account for the growing times of many crops, the total forest area in the watershed is 278 km², representing 31% of the total watershed area, and the total urban area is 125.6 km², representing 14%. The total land area available for rehabilitation with agroforestry or reforestation under this project activity is 403.4 km², representing 55% of the watershed.

Priority intervention areas for agroforestry and reforestation interventions

The priority intervention areas for agroforestry and reforestation interventions in the TB watershed were defined using a multi-criteria model that considered three variables. These were the slope of the terrain, distance from rivers and/or water sources, and proximity to houses/buildings. The terrain slope was derived from a digital surface model with a 20-metre spatial resolution pixel, generated from the Panoramic Remote-Sensing Instrument (Pleiades) satellite imagery. The distance from rivers and/or water sources were calculated using polygons identified as priority areas were established. The multi-criteria model was then built to classify the optimal land use for each of these variables. Based on this analysis, 77 km2 of land is classified as a priority area for agroforestry, 75.2 km2 is classified as a priority area for agroforestry systems, and a further 102.2 km2 is classified as a priority area for reforestation. The selection process undertaken in the proposed project is presented in Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

The locations of priority areas for reforestation and agroforestry interventions are presented in Figure B9 (Figure 3a and 3b below).

# Output 2: Promote excellent agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets to restore POM SLIM and reduce degradation in the Troca-River watershed

## Activity 2.1: Strengthen institutional capacity at the commune, inter-commune and department levels in Haiti's Troca-River Watershed

Under this activity, a capacity needs assessment will be conducted for the agricultural sector in the 7 targeted communes of the Troca-River Watershed. The assessment will focus on the following areas:

- **Agricultural value chains**: This includes the identification and analysis of existing value chains, their strengths and weaknesses, and opportunities to improve them.
- **Natural resource management**: This involves assessing the current state of natural resources in the watershed, including soil health, water availability, and biodiversity. The aim is to identify areas where interventions can be made to enhance the management of these resources.

### Goal
The goal translates to "a band of food" (haydages) which could permit perennial crops (e.g., plants and cassava), in combination with annual crops such as maize and beans, which provide structural integrity to stabilize soil to prevent erosion, while the crops provide a source of food security and income generation.

### Expected Outcomes
The expected outcomes of this project related to institutional capacity building at national level will be strongly complementary security and resilience in natural resources management that will contribute to a paradigm shift in Haiti towards climate-resilient sustainable development.

work with relevant stakeholders in the 31 catchments and sub-catchments to manage the efficient use of land and water resources. Furthermore, the committees will be responsible for fostering community empowerment and overall governance of the Water Act. ### 86. To support the implementation of the Water Act and the transition towards an integrated management structure for land and water resources in the TR watershed, a Catchment Integrated Water Resources Management Committee (CIRMC) will be established at each catchment/sub-catchment level. These committees will consist of key stakeholders sectors (e.g., agriculture), land users at sub-catchment level and Sub-Catchment Water Resources User Associations (SCWRUAs). The CIRMCs will be responsible for the development, implementation, and monitoring of governance framework in the watershed. This will involve these committees working together to develop climate change resilient water resources management strategies that are consistent with the principles of integrated sustainable water resources management and an equitable distribution of the available resources at the catchment and sub-catchment levels. The CIRMCs will also be responsible for monitoring and reporting on the impact of the Water Act, which will ensure that the management of water resources in the catchment considers climate change impacts, including the potential increased impacts of landscape level (Sub Action 3.2) as well as an upstream and downstream flow dynamics.

### 87. Ensuring that all adaptation interventions are implemented throughout the implementation phase for facilitation of monitoring and assessment will be achieved through the implementation of Sub Actions 1-4 under Outcome 3 on climate change-induced flooding in the TR watershed. The implementation of ESA Interventions under Output 1, combined with the implementation of the Water Act under Output 2, will ensure that the management of water resources towards the TR watershed is done in a manner that is consistent with the principles of integrated sustainable water resources management towards the use of an integrated governance framework that is resilient to the impacts of climate change. This will be achieved through the development and implementation of these interventions at individual, households and communities living in the TR watershed, reducing the spread of water- and vectorborne diseases as a result of implementing ESA Interventions under Output 1 (Sub action 3.3.3). Finally, lessons generated from the implementation of these interventions will be shared with relevant stakeholders sectors and associations at the catchment and sub-catchment levels to inform adaptive management of these interventions.

### 88. By strengthening national capacity for the implementation of the Water Act, promoting the adoption of an integrated, climate-resilient water management governance framework at the catchment and sub-catchment level with the development of a Catchment Integrated Water Resources Management Committee (CIRMC), the implementation of Sub Action 3 will be to establish an enabling environment that is conducive for the effective implementation of the Water Act. This will ensure that the capacity of the Govt to develop and implement the Water Act, which is an essential legal foundation for enabling participatory water resources management at all levels in the TR watershed. The CIRMCs will also be responsible for monitoring and reporting on the impact of the Water Act, which will ensure that the management of water resources at the local level can then be improved in line with the Water Act to promote the more sustainable use of land and water resources in the TR watershed.

Finally, to complete the cycle of understanding and improving water resources management: adequate monitoring of these resources is required. This should consider flooding and its impacts on health – it is important to feed back into regular updating of the Water Act and facilitate adaptive management through the two-level regulatory structures.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 31

90. Project activities that will be implemented under Output 3 are summarized below. Further details on these activities, including on the associated sub-activities and deliverables, are presented in Section 6.E of this Funding Proposal and in the Small Grants Program Manual.

#### Activity 3.1 | Strengthen institutional capacities for the implementation of the Water Act

91. Under this activity, the capacity of the Gowl will be strengthened to develop and implement the Water Act, which is aimed at improving water resources management through integrated watershed management in the Rio Tres Watershed. At present, the government is organized along departmental and command lines. As a result, there is a tendency for each sector to act independently without considering the inter-linkages between sectors.

92. This leads to challenges in coordination and planning, as well as the effective and efficient implementation of an integrated water resources management strategy. The Rio Tres Watershed has been identified by the Gowl as one of its priority areas in drafting the Water Act to address these challenges; there is a need for technical assistance for the last mile of its drafting process.

93. To ensure that the Water Act is effectively implemented, it will be necessary to develop an integrated approach to water resources management, including considerations for the coordinated development of water and land use planning in the Rio Tres Watershed. The Gowl will also work with the relevant stakeholders (e.g., communities) on how to implement this approach.

94. Provisions to the Water Act will also recommend the establishment of Catchment WRM Committees (Comités de Gestion des Ressources en Eau dans le Bassin versant) at the sub-catchment level and the establishment of Sub-Catchment Water Resource Users Associations (Associations d'Utilisateurs des Ressources en Eau au Niveau du Sous-Bassin).

#### Activity 3.2 | Develop an integrated, climate-resilient water management governance framework targeting the catchment and sub-catchment levels in the Rio Tres Watershed

95. Given that the Rio Tres Watershed is one of the most vulnerable watersheds in Haiti to climate change-induced flooding, the implementation of an integrated water resources management strategy is a priority action for increasing the resilience of communities living within this watershed.

96. A governance framework will be developed for the TR catchment, namely the Catchment Integrated Water Resources Management Committee (Comité de Gestion Intégrée des Ressources en Eau dans le Bassin versant), which will include indirectly involved actors in the agricultural and water sector (state institutions including the Moll and Haiti's Hydrographic Institute, as well as civil society organizations). The committee will be composed of representatives from the government approach), representatives of civil society (economic or social organizations, producers' organizations), and representatives of communities living within the catchment.

97. Together they will develop a climate-resilient integrated water resource management plan for end users at the sub-catchment and catchment levels that promote sustainable water resources management and equitable distribution of benefits from these resources to all stakeholders in the watershed, including the most vulnerable groups. The roles and responsibilities of institutions and civil society stakeholders for water resources management (complementary to the role of the government) will be defined.

98. They will also promote a landscape management approach that will be linked to the implementation of community land use plans developed by communities living within the catchment, in collaboration with the relevant actors and SCWRIARs, to ensure that the management of water resources in the catchment complies with the principles of sustainable development and is consistent with the objectives of reducing downstream flood dynamics. Training on the climate resilient WRM plans will also be delivered to these stakeholder groups.

99. The combined result of these actions will be the establishment and operation of an integrated governance framework that will contribute to preserving climate-resilient, sustainable land and water resource management beyond the project period. This will ensure that the benefits from the implementation of the project will be secured equitably representing participation and leadership of women.

#### Activity 3.3 | Implement Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EBA) solutions at the catchment and sub-catchment levels to support the implementation of integrated water resources management (WRM) plans

100. Flood risk reduction measures, including the construction of check dams and terraces, will be undertaken and will continue throughout the implementation phase. The implementation of EBA solutions under Output 1 of the project will also contribute to reducing flood risks in the Rio Tres Watershed.

101. Floods resulting from heavy rainfall are also likely to lead to the spread of water-borne diseases such as cholera, which is endemic in Haiti. Therefore, it is important that the project monitors the evolution, frequency and location of flood-related illnesses from the start of the project; the impact of the project can be assessed once a sufficient time series of observations is completed. UNDP will commission

## A.1 HACT microassessment has been developed for the MoE identifying its capacity to manage projects with UNDP and partners. The microassessment is based on a risk assessment framework that identifies three levels of risk: low, medium, and high. It provides guidance on how to mitigate risks in partnerships and collaborations, moderate risks in governance, project management and human resources and a significant risk in monitoring and evaluation and procurement and financial management.

## The HACT microassessment will be used by the MoE to assess its capacity for direct implementation of projects. This includes the procurement of goods and services through UNDP processes, providing direct payments to suppliers and Reporting on Project Outputs. The MoE will make payments and provide related services at the instruction of the MoE who will be responsible for the preparation of all documentation required by UNDP. The MoE will also be responsible for ensuring that the HACT microassessment is applied in all cases where the MoE makes direct payments or provides related services. The MoE will agree that those services are not mandatory and will be provided only upon Government request and specified in the Letter of Agreement (LoA) between the MoE and UNDP. The MoE will also be responsible for ensuring that the HACT microassessment is applied to all cases where the MoE makes direct project costs relating to GCF funded projects. The project has been designed to enable GDFUN to be able to provide support in the areas of procurement, financial management and reporting on Project Outputs within the budget period. In addition UNDP is providing co-financing support to enhance project management and all other aspects of project implementation.

## 105. UNDP will provide implementation support in the delivery of activities included within the project’s Output 3. A firewall will be implemented to separate the work being provided for the implementation of Output 3 and UNDP’s oversight of the MoE’s direct implementation capacity as described above. The MoE has agreed in paragraph 80 that it is bound between the Government of Haiti and UNDP specifying UNDP’s role in Output 3.

### Accredited Entity

The MoE provides a three - tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional and headquarters levels. The quality assurance role supports the Project Board to carrying out objective assessments of project performance against agreed indicators, including progress towards achievement of milestones are managed and completed. Project Assurance must be independent of the Project Management function and should not be provided by the same person who is responsible for project implementation.

The project assurance role will be covered by the accredited entity fee provided by the GCF. As an Accredited Entity to the CCF, UNDP will provide oversight in the areas of quality assurance, financial management, procurement and project delivery oversight supervision., (Oversight of project completion), (Oversight of project reporting).

### 107. The justification to engage UNDP support services responds to HACT evaluation assessment of the MoE in particular regarding its capacity for direct implementation of projects. The MoE has agreed that it will be able to engage various partners from both public and private sector, either following competitive procurement process or other partnership arrangements. However, the MoE is not yet prepared to manage all aspects of project implementation (Lack for UNDP support services). As the support services will be provided by UNDP, usual UNDP regulations, rules and procedures will apply in relation to the provision of these services including those relating to conflict of interest operations.

### Responsible Parties

The responsible parties will be overseen by and report to the MoE in its role as Executing Entity. As such they will enter upon written agreement with the MoE specifying their role in project implementation including deliverables, budgetary implications and reporting requirements. The MoE will also provide a letter of commitment to UNDP in relation to the MoE.

### 108. Haiti International Hall will act as Responsible Party for Output 3. Haiti International Hall will also execute all activities under Output 2 from its own co-finance. Once the FAA is signed between UNDP and the GCF and after the project has been approved by the GCF, Haiti International Hall will be responsible to the International Hall on behalf of the Executing Entity for the provision of services for the execution of these outputs.

### 109. Haiti International Hall is recognized in Haiti as a non-profit corporation working in Haiti since 1999 by providing technical assistance and capacity building support to government institutions, civil society organizations, authorities, and government institutions. Its work places an emphasis on community mobilization, resilience, social capital development, environmental protection, personal leadership, the promotion of gender equity, agricultural

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WEBSITE V3.0 PAGE 14

## Project Board Responsibilities

The Project Board will ensure that activities under all three outputs of the project comply with the strategies, principles and overall approach outlined in this proposal. The Project Board is responsible for making decisions by considering the information provided to it by the UNDP Project Manager (PM) on the basis of the approved work plans, reservations/rejections and acceptance of the project progress reports. In order to ensure UNDP's ultimate accountability for the project, the Project Board will be responsible for reviewing the project management for development results, the best value for money, fairness, integrity, transparency and effective implementation of the project. The Project Board will also review the annual work plan and budget prepared by UNDP. Project reviews by the Board will take place at least twice a year, or as necessary when raised by the Project Manager or other stakeholders. The Project Board will be responsible for approving the Annual Work Plan where there are substantial changes required to the Annual Workplan via more deviation tolerances set by a donor or UNDP.

### Specific Roles of the Project Board

- **set strategic direction**: reflect government leadership of the program and coordinate all interventions;
- **authorize the Annual Work Plan (prior to approval by UNDP)**: consider the project progress reports, reservations/rejections and provide direction and recommendations to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily according to the approved Annual Work Plan;
- **approve the budget**: review and approve all activities that are supported by the program based on the program objectives, work plan and budget;
- **provide technical advice** to create synergy and uniformity between program supported activities and policy guidance;
- **provide support in resource mobilization** to support program funding gaps;
- **monitor progress of project activities**: including national/local site visits; and
- **receive reports on all activities supported by the program** to serve as an additional basis to assess and monitor the program performance and delivery.

### The Project Board will be led by the Executive

The Executive will work closely with each participant's roles and responsibilities are listed below:

* **UNDP**: UNDP will provide strategic direction for the project, which includes funding and/or technical expertise to the project (designing, developing, facilitating, procuring, implementing, monitoring and evaluating). UNDP will also be responsible for ensuring that the project is in line with the Ministry of Environment's (MoE) role to ensure that environment decisions at the project level are relevant to the national environmental policy. UNDP will have the authority to commit or acquire the necessary resources to carry out its function to the full extent.
* **The Ministry of Agriculture, Natural Resources and Rural Development (MARDIN)**: will fulfill the 'Main Bank' role in the project by ensuring that the project is aligned with the national development agenda; representing the interests of project beneficiaries to ensure that project outputs serve the needs of the beneficiaries. Representatives from MARDIN will attend all Project Board meetings by providing necessary information on the execution of activities and as a non-voting observer.
* **Project Management Unit (PMU)**: will provide strategic direction to the proposed project and ensure technical and operational coordination of the Project Management Unit (PMU) that receives training from UNDP. Specifically, the PM will undertake day-to-day management and decision-making for the project. In line with this mandate, the PM will be responsible for ensuring that all activities are carried out in accordance with the work plans as specified in the Logical Framework (Section E of this Funding Proposal) and ensuring that these interventions contribute to the achievement of the project's outputs.

## Experience and track record of UNDP as the Accredited Entity & Support Services provider:

16. Within its central role in the UN system, UNDP facilitates a multi-sectoral approach in assisting developing countries to achieve their development goals through capacity building and knowledge sharing. Through its long-term, people-centred approach to capacity development, UNDP is recognized as an experienced agency with a strong track record of supporting governments in implementing water resources management.

17. To date, UNDP has led numerous environmental protection and climate change responses in Haiti through the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Small Grants Programme, and knowledge sharing. Through these actions, UNDP has built strong relationships with decision-makers at all levels and proven its strengths as an impartial provider of technical assistance to governments. In addition to the GEF Small Grants Programme, UNDP has also implemented the Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EBA) approach that were managed and implemented by UNDP in Haiti in recent years are listed below:

- **Haiti Forests for Climate Change and Biodiversity**: Approximately US$ 9.3 million
- **The Global Environment Facility Small Grants Programme**: United Nations Development Programme, Korea International Cooperation Agency (KOICA), World Food Programme; approximately US$ 15 million
- **Support for the Preparation and Implementation of Disaster Risk Management Plans in Haiti**: Government of Japan; approximately US$ 2.1 million
- **The National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan**: United Nations Development Programme, Korea International Cooperation Agency (KOICA), Norwegian Ministry of Foreign Affairs, approximately US$ 1.7 million
- **National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Haiti**: United Nations Development Programme, Global Environment Facility (GEF), USAID, European Commission, The World Bank; approximately US$ 10.5 million
- **Resilience to Disasters in Department North**: Government of Japan; approximately US$ 4.6 million

121. As a Caribbean SIDS and LDC, Haiti is one of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change and extreme weather events. The country has been experiencing an increase in frequency and intensity of such events already adversely affecting the country's economy, with negative impacts extending across several of Haiti's major economic sectors. Between 1975 and 2013, such events resulted in damages and losses equivalent to 2% of the

B

## Country is highly vulnerable per year. Since then, the impacts of climate change have continued to negatively affect the population of Haiti. For example, Hurricane Matthew (Category [5]) that struck Haiti in 2016 resulted in the deaths of 49 people and affected more than 837,000 people. The hurricane caused severe flooding, which led to landslides requiring immediate humanitarian assistance. The storm also inflicted economic damages and losses estimated at nearly US$1 billion. In addition, the hurricane caused a significant increase in the number of cases of cholera in some areas of the country, and caused a sharp increase in the number of cholera cases. The intense rainfall associated with Hurricane Matthew led to flooding in several parts of Haiti, resulting in severe damage to infrastructure impacts. For example, in 2004, Hurricane Jeanne caused severe flooding in northern Haiti, resulting in more than 2,659 deaths and affecting nearly 1 million people. In addition, the hurricane left approximately 380,000 homes that clogged drainage networks and infrastructure across the country.

### The Tolo River (TR) watershed is the north of the country is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The TR watershed has experienced a significant increase in rainfall intensity over recent decades, which has led to an increase in the frequency and magnitude of flooding events. These flooding causes severe damage to crops and farmland, as well as agricultural infrastructure and equipment. Flooding also leads to soil erosion and sedimentation, which can reduce crop yields and productivity. In addition, the TR watershed is particularly vulnerable to climate change due to its high population density and the risk of loss of human lives and livelihoods. Climate change projections simulate an increase in rainfall intensity in the TR watershed over recent decades, which will likely lead to more frequent and severe flooding events.

### The Government of Haiti (Gh) does not have the capacity to fully finance integrated climate resilient food management interventions that are required for the country to be able to adapt to the impacts of climate change. At present, the Gh does not have the capacity to fully finance integrated climate resilient food management interventions that are required for the country to be able to adapt to the impacts of climate change.

### Currently, foreign aid accounts for more than half of Haiti's national budget. The International Monetary Fund (IMF) estimates that Haiti will need approximately US$2 billion in 2016 and approximately US$3 billion in 2021 to meet its debt service obligations. Such consideration is based on the assumption that the country will continue to receive concessional loans from multilateral commercial banks, with the account deficit (including international grants) estimated at approximately $4 for 2016 and $5 for 2021.

### Several alternative financing options were considered during project development, as fully financing the project through domestic resources is not feasible. However, public debt burden limits the Gh's access to loans from both regional and international development banks. Given that the Gh does not have the capacity to fully finance integrated climate resilient food management interventions required for the country to be able to adapt to the impacts of climate change, it is essential to seek support from the Heifer International Haiti to support the implementation of interventions under Output 2. While this contribution is significant in terms of financing, it will also provide a number of co-benefits such as improved water and water resources management that can reduce the impacts of climate change-induced flooding on the TR watershed.

### External support in the form of GCF grant funding will be necessary to catalyze the adoption of such an integrated approach. The proposed project is expected to deliver several adaptation benefits that will contribute to achieving the GCF program shift objective of climate-resilient sustainable development in the country. Specifically, this will be achieved by implementing agriculture.
