# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

17,740 ha of waterbody systems and reforesting 7,700 ha in priority sites** in the TR watershed; to enhance national and local technical and institutional capacity for productive climate-resilient land management; and iii) strengthening the capacity of the Water Resources Management Authority (WRMA) to implement Watershed Management (WIM). Implementing this suite of adaptation interventions in the TR watershed will reduce the impacts of climate change on water resources, increase the resilience of the agricultural sector, and enhance the resilience of the agricultural sector – a major economic sector in the country – to these impacts. Moreover, adoption of the WIM approach will lead to improved water management improvements; as the prevalence of waterborne diseases will decrease alongside improved flood management.

128. Without GCF support, the GoR will continue to operate under the existing paradigm of reactive response from the government and communities in the TR watershed. This will result in a continued lack of capacity among communities in their capacity to plan for and respond to the impacts of climate change – particularly those related to increasingly severe flooding events, increased waterborne diseases, and reduced agricultural productivity due to changes in weather patterns and ecosystems as a result of a flooding, as well as projected reductions in annual rainfall and increases in maximum temperature. This will lead to an increase in food insecurity, malnutrition, and poverty among vulnerable communities impacted. Under future climate change conditions, this will result in inter alia: continued declines in agricultural productivity; increased waterborne diseases; increased cases of malaria and other vector-borne diseases; and increased economic damages and loss of life.

B. 8.1 Project Description (approximately 1 page)

127. The proposed project interventions have been designed to deliver maximum adaptation benefits to vulnerable communities in the Heleis Rivers (TR) watershed beyond the project lifetime. Such benefits include reducing flood damage, increasing agricultural productivity, and improving water quality. These benefits will be achieved under future climate change conditions through implementing sustainable land and water resources management practices that are integrated into the livelihoods of the most vulnerable groups. The project interventions have been incorporated into the design of all project interventions, as described below.

Participatory approach to climate-resilient sustainable development

128. The project has adopted a participatory approach in its planning, implementation and review and consultative process, enabling all relevant stakeholders to contribute to the design of interventions. These interventions are centred around improving livelihoods for vulnerable communities through integrated land and water resources management using an integrated approach to land and water resources management. Upscaling of such an approach across the watershed will ensure that the benefits from these interventions are sustained beyond project completion.

129. Given that the project promotes an integrated approach to land and water resources management, participation from national and local stakeholders, particularly communities and other most vulnerable groups (e.g., women and people with disabilities) in critical decision-making processes is essential for successful implementation. As a result, all project stakeholders are expected to be engaged in the implementation of relevant interventions throughout the project life cycle.

Activity 1.2: Promoting community participation in land use planning and water resources management

This activity will promote the adoption of sustainable land-use practices and promote their adoption. These campaigns will also encourage communities to secure the buy-in of stakeholders at the community level, which will ensure the sustainability of on-the-ground interventions beyond project completion.

Activity 1.3: Promoting women’s participation in land use planning and rehabilitation of water bodies

This activity is designed to promote women's participation in land use planning and rehabilitation of water bodies (Activity 1.2) by promoting community involvement in related activities, such as the sustainable management of water resources and the establishment of water resource user groups.

Beyond the project lifetime:

An enhanced gender engagement plan (Annex B7) has also been developed to ensure that this participatory approach is continued during implementation. The impact and sustainability of project interventions will be further enhanced by ensuring that women’s voices are heard in all aspects of project planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting.

Gender-sensitive targets for all project activities have therefore been built into the project's Gender Action Plan (Annex B7) to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed throughout the project. This will ensure that the benefits from these activities by both women and men which, in turn, will result in gender-sensitive adaptation benefits being conferred beyond the project lifetime.

### Strengthening Technical and Institutional Capacity for Sustainable Climate-Resilient Land and Water Resources Management

132. In this project, the technical and institutional capacity of relevant stakeholders at national, departmental and commune levels will be strengthened for the implementation of an integrated approach to land and water resources management (LWRM) in the TR watershed. This will be achieved by building the capacity of key government entities, including the Ministry of Agriculture, Natural Resources and Rural Development (MINARDN), MINARDN’s Department of Water Resources Management (DWMR), and the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Land Management (SLM) at the department level. Doing so, we will enable the compatibility of SLM with other efforts in the project area, such as the implementation of integrated water resources management (IWRM) across the TR watershed. By building by the capacity of the targeted institutions, the transfer of knowledge and skills related to climate change adaptation and sustainable land use practices will be enhanced among farmers and households across the TR watershed. Activity 1.1). This training will focus on climate-resilient land management to enhance productivity and income generation from agricultural activities in the TR watershed while improving water resources sustainability.

132. Such capacity-building activities will also extend beyond government departments and ministries to development partners, including the MIEE and MARIDON. The project will work with these institutions as a viable climate change adaptation option to be implemented in Haiti. Specifically, this will involve training community-based organizations (CBOs) on climate-resilient land management practices that can be adopted across the TR watershed. (Activity 1.1). This training will focus on climate-resilient land management to enhance productivity and income generation from agricultural activities in the TR watershed while improving water resources sustainability.

132. In addition, technical capacity will be strengthened among farmers in the TR watershed through participatory training workshops that will be organized by CBOs. These workshops will focus on the implementation of interventions that will provide households with increased market access in the long term for products such as coffee and cocoa. The project will also work to ensure that the institutional framework for land sustainability of the project will be further reinforced by providing training to these households on the development of sustainable agriculture business models (Activity 2.1). This will include the promotion and enhancing the implementation of sustainable land use practices. These models will be designed to promote a production system that is both economically viable and environmentally sound, with an emphasis on the development of sustainable agribusiness activities (Activity 2.1). To support this training, institutional capacity for sustainable land management in the TR watershed will also be strengthened by establishing a framework for the establishment of an integrated governance framework for SLM under newly established communal development councils (CDCs) and other local institutions. The CDCs will be responsible for coordinating activities that represent all agricultural sector stakeholders, including smallholder farmers, both during and after the project implementation period.

132. In addition to strengthening governance for SLM in the TR watershed, capacity will be built for the climate-resilient management of water resources in Haiti (Upzaid). This will be achieved by drafting provisions to the Water Act (Law 87-04) that will provide a legal framework for the sustainable use and management of water resources in the country. This will establish a legal foundation for optimizing the use of these resources without compromising the sustainability of the environment, which is critical for ensuring food security, poverty reduction, economic and social well-being. The Water Act will also provide a framework for managing water resources within watersheds such as the TR watershed (Upzaid) in Haiti – through establishing Catchment Watershed Management Units (CWMS) under the supervision of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Land Management (MINARDN) and the National Commission for the Regulation of Water Resources (CNRWR). The primary functions of these CWMS will be to coordinate planning of water and land use that is both economically viable and environmentally sound. This will be achieved through a participatory, bottom-up process for the development and implementation of Integrated Water Resources Management Plans (IWRMPs) in each watershed. These plans will be developed by stakeholders within each watershed framework will be further enhanced through the generation of lessons under Activity 3.3 and the dissemination of best practices to other watersheds across Haiti. The project will also work with MINARDN and CNRWR to improve future adaptation efforts as well as adaptive management of the project, thereby enhancing the success of climate-resilient sustainable development in Haiti.

133. In addition to strengthening governance for SLM in the TR watershed, capacity will be built for the climate- resilient management of water resources in Haiti (Upzaid). This will be achieved by drafting provisions to the Water Act (Law 87-04) that will provide a legal framework for the sustainable use and management of water resources in the country. This will establish a legal foundation for optimizing the use of these resources without compromising the sustainability of the environment, which is critical for ensuring food security, poverty reduction, economic and social well-being. The Water Act will also provide a framework for managing water resources within watersheds such as the TR watershed (Upzaid) in Haiti – through establishing Catchment Watershed Management Units (CWMS) under the supervision of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Land Management (MINARDN) and the National Commission for the Regulation of Water Resources (CNRWR). ## Strengthening agroecological value chains and unlocking public and private sector investment into sustainable climate-resilient land-use practices

129. The GCF will ensure through the GoH commitment to prioritize climate change adaptation, including in national budgeting. This commitment is evidenced in Activity 3.2 of the project, in particular where the GoH has committed to work with UNDP and other partners at the district/catchment level and SCWRLUs to develop climate-resilient WRM plans for end users. These plans will promote ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and sustainable land management practices.

130. The financial sustainability of the project will be further secured by establishing an adaptation finance mechanism under Activity 4.2, which will provide a framework for scaling up the project’s interventions to other districts and for smallholder farmers, providing much needed financial services for continued investment in the maintenance and enhancement of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) practices.

131. The GoH has committed to work with UNDP and other partners at the district/catchment level to reduce vulnerability from unsustainable farming practices such as charcoal production by establishing financially sustainable ecosystem conservation and restoration activities, including through the creation of 50 new VSCAs in the TW watershed, households affected by chronic food insecurity, enabling them to recover their nutritional and financial autonomy simultaneously. This will be achieved through a) the establishment of a community-based nutrition program; b) the establishment and operationalisation of a food coupling mechanism targeting these vulnerable households.

132. To support the GoH in achieving this commitment, UNDP will provide technical assistance for the design and implementation of interventions supported by the creation of 150 new VSCAs in the TW watershed. Furthermore, a network of community-based nutrition officers will be established to monitor the nutritional status of households and create new i) monitoring market performance and implementing adjustments to the system where required; ii) establishing a food coupling mechanism that contributes to securing the financial sustainability of interventions beyond the project lifetime.

### Operation and management of all adaptation interventions under this project

135. Sustainable operation and management of all adaptation interventions under the project has been secured by i) developing a detailed operational plan for each intervention, including a budget; ii) ensuring that the GoH is able to finance all operations, maintenance and insurance activities required under the project; and iii) supporting the generation of additional income streams from the project’s interventions. The GoH will provide funding under Activity 1.1 To support the GoH and ensure that adaptation interventions are implemented efficiently and within budget. UNDP will provide technical assistance to the GoH in developing a detailed operational plan for each intervention, including a budget. These plans will include specific financial requirements and how these will be met. The institutional partners will provide specific technical inputs and support to MoGE on the implementation of all interventions. The RPH has committed to providing additional funding under Activity 1.2 To support the GoH in implementing the outputs of Group 3. The GCF is committed to undertaking the required QM of these interventions to ensure that they contribute towards the achievement of the project’s objectives and are sustainable beyond the project lifetime. Any costs attached to this FP O&M will be further secured through incentivising community groups (e.g., farmer associations, ROCAIs, etc.) to generate additional income streams from their support and development income streams provided by these interventions.

136. UNDP will work with the GoH and UNDP partners involved in the project to ensure that the institutional capacity building to facilitate the sustainability and durability of projects. UNDP, as accredited entity, will work in collaboration with the GoH and other partners to provide technical assistance for the implementation of this project even after its lifetime. In this capacity building, UNDP will first train the institutional managers to facilitate the project’s interventions and then equip them with the necessary tools and resources.

137. Two recent examples of projects that implemented similar activities (agroforestry and supply chain) that have proven to be sustainable beyond the project implementation are: Increasing resilience of ecosystems and communities in the Upper Tana Watershed through agroforestry and supply chain development (funded by LDCF) and Strengthening adaptive capacity to address climate change threats in the Lower Tana Watershed through agroforestry and supply chain development (funded by LDCF).

#### Technical assistance
138. Output 1 of the proposed project will facilitate technology transfer by implementing EBA interventions in the TR watershed. The technical assistance activities under Output 1 and Outputs 2 and 3 of the project will include establishing 17,740 ha of agrosystemry systems and rehabilitating 7,700 ha of water storage structures (e.g., small dams, check dams) to improve water availability for agriculture. These interventions implemented under the project are not entirely new to Haiti, having been all implemented in various projects. For example, the rehabilitation of existing water storage structures has been done by other donors such as USAID, and mangroves and sea grape trees were planted on shores and in river mouths to reduce the impacts of flooding in coastal areas. The proposed project will build upon these experiences and lessons learned from previous interventions.

140. To support the implementation of EBA interventions under Activity 1.2, the technical and institutional capacity-building activities that will be implemented are as follows: Under Output 1 (Activity 1) and all Outputs 2 and 3 of the project. These capacity-building activities will focus on creating the enabling environment for the adoption of climate-resilient sustainable land use practices in Haiti's TR watershed. The technical assistance under Activity 1.2 will be implemented by a local partner, CERES, which has extensive experience in Haiti's TR watershed including capacity for carbon monitoring. In particular, capacity-building activities under Output 1 (Activity 1) and all Outputs 2 and 3 of the project include: Capacity building at the department level within Haiti's TR watershed for promoting and sustainable land-use management and its enhancement through the implementation of EBA interventions; capacity building at the commune level in agriculture in Haiti's TR watershed for adopting climate-resilient sustainable land use practices (Activity 2.2); Under Outputs 2 and 3 of the project, the capacity-building initiatives will focus on improving water resources management capacities at all levels for climate-resilient sustainable land-use management and its enhancement through the implementation of EBA interventions; and developing capacities for the implementation of the Water Act in: developing an integrated, climate-resilient water

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project proposal against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Fund-level outcomes under IRMF

143. The proposed project will enhance the sustainability of Hall’s approach to flood management in the TR region by:

- **AR0 – Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions**
    - Improved health, wellbeing, food and water security.
    - AR0 – Improved restoration of ecosystems and ecosystem services.

### Project Outcomes

144. The proposed project will be evaluated against the relevant factors specified in the GCF Integrated Results Framework (IRF) to determine whether it is likely to contribute to achieving three GCF Fund-level enabling environment outcomes, as described below:

- **Core Outcome 2: National governments and other stakeholders contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner.**
    - Core Activity 1: The project will meet the criteria for technology deployment, development, or transfer.
    - Core Activity 3: The project will contribute to market developments or adaptation at the sectoral, local, or national level.

145. The proposed project outputs include activities for enhancing climate-resilient farming planning and development – in particular, the implementation of a series of agroforestry systems and rehabilitation interventions in Hall. First, the collaborative development of community land-use plans will provide an integrated framework for sustainable landscape management that includes the establishment of agroforestry systems (Activity 3.1). Second, the creation of communal development councils and the provision of training to appropriate public stakeholders, ensuring the effective implementation of the land-use plans, will contribute to institutional strengthening in Hall (Activity 3.2). Third, Water Act by providing incentives for Catchment IRMRR Committees and Sub-Catchment Water Resource User Associations will provide a legal framework that supports the development of agroforestry systems through horizontal collaboration between stakeholders (Activity 3.3). This collaboration will be supported by the development of a community-based monitoring system to track progress in the implementation of relevant institutions on the implementation of IRMRR in decision-making activities (Activity 3.2.1). Finally, the implementation of conservation agriculture and agroforestry systems is expected to provide an important regulatory function in optimizing the uptake and effectiveness of climate-resilient flood management interventions.

146. Implementing 17,740 ha of agroforestry systems and rehabilitating 7,700 ha of forest understorey over 12.2 years will restore degraded ecosystems and considerably reduce the impacts of climate change-induced flooding in the TR watershed. The project is expected to achieve a 50% reduction in flood events (Activity 3.4). This corresponds with rehabilitation interventions expected to reduce floodfootprint extent in the target areas by 20%, and 26% under the 20-year horizon, respectively. These reductions are expected to result in more than a 50% reduction in the number of households affected by 120-year flood events (objectives 3.4.1) and a 70% reduction in the number of households affected by 60-year flood events (objectives 3.4.2). Additionally, agroforestry and rehabilitation interventions under this project activity are expected to result in a 50% reduction in the number of households at risk due to 20-year flood events in the TR watershed.

Detailed information on the assumptions and estimates of the impact potential of the proposed project are provided in the project's results framework. A summary of the key assumptions and estimates for each output will be implemented under each output to achieve the GCF project outcomes is presented in Section B.3 of the Funding Proposal.

147. In addition to their significant flood-reduction potential, the EBA interventions will also deliver numerous social benefits. The implementation of agroforestry systems in the seven target communities will increase the ecosystem goods and services they provide (e.g., improved quality and quantity of water supply, increased biodiversity, reduced soil erosion). These benefits are expected to be further enhanced by the additional income generated through the implementation of agroforestry systems in the seven target communities. This will contribute towards poverty reduction and food security for smallholder farmers who are currently engaged in crop production – towards the adoption and implementation of sustainable, climate-resilient land uses. Moreover, these interventions will also provide a range of co-benefits to other stakeholders living within or near the areas affected by flooding, including those living in the RT watershed, including: reducing the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases as continuation of contamination through floodwater is reduced; improving access to clean drinking water (e.g., through improved sanitation); and will: i) reduce the transmission of communicable diseases ii) safeguard agricultural livelihoods; and iii) improve basic services such as health, education, and infrastructure. The EBA interventions will also provide particular benefits in terms of reducing the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases including, inter alia, cholera, diphtheria and typhoid fever. These interventions are expected to reduce the number of cases by approximately 10% annually. Finally, the development of rigorous monitoring and evaluation systems will provide a basis for assessing the impact of EBA interventions on disease transmission practices, to ensure the effectiveness of inter alia, EBA interventions in reducing the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases.

148. Demonstrating the adaptation and livelihood benefits of EDA Interventions will be supported by awareness-raising activities under Output 1. These activities will strengthen the adaptive capacity of communities by increasing their knowledge about climate change impacts on agriculture, food security, and human security as well as the alternative livelihood options that these activities provide (Activity 1.1.). As a result, project beneficiaries are expected to adopt more sustainable agricultural practices, which will improve food security and income security. Moreover, the dissemination of information on sustainable charcoal production and reforestation will enhance the adaptive capacity of communities by increasing their knowledge about climate change impacts on agriculture, food security, and human security as well as the alternative livelihood options that these activities provide (Activity 1.2.). The adaptive capacity of communities will be further strengthened by: i) the provision of climate-resilient agricultural inputs; ii) the establishment of a social protection system for food insecure households (Activity 2.3). These activities will provide communities with the necessary tools to adapt and respond effectively to climate change impacts, including induced flooding, ensuring that the most vulnerable smallholder farmers are included in the agricultural value chain and benefit from the EDA interventions.

149. The proposed project will be implemented in the RT watershed area, covering 33 communal sections and 118 communities within the seven target communities. As a result of direct investments from the GCF and other investment partners, it is expected that approximately 250,670 people (approximately 124,900 men and 125,770 women) will be directly impacted by this project. The project will also have an indirect impact on approximately 282,600T people (approximately 132,150 men and 149,460 women) and approximately 441,272C people (approximately 220,536 men and 220,736 women). The project will contribute to Haiti’s approach to land and water resources management and create an enabling environment for the uptake of climate-resilient agricultural practices. As a result, it is expected that approximately 184,900 people will become possible to scale up and replicate these practices nationally, as well as across the Caribbean region.

The proposed project will catalyze a paradigm shift in Haiti’s approach to flood management in the SW region by initiating a shift away from uncoordinated decision-making and unsustainable land-use practices towards an integrated, climate-resilient land-management framework that is underpinned by three main interventions which all contribute to GCF’s Paradigm Shift Objective of climate-resilient sustainable development are presented below.

Potential for scaling up and replication, as well as strengthening Haiti's institutional and regulatory framework to address the severe impacts of climate change-induced flooding on soil erosion, well-being and livelihoods of vulnerable communities in the SW region of Haiti. The project will be implemented over a period of 5 years and i) reduce surface runoff and flood extent; ii) provide alternative livelihood options to the unsustainable land-use practices on which many households depend for their survival; iii) contribute to reducing the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases such as cholera by improving water quality and reducing flooding; iv) improve food security through the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural practices, v) establish the local governance structures required to implement and manage climate-resilient land-use practices and promote sustainable use of water resources vi) provide a framework for integrating climate change into national planning processes vii) support climate-responsive planning. Second, complementary interventions focused on enhancing technical and policy capacity in Haiti will be implemented through the following activities: i) strengthening institutional capacity to implement climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustainable land management Last, the necessary governance reforms required to ensure that these interventions are effectively implemented will be supported by the update of climate-resilient land-management systems and facilitate sustainable use and management of water resources in Haiti. The project will also contribute to the implementation of a more coherent land-use framework through a shift away from uncoordinated, compartmentalized decision-making around flood management towards an integrated approach that is underpinned by three main interventions.

To enhance the adaptation impact of these interventions, Haiti’s governance frameworks for the sustainable management of natural resources will be strengthened. This will be done primarily through adapting the draft Water Act to include provisions on climate change and land-use planning. The project will also contribute to this end as a result of these changes, a new strategy will be developed for WFP in Haiti that addresses both the existing challenges related to food security and nutrition and those related to climate change. This will include an updated catchment level, as well as the current and projected impacts of climate change-induced flooding. Regulation and enforcement mechanisms will also be strengthened through the development of a new land-use framework for the project through the development of community land-use plans, which will facilitate the implementation of a landscape approach to flood management in Haiti. The project will also contribute to the development of a more coherent land and water resources management framework in Haiti that considers climate change.

## Knowledge sharing, awareness and the creation of an enabling environment

154. The adoption of a platform for coordination between the multiple stakeholders involved in land and water development and management is essential to address the fragmented and ineffective decision-making as well as the duplication of activities. In this context, horizontal coordination will be enabled through the establishment of a Catchment Water Resource Management Committee (CWRMC) that will bring together all stakeholders regarding resource use and allocation. The project will also strengthen vertical coordination by establishing a multi-stakeholder platform for the sustainable management of land and water resources at different levels.

155. To further support the shift away from uncoordinated decision-making and unsustainable land-use practices, agricultural productivity in the TR watershed will be enhanced. This will be achieved by: (i) strengthening the capacity of the Department of Agriculture and Water Resources (DAWR) and other relevant government departments for implementing productive climate-resilient agricultural practices; (ii) enhancing smallholder farmers' access to improved seeds, tools, inputs, and services through the establishment of a seed bank and training department levels for implementing productive climate-resilient agricultural practices; (iii) enhancing smallholder farmers' access to improved seeds, tools, inputs, and services through the establishment of a seed bank and training department levels for implementing productive climate-resilient agricultural practices; and (iv) establishing a social protection system to provide immediate support to vulnerable households affected by droughts or other natural disasters. The project will also promote the adoption of sustainable land management practices and enhance the resilience of beneficiary communities, resulting in improved food security for subsistence farmers as well as higher economic returns from their agricultural activities.

## D.3 Sustainable development (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project will contribute towards the achievement of at least 5 of the UN Millennium Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These include: i) SDG 6 – Clean Water and Sanitation; ii) SDG 7 – Affordable and Clean Energy; iii) SDG 8 – Decent Work and Economic Growth; iv) SDG 10 – Reduced Inequality; v) SDG 11 – Sustainable Cities and Communities; and vi) SDG 15 – Life on Land. In line with the project’s overall objective, these interventions will generate several environmental, social, economic, and gender-responsive co-benefits, as described below.

### Environmental co-benefits

The project will: i) degraded forests through targeted reforestation intervention; ii) expanded agroforestry practices, as well as the implementation of integrated water resource management (IWRM) practices (Outputs 1.1 and 2.3). These interventions are expected to generate several environmental co-benefits including reduced surface runoff and soil erosion, carbon sequestration and improvements in air quality.

### Historical deforestation

Historically, deforestation in Haiti has increased erosion rates by six fold and river flows by 250%.[1] This problem is exacerbated by climate change-induced flooding. To address this climate change problem, 17.8 ha of agroforestry systems will be implemented as part of the project interventions (Output 3). These agroforestry systems are expected to not only have direct flood reduction benefits for vulnerable communities in the watershed[2], but they will also contribute to improved water quality and quantity, particularly in areas where climate change-induced flooding is a problem. This will result in greater biodiversity conservation, increased soil fertility, improved food security, better livestock productivity, higher crop yields and quality and productivity. Furthermore, the stabilization of degraded watersheds will result in greater biodiversity increases, as well as an increase in the number of trees per hectare (trees/ha) and a 20% increase in the improved conservation of indigenous plant and animal species[3].

### Most environmental co-benefits

While most of the environmental co-benefits that will be conferred by agroforestry and reforestation interventions are expected to be realized within the project’s watershed, some co-benefits can also be provided through the project’s CSA demonstration sites. Implementing agroforestry and reforestation activities will contribute to enhancing the carbon sequestration potential of these demonstration sites (Output 2). The implementation of landscape management practices (Activity 2.c) will catalyze a shift away from unsustainable charcoal production that contributes to increased soil erosion, deforestation, and climate change-induced flooding. These interventions are expected to generate several environmental co-benefits for the project as the potential benefits will be paramount to this activity. The implementation of landscape management practices (Activity 2.c) is also expected to contribute to improved water quality and quantity in areas where climate change-induced flooding is a problem.

### Socio-economic co-benefits

Implementing targeted landscape restoration and IWRM, as well as enhancing the technical capacity of smallholder farmers will generate several socio-economic co-benefits. These benefits are described in further detail below and include improved food security, social empowerment, greater energy security, as well as health benefits.

## Project Interventions and Economic Co-benefits

### Project Interventions

161. Transforming communities and individual farmers in climate-resilient agriculture practices (Activity 1.1.1), as well as expanding access to agricultural markets and credit facilities (Activity 2.1.2) will improve food security at the household level, increase income generation opportunities for smallholder farmers and beneficiary communities.
In addition, a reduction in the incidence and intensity of flooding achieved through reforestation and agroforestry activities (Activities 3.1 & 3.2), as well as improved water management practices (Activity 2.2) will, in particular, productive floodplains are impacted by increased rates of erosion, which contributes to food and water being contaminated with sediment and nutrients. This will also reduce the risk of landslides and mudslides, and also increases the likelihood of project beneficiaries continuing agroforestry practices and the conservation of climate-resilient livelihoods.

162. Project interventions will also generate positive social co-benefits for beneficiary communities in the form of greater social empowerment. First, communities will have improved stewardship over forests and other natural resources through the establishment of community land-use plans (Activity 1.1) and the creation of catchment area councils (Activity 3.1). Catchment WRM Committees and Sub-Catchment Resource Users Associations (Activity 3.2) will be established to manage water resources in a sustainable manner, as well as to empower communities—on natural resource management. These benefits will be supported by the diversified and improved agricultural production systems (Activity 2.1), which will increase food security levels and improve livelihoods.

### Economic Co-benefits

163. The project is, consequently, retrofuturistically, IMFW and the expansion of agricultural markets—will generate several economic co-benefits. These are described in detail below and include reduced damage to infrastructure and improved access to markets for smallholder farmers and other community beneficiaries as a result of climate-resilient livelihoods amongst smallholder farmers and other community beneficiaries.

164. At present, approximately 20% of built-up areas in Haiti are vulnerable to climate change-induced flooding. This vulnerability is exacerbated by the fact that over 95% of Haiti’s population lives within 30 km of rivers. In 2008, 40% of the country was flooded after Hurricane Jeanne made landfall in northern Haiti, a tropical storm which caused approximately US$1 billion worth of damage to infrastructure and property. The project will reduce this vulnerability by improving surface runoff and flood-exits in Haiti’s TR watershed. This, in turn, will contribute to reducing the economic damages associated with climate change-induced flooding. In addition, the project will also generate co-benefits for other social and developmental investments. These economic co-benefits will be supported by the creation of livelihoods opportunities through improved agricultural production systems (Activity 2.1) and access to markets.

165. Smallholder farmers and beneficiary communities will also secure climate-resilient livelihoods as a result of several project interventions including: i) establishing 17,400 ha of agroforestry systems (Activity 1.2), which will diversify the income-generating activities for smallholder farmers and beneficiary communities; ii) providing a wider range of agricultural practices and sustainable land use options to those who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods (Activity 2.1); iii) improving access to markets and credit facilities (Activity 2.2), which will allow beneficiaries to purchase necessary agricultural inputs and improve their productivity, as well as diversify the income-generating activities of smallholder farmers; iv) establishing forest and priority areas in the TR watershed where possible, and also inter-rias, reduced erosion and improved soil fertility. This will bolster yields, resulting a higher income levels and contributing to more resilient livelihoods.

Gender-sensitive development impact

106. Approximately 52% of direct beneficiaries of the proposed project are women. In addition, project interventions will contribute to gender-specific co-benefits described below, including gender-responsive health and socio-economic benefits.

107. Implementing an integrated approach to food management in the TR watershed will have several positive gender-sensitive development impacts. The project will reduce the incidence of gender-based violence—particularly sexual violence—experienced by women. Previous studies have documented increased incidence of gender-based violence during times of water scarcity, and a number of recent assessments and rapid needs assessments reported an increased incidence of sexual violence against displaced women and girls. Increased incidences of sexual violence are also associated with the breakdown of traditional social networks between neighbours and communities that occur when people are forced to relocate in search of water.

108. Additional gender-responsive health co-benefits that will be achieved in the proposed project include a reduction in the horizontal transmission of HIV/AIDS and other STIs among women because of improved food security. This is because women who have access to sufficient food are less likely to engage in risky sexual behaviour, including sex work—which exposes them to the disease. The project will also generate gender-responsive economic benefits for women by increasing their income through increased agricultural productivity on smallholder farms (approximately 25%), many of whom are smallholder farmers. These women, in particular, will benefit from improved access to markets and credit facilities. Women will also benefit from improved water and sustainable land management as well as the extension of agricultural finance and provision of climate-resilient agricultural inputs such as seeds and fertilizers. This will lead to increased food security for these households, with increased livestock production levels overall, as well as increased levels of food availability and a decline in average food prices.

109. The promotion of gender equality has been mainstreamed throughout proposed project interventions wherever possible. In addition, the project will contribute to the achievement of the Millennium Development Goals by promoting the gender-sensitive development beyond the conclusion of the project.


Vulnerability of the country

113. Ranking 162nd out of 188 countries on the 2016 UN Human Development Index, Haiti faces multiple development challenges including poverty, food insecurity, lack of access to basic services, unemployment and environmental degradation. These challenges are compounded by extreme climate events such as hurricanes and tropical storms that cause widespread damage to infrastructure and loss of life.

The country is the inhabitants of the SIDS and LDCs, most Haitians are particularly vulnerable to impacts of these events. Indeed, Haiti has been ranked among the top 10 countries in terms of vulnerability to natural disasters. It is considered one of the world’s most vulnerable countries to acute shocks and chronic stresses of climate change. The country was heavily affected by the 2010 earthquake, which killed over 300,000 people. Haiti has been affected by hydrometeorological disasters that occurred during the period 1998-2016. Additionally, with a vulnerability index score of 57%, Haiti is considered as one of the most vulnerable country to climate change in Latin America and the Caribbean.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND WIND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 54

171. In the last two decades, Haiti has experienced 26 floods (19 of which were catastrophic), 8 hurricanes, and numerous other extreme climate events such as tropical storms and earthquakes. With a total of 55 extreme climate events in the period from 1990 to 2017, Haiti is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change impacts. In 1999-2018, the country ranks in the top three at risk countries to climate change impacts[3]. Of these impacts, the damage caused by extreme weather events has been particularly significant. For example, Hurricane Jeanne caused severe flooding in the northern part of Haiti, resulting in more than 2,600 deaths and approximately $1 billion in damages. The hurricane also resulted in 54,000 displaced people, over 37,000 deaths and the loss of up to 90% of crops and livestock in some areas[4]. The frequency and intensity of flood events have increased significantly since 2008, with an average of one extreme weather event per year. This is a result of projected higher average annual temperatures and increased intensity and concentration of precipitation events. Haiti has experienced more than 150 days of rain in the last decade[5]. The country’s agricultural sector is already extremely vulnerable to external shocks caused by climate change. This is because Haiti is currently located at the bottom of the global food security index, with a score of 23 out of 100. In addition, there are limited implementation of climate resilient approaches to managing the cross-sectoral impacts of flooding in the country. There is also a lack of capacity and resources for the government to provide technical assistance and training to countries for importing critical supplies. For example, approximately 50% of the national budget comes from foreign aid[6]. The agricultural sector accounts for about 32% of GDP and employs more than half of the population in Haiti. In addition, agriculture being the primary economic activity. Extreme climate events are already affecting more smallholder farmers who have limited access to credit or insurance products that can help them recover becoming unaffordable. The cumulative impact of these factors inhibits the capacity of national and local decision-makers to implement long term strategies for responding to the adverse impacts of climate change on the country’s population.

Vulnerability of marginalized groups

172. The TR region is one of three departments in Haiti (namely the Artibonite, North and Northwest departments), the Northwest department has the joint highest number of inhabitants living under poverty, with an extreme vulnerability to flooding events. In contrast, the TR region has a relatively low population density. It is particularly prevalent in the Tris Rivers (TR) region, which is the most populous of the three watersheds in the country and where 80% of Haiti’s population lives[7]. The TR region is also one of the poorest regions with an average income at $365 per year. High levels of poverty and food insecurity experienced in the TR region contribute directly to increased vulnerability.

173. Within the Northwest department, the TR region is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding. The reason for this are two first: First, the region’s steep topography contributes to increased runoff during heavy rainfall events. Second, the population density and poverty levels in the region are high. The region is located near Haiti's north coast which increases the exposure of the region's population to the impacts of hurricanes and tropical storms. In addition, the region has a very low level of infrastructure development. The majority of flood and extreme events are compounded by land degradation in the TR region, resulting from deforestation and unsustainable agricultural practices such as slash-and-burn agriculture[8]. This is further exacerbated by increasing prevalence of annual crop cultivation which leaves the soil bare during most of the year – and charcoal production for cooking fuel. In addition, the majority of households in the region are smallholder farmers whose practices have resulted in large-scale changes in the natural landscape of the TR region, with the clearing of trees, in particular, to make way for agriculture[9]. The combination of these factors has led to increased soil erosion and land degradation within the catchment area, further exacerbating flood impacts.

Socioeconomic status

174. Haiti is the poorest LDC in the Western Hemisphere and the least wealthy country in the America with an annual GDP per capita of approximately US$850. Poverty is endemic with approximately 95% of the population living below the poverty line[10]. The average income for a household in Haiti is $234 per year, which is less than one third of the poverty line of US$1,236 per day. In rural areas, poverty is highest; where 52% of the population and 63% of extreme poor live[11]. The majority of households are smallholder farmers who have limited access to credit or adopt adequate livelihoods, resulting in an increased dependence on agriculture. Approximately 40% of the population is dependent

#### Institutional capacity needs

175. The country's small and market base as well as its high public debt—outstanding external debt was cancelled under the HIPC Initiative in December 2016, but the last tranche of the HIPC Initiative was suspended on 31st December 2017—constitute the government’s ability to allocate funding from the national budget for climate change adaptation. The country has a limited capacity to mobilize domestic resources for addressing climate change impacts, which is estimated to cost the country approximately US$200 million per year by 2030. Given the projected increase in future climate change conditions, external investment is critical to increase the climate resilience of Haiti’s population.

#### National water and land resource management

176. Most national and development partners are aware of the adverse impacts of climate change induced flooding on land and water resources in the TR catchment. However, there is limited knowledge on adaptation strategies that can be implemented at scale to address these challenges. The current approach to flood management in the region. Consequently, people in the TR region that rely on farming for their livelihoods are vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased flooding, soil erosion and degradation of land under climate change conditions. For example, the cultivation of annual crops leaves soil bare over much of the year, which increases its vulnerability to runoff and erosion. The lack of vegetation cover surface cover and reduced infiltration capacity of the soil, subsequently exacerbating the impacts of flooding on the landscape. In addition, the lack of infrastructure for flood management in the region is a major challenge and government budgets are extremely limited. As a result, on-farm decision-making is aimed at sustaining livelihoods rather than addressing climate change impacts.

177. Most national and development partners agree that there is an urgent need to develop a strategy of climate-related activities within the catchment. GCF resources supported by government co-financing will therefore be used to support the implementation of this strategy, which will include: (i) strengthening the approach in the region; and ii) strengthening the technical and institutional capacities of national and local decision-makers to address climate change impacts on land and water resources.

178. The proposed project is strongly aligned with Haiti’s national priorities, having been designed in collaboration with relevant stakeholders as outlined below:

- **The revised National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA)** developed in 2017 identified four priority areas for addressing the impacts of climate change—namely, soils, agriculture, coastal zones and water resources. The project will contribute to these priorities by addressing the negative impacts of climate change such as flooding, hurricanes and tropical storms, and addressing these negative impacts will contribute to the achievement of Haiti’s national development goals. The project is also aligned with other national-level plans and priorities centred around climate change adaptation and mitigation. For example, the project will be integrated into the revised NAPA which includes the Interim Cooperation Frameworks (ICF) and Poverty Reduction Strategy.

## Strategic Plan (SPS) [18] These policies share common goals relevant to the proposed project such as ramping up agricultural production in a sustainable way, preventing land degradation and improving access to water resources for agriculture and other uses, while also addressing the vulnerability of communities to climate change alongside the National Disaster Risk Management Plan (NDMP) [20]. The SPS was developed by the Government of Haiti through the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), released in 1999, which prioritises addressing the impacts of climate change, particularly flooding, on land and water resources management. The NAP also outlines a series of priority actions to be taken as part of the response to priority needs outlined within this national plan, as well as other plans that have been integrated into it. These include: (i) increasing access to agricultural inputs; (ii) improving access to information on land and water resources management to increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities in the TR region.

### The proposed project aims to build on achieved results of the national adaptation planning process (NAP), processes for the development of sectoral regional action plans (SRAPs), and the implementation of the NAP, as well as the strengthening of existing frameworks and systems. The NAPA called for increased local and sub-regional capacity in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of climate change adaptation sectoral Regional Programmatic Approaches (RAPs). This approach aims to better focus the efforts of government agencies on the most pressing needs of communities at risk from climate change impacts by implementing projects and programs, and to better meet the needs of populations in the regions of the country. The project will build on the NAPA and the RAPs, as well as other national and international initiatives, and to their capacities for influencing and implementing localized actions on climate change adaptation and sustainable development at the local level.

### The Strategic Development Plan for Haiti (SDPH) [21] (Plan Stratégique de Développement d’Haïti) of 2012 sets out a vision for Haiti’s future, with a focus on poverty reduction. It is based on four pillars: (i) building resilience to climate change impacts by: a) mainstreaming climate change considerations into planning and assessment processes; b) improving the capacity of communities to adapt to climate change impacts; c) restoring degraded ecosystems; and d) managing use of water resources and forests in a sustainable way. The project will contribute to these goals through its focus on building resilience to climate change by increasing the climate resilience of the TR region through ecosystem restoration, as well as improved watershed and land management.

### Haiti’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) [22] has the following adaptation priorities: i) enhancing the capacity of communities in vulnerable areas to adapt to climate change; ii) implementing conservation activities on 157,000 ha of land; iii) strengthening the protection of coastal areas; and iv) preserving marine protected areas. The project will contribute to these goals by promoting the adoption of an integrated approach to resilient land and water resources management supported by agroforestry investments is, therefore, strongly aligned with Haiti’s NDC.

### The Initial (First) and Second National Communication on Climate Change to the UNFCCC [23] and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) [24] highlight the need for a more integrated approach in communications emphasis the need to: i) implement climate-resilient solutions that will address the impacts of climate change; ii) promote the adoption of an integrated approach to land and water resources management in the country; iii) promote the adoption of an integrated approach to land and water resources management in the country; and iv) build capacity for the implementation of such approaches. The project will contribute to these goals by promoting integrated land and water resources management, informed by best practices generated from past projects and/or programmes across the country and Caribbean region. Specifically, project interventions will promote the adoption of an integrated approach to land and water resources management in the country that is based on a participatory process and builds upon existing decentralized management of natural resources towards climate-resilient sustainable development.

## Stakeholder engagement

The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultations with members of the Haitian government and civil society organizations (CSOs) in Haiti. The MEF, the MoH, the MoI have all been involved in the development of this project since inception through their valuable contributions to the consultation process. These stakeholders also provided feedback on the draft project document during its preparation phase.

The proposed project has been validated by all the relevant project stakeholders, including the NDA.

180. Consultations were organized by UNDP in the Nord-West, Nord and Artibonite regions of Haiti. Given the documented vulnerability to climate change and natural hazards in these regions, consultations with local communities supported by technical assistance to address this problem were prioritized by the GoH and other stakeholders involved in the project.

The Ministry of Public Works (Ministère des Travaux Publics - MTP), the Ministry of Finance and Economy (Direction Générale des Finances Publiques - DGFP), the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MEE), the National Directorate for Water Supply and Sanitation (DIRECTION NATIONALE DE L'ALIMENTATION EN EAU ET SANITÉ - DNEES), the Office of Rural Development and Sanitation Office (OIREPA) and irrigation associations — also underlined the need for a coordinated effort towards climate change adaptation in their respective regions. The MEE, DGFP, and MTP noted that the floods induced flooding. Additional adaptation priorities that were discussed during stakeholder consultations included the need to address the impacts of climate change on water resources management, including the development of projects, donors, and their executing entities; further information on climate change and adaptation solutions to facilitate the identification of potential project activities in this area; and the implementation of measures to reduce the impact of climate change and natural hazards on vulnerable communities in the regions.

181. During the implementation phase, all target beneficiary groups will remain engaged to ensure that the benefits communicated during the project preparation phase are realized. The GoH has committed to engaging with stronger inputs from stakeholders, while allowing for challenges that may be encountered to be addressed early on, which will help to ensure a more effective and efficient project implementation.

The stakeholder engagement process relevant to the project development and implementation phases are presented in Annex 7: Stakeholder engagement process. The environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and climate change adaptation measures presented in Section 10 of Annex 6: Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Management Plan.

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The GCF will provide US$24 million to the GoH to finance implementation of integrated water resources management (IWRM) in response to the impacts of flooding on the Trois Rivieres (TR) watershed. The project will also provide US$36 million for climate change adaptation, including US$10 million** in co-financing for the implementation of project interventions.

Funding from the GCF will only be used to address the impacts of climate change and natural hazards on water resources management. These costs — such as business-as-usual operations and maintenance necessary to ensure sustainable land and water resources management, and the development of new infrastructure that will provide additional benefits using an integrated approach to land and water resources management that will contribute to achieving a paradigm shift in the country's approach to addressing the impacts of climate change-induced flooding**. grant is considered to be the most appropriate level of concessionality given: i) the public good nature of the project activities and lack of financial referees; ii) Haiti’s status as a SIDS and LDC; and iii) the level of debt of the country, with its limited capacity to make these investments. Indeed, the absence of financial resources required to implement the necessary investment acquisitions has been identified as a considerable barrier to implementing climate-resilient WRM in Haiti.

183. The combined investment of nearly US$31.58 million will be used efficiently and effectively to achieve three main project objectives: i) improving water management practices at catchment level; ii) increasing the resilience of communities living in the TR watershed to improve the infiltration of water into soil and increase surface recharge (this will contribute to reducing the prevalence of waterborne diseases); iii) increasing the productivity of agricultural livelihoods, including even export. A shift from unsustainable land use practices, including charcoal production, towards productive land uses such as agroforestry and tree-based farming systems is expected to be achieved through capacity building of institutional capacity of decision-makers involved in agriculture and charcoal production at the commune, inter-communal and national levels. This will be done by providing training on climate-resilient WRM and sustainable land management techniques. Specifically, communal development councils and multi-sectoral management structures will be established and implemented to ensure that communities are able to implement and sustainably manage their agricultural interventions. To support this, training will be provided to representatives from relevant government entities, including the Ministry of Agriculture, Environment and Natural Resources (MENR), as well as local NGOs. An agricultural financial mechanism will be established under the VSCA. Under this mechanism, financing will be made available for farmers who are able to demonstrate that they have adopted climate-resilient WRM and sustainable land management practices.

184. The last, required governance framework for land and water resources management will be established and implemented at catchment and sub-catchment levels in the TR watershed to optimise the sustainability of project interventions as well as a summary of the international best practices and lessons learned used to inform project design.

## Cost-effectiveness of project interventions

185. Climate change-induced flooding in Haiti severely impacts vulnerable communities living in the TR region through increased prevalence of waterborne diseases, loss of livelihoods, reduction in productivity of agricultural livelihoods and increased prevalence of waterborne diseases. The projected reduction in total average annual rainfall combined with an increase in temperature will lead to more frequent and intense precipitation events which are expected to exacerbate these flood impacts. In order to take decisions regarding the type of climate change adaptation options that the project should implement, it is necessary to compare the costs associated with each option against their respective adaptation expenditure options and expected internal rate of return (IRR) on these options. To do this, a financial and economic analysis was undertaken to estimate the net present value (NPV) of interventions in terms of facilitating sustainable catchment management practices with the strengthening of agricultural value chains; and/or supporting the development of alternative livelihoods.

186. The economic analysis estimated the net present value (NPV) of interventions to be US$736 million with an internal rate of return (IRR) of 40.7%, therefore representing a highly cost-effective spend for Haiti. The estimated NPV is based on the assumption that the project will have a positive impact on the livelihoods and well-being of project beneficiaries, likely increase in prevalence because of increased flooding and related impacts. Relevant literature on the link between these diseases and floods was used to estimate the likely number of additional cases.

For consistency purposes, all proposals developed with the support of UNDP are based on the IRR of 10% discount rate. The EIRR is calculated using the interest rates offered by national development banks.

191 Project components included in the economic analysis are health benefits of the project and improvement in rural livelihoods. Both of these benefits are estimated on an annual basis, comparing with and without project scenarios.

### Health benefits

Health benefits were limited to a reduction in the number of cases of cholera and mortality. These reductions were estimated by constructing a scenario of the potential number of such cases (of cholera and mortality) that could be prevented if the project was implemented. The estimates are based on the number of such cases with climate change with the project. Three scenarios are developed in terms of possible impacts of the project: no impact, 10% reduction; 25% reduction; and 30% - 40% reduction. On best-based available information, a cost per case of cholera in Haiti was estimated at US$879.60 (a sensitivity analysis on this estimate was also calculated). Death cases were valued using the average annual income of a rural household in Haiti as a proxy for the value of statistical life (VSL) of $232,500 to be used in the analysis (a sensitivity analysis on this estimate was also calculated).

### Rural livelihood benefits

A financial analysis of agroforestry was carried out based on field data. The table below provides a summary of these results with and without GCF support:

| Perspective | Without GCF Support | With GCF Support | Post-flood Feedback |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Smallholder farmer | US$ 1,836.00 | US$ 4,925.00 | 20% | 42% | 4% |

### Additional assumptions for the economic analysis are:

- As reported in the Feasibility Study, the average farm size within the TCF catchment ranges between 0.8 and 1.3 hectares.
- The average number of trees per hectare is estimated to be around 500. It is assumed that average farm size is 1–1.3 hectares.
- It is also assumed that the number of households benefiting from the agroforestry activities of the project is 17,249 (based on a sample survey conducted in 2016).
- Profit margins in the sector are estimated to be approximately 10%.

### It is important to note that the net incremental benefits from agroforestry are deducted by US$ 15 per ha which is an estimate of the foregone net revenues from charcoal production, (T).

### Infrastructure benefits

The proposed investment project shows a significant reduction of the projected extent of flood damage in comparison with the baseline scenario. The project reduces exposure to projected floods (relative to a no-project scenario). There is however limited information currently available on historic losses and damages due to flooding in Haiti. In 2014, the World Bank conducted a survey among households that estimated the direct loss due to flooding in 2004 for interviewing households through the Gonave region (documented in Table 3 of the Feasibility Study). The average cost per household was US$859.67 (including the houses due to flood damages) at between US$690.85 and US$1,049.84 per household. It is important to note that these figures are based on a sample survey conducted in 2014. The project will reduce flooding damage costs and not costs to public infrastructure, schools, hospitals, public administration. Therefore, the cost of flood damages due to flooding in the baseline scenario (without the project) showing damages the project will reduce. These costs were included in the economic analysis without changing results significantly.

### Economic efficiency

In the base case, the NPV of the project is estimated to reach between $375 and $412 million depending on the scenario of flood reduction benefits developed earlier (as presented above). The IRR ranges between 6% and 45%.

## Application of best practices in land and water resources management

### Best practices identified for WRM interventions
- **Engage stakeholders:** Regular engagement with stakeholders through participatory planning and monitoring processes are implemented using an integrated and iterative approach to account for the dynamic nature of landscape processes.
- **Regulatory equity:** The need to regularly engage stakeholders in an equitable manner to ensure that adaptation benefits from water resources management are equitably shared among all target groups.
- **Linking WRM to overall strategy:** Ensuring that the implementation of water resource planning and management is linked to Haiti's overall sustainable development strategy and public administration framework.
- **Adaptation to climate change:** The pressure on natural resources changes due to climate change will require a more adaptive approach in water resources management.
- **Inclusive decision-making:** Facilitating inclusive decision-making and adaptive management by ensuring that information is widely accessible and not controlled by any single stakeholder.
- **Supporting local communities:** Supporting local communities for farming, farmland, and local communities on climate-resilient agricultural practices as well as land and water resources management strategies.

### Additional best practices and lessons learned from the project are presented in Section 7 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

While several initiatives have attempted to introduce sustainable land systems in Haiti, the lack of a coordinated and integrated decision-making process by land planners and users as well as knowledge and technical capacity to implement actions to address economic vulnerability is compounded with effects of agroforestry and other agricultural practices in Haiti. Lack of information on the benefits of agroforestry at the community level for example have made this transformation difficult, in light of immediate economic needs and there are limited financial mechanisms that help enable these.

Together, project interventions will transform the landscape from one that is heavily reliant on uncontrolled land use to one that is focused on food management, towards an integrated approach. This will be achieved by replicating national policies linked to agroforestry (reducing the risk of soil erosion and increasing water infiltration) and uptake of project interventions across sectors. From a developmental perspective it will also provide social protection to communities during the transition period from uncontrolled land use management practices to encourage a just transition towards sustainable livelihoods, linking ESA to green growth opportunities. It is expected that these benefits will be achieved through VSCA to help not only sustain these gains but also increase their replicability through access to savings.

The replicability of the project interventions will be underpinned by the drafting of revisions to existing policies and the establishment of pilot projects in the region of Artibonite in Haiti as well as the establishment of a Sub-Catchment Water Resource Users Association (WRA) for watersheds (Output 3). As a result of these outputs, there will be a new policy for WRM in Haiti that addresses both the existing and emerging challenges related to uncontrolled system of land management, water resources and climate change. This will also address the impacts of climate-change-induced flooding. The project will work with the relevant government entities, including the Ministry of Agriculture, to ensure implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustainable livelihoods are transferred across other regions in the country.

# Sustainability

Although the establishment of a Water Act has been identified as one of the Ministry of Environment and Energy’s National Climate Change Plan, there are currently no regulations around climate-sensitive water or sustainable water management policies and governance in Haiti to allow for its productive implementation.

The country lacks overarching water management legislation, a broad-based standard practice for land use planning, or relevant strategies to promote integrated landscape management. Household make decisions based on their immediate interest without necessarily considering the impact of those decisions on the future, which has led to the long-term impacts on their lives, including the increasing vulnerability to climate impacts such as flooding.

<sup>18</sup>The change in food security will be measured using the Household Economy Approach — HEA. The HEA methodology is based on the analysis of family household livelihoods. It highlights how households manage their resources and respond to shocks, stresses and trends over time. The HEA approach classifies households into four socio-economic groups, namely the “very poor”, “poor”, “better off” and “haves”. ### E.A. GCF Outcome Level: Enabling environment (RMF core indicators 5-8 as applicable)

#### Baseline context
The current state of water management is organized along administrative and communal lines. As a result, there is overlap between existing water governance structures at the local and sub-catchment levels.

#### Rating for current state (baseline)
Low

#### Target scenario
The project will develop and implement a national strategy not only to regional level governance but also to the adaptation needs of vulnerable communities living in vulnerable areas. The Water Act will be strengthened to develop an enabling participatory integrated water resources management framework.

#### How the project will contribute
Vulnerable communities are in turn empowered to act as active participants through coordinated and inclusive governance mechanisms.

Plains, Marmelos, Giro Morro) will be trained on climate-resilient land-use management. 118 communities will be reached through this training and associated training, which will focus on:

- the expected impacts of climate change on the community's health and the associated consequences of these changes to the community's livelihoods.
- the link between land use and hydrology of the TR watershed, including unsustainable land practices on the frequency and intensity of flooding in the watershed.
- adaptation strategies, climate-smart agriculture, such as crop diversification, climate-resilient crops, agroforestry, soil conservation, etc., as well as the importance of protecting forest areas for maintaining the hydrological function of the watershed and community livelihoods.
- the role of the community in reforestation, afforestation, and reforestation, as a strategy for increasing resilience to climate change.
- practical aspects of agroforestry as a climate-resilient livelihood alternative.

## Activity 1.2: Implement ecosystem-based flood management solutions in the Tros-Rivieres watershed

This project activity will focus on reducing flooding in the TR catchment and Port-de-Paix, by implementing agroforestry systems (AFS) in priority areas within the watershed.

### 1.2.1 Establish 17,740 ha of agroforestry systems to protect against flooding in the Tros watershed

The project will establish 17,740 ha of agroforestry systems and trees on private lands in priority areas in the watershed. Implementing these systems will help reduce peak flood runoff, with rehabilitation measures aimed at reducing floodplain extents in the target area.

- **17,740 ha** of agroforestry systems
  - Agroforestry systems – which are a combination of crops and trees such as cocoa, coffee, and silvopastoral systems – which combine crops, trees, and domesticated animals; and agroecological systems that combine crops, trees, and cattle.
- **60 ha** of forests in priority areas within the Tros watershed

## Actions 2.1: Strengthen traditional knowledge and practices in sustainable land-use management

Under this activity, a capacity needs assessment will be conducted to identify the technical and institutional constraints that hinder the agricultural sector from unsustainable land uses. The approach will be based on the landscape management (LM) approach to be used for the development of climate-resilient landscapes. Capacity will be strengthened at the community level in seven priority communes in the TR region.

### 2.1.1 Conduct capacity gap needs assessment
- Identify and prioritize gaps in seven priority communes in the TR region.
- Establish communal development structures to oversee and manage the implementation of LM activities in priority areas identified in Sub-activity 2.1.3.
- Train representatives from appropriate government entities, including MARDNR, NCFs and MSAL, on GUM in the TR watershed.

### 2.1.2 Restore 7,500 ha of forests in priority areas through planting indigenous tree species
- Engage community leaders, farmers, and women's associations to facilitate implementation of Sub-activities 1.1.2 and 1.1.3.

produce (e.g., by developing agricultural tracks, as well as improving water supply systems (e.g., irrigation and water pumps) and storage facilities). The interventions caused by the impacts of climate change, such as increased temperatures, reduced rainfall, etc., will also be undertaken in the form of investments to improve the overall quality of post-harvest facilities, as well as establishing cold chain infrastructure for agricultural produce to optimise agricultural production and increase market access. These interventions will be supported by the implementation of a Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) approach that is tailored to the specific needs of smallholder farmers in vulnerable households that depend on agriculture for their livelihoods.

This will be supplemented by delivering training workshops on climate-resilient agricultural techniques and SLM to smallholder farmers, thereby directly enhancing their ability to effectively manage their farms. This will include: i) facilitating participatory assessments of farm equipment, maintenance facilities; and ii) equipping them with the necessary tools.

Strengthening these key elements of the agricultural value chain in Haiti's TR watershed will contribute significantly to climate resilience of agricultural production in the region. The interventions will increase food security in the watershed by providing smallholder farmers and those who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods with increased market access will be supported by the implementation of a credit finance mechanism under the Agricultural
