## Activity 2.3: Implement a social protection system to reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers at risk to food insecurity because of climate change

### Village Savings and Credit Associations (VSCAs)
Financing will be made available to smallholder farmers under this mechanism for the establishment, operation, and implementation of sustainable, climate-resilient agricultural production systems. The farmers will be able to access financing from VSCAs immediately after the impacts of a climate change event have been assessed locally. This will promote the adoption of more resilient agricultural practices that reduce vulnerability to climate change.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.1: Implement a long-term solution addressing these needs
Specifically, Activity 2.3.1 provides a long-term solution to addressing these needs by providing the necessary resources for smallholder farmers affected by these events to adopt more resilient agricultural practices and adapt their environment by providing an alternative source of income through sustainable value chain production. Specifically, Activity 2.3.1 will provide social safety net whereby food coupons will be provided to vulnerable households affected by food insecurity. To determine which households are most vulnerable, a list of the most vulnerable households will be identified in each village and linked with their details captured in a database to ensure that only those households receive the coupons.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.2: Establish and operationalize a pilot coupon mechanism in the TIR watershed
The food coupons will be redeemable at both primary and secondary markets. These coupons can be used for cash or e-coupons, which will provide recipients with the ability to exchange the coupons for specific goods. A list of vendors will also participate in the operation of the food

#### Sub-activity 2.3.3: Conduct a site survey/assessment
The survey will assess the feasibility of receiving food coupons to be distributed under sub-activity 2.3.2.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.4: Establish and operationalize a pilot coupon mechanism in the TIR watershed to enhance the food security of smallholder farmers

### Operational food coupons mechanism
- Site survey/assessment
- Establish and operationalize a pilot coupon mechanism in the TIR watershed to enhance the food security of smallholder farmers
- 150 new VSCAs created in the TIR watershed
- A network of village agents to monitor existing VSCAs as well as to monitor market performance and food coupons mechanisms

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 90

## Activity 3.2: Develop an integrated climate-resilient water management governance framework at the catchment and sub-catchment levels in the basin, taking into account available resources among all users.

The activity will establish a landscape management approach that is complementary to the existing Water Resources Management Plan (WRMP) and will be based on a landscape management approach that is consistent with the relevant land use plans and community land use plans (Activity 1.1). The activity will also train the Catchment Integrated Water Resources Management Committee (CWRMC), together with the relevant land users, in the application of the WRMP in decision making.

### Sub-activity 3.2.1: Establish the Catchment Integrated Water Resources Management Committee (CWRMC) to manage water resources at the sub-catchment level.
* **Establish CWRMC:** The CWRMC will work with representatives from production sector users, land users at the catchment and sub-catchment levels and Sub-Catchment Water Resources User Associations (SCWRUAs) to achieve two main objectives: (i) to develop climate-resilient WRMP plans for end users at the sub-catchment level; and (ii) to promote a participatory, gender-responsive approach in the development of the CWRMC and production sector user engagement with the SCWRUAs.

### Sub-activity 3.2.2: Develop climate-resilient WRMP plans for end users at the sub-catchment level.
* **Develop Climate Resilient WRMP Plans:** The CWRMC will work in a participatory, gender-responsive approach to develop climate-resilient WRMP plans for end users at the sub-catchment level and Sub-Catchment Water Resources User Associations (SCWRUAs).

### Sub-activity 3.2.3: Promote a climate-resilient water management governance framework in the TR watershed that is consistent with the relevant land use plans and community land use plans.
* **Promote Climate Resilient Framework:** The CWRMC will work to promote a climate-resilient water management governance framework at the sub-catchment level, which is consistent with the relevant land use plans and community land use plans.

### Sub-activity 3.2.4: Train the CWRMC (established under Sub-activity 3.2.1) and selected SCWRUAs in a participatory approach to water resources management.
* **Train CWRMC:** The CWRMC will be trained together with relevant land users, including SCWRUAs, on the application of the WRMP in decision making.

### Training delivered to the CWRMC
* **Training Delivery:** Training will be provided by the CWRMC (established under Sub-activity 3.2.1) and representatives from production sector users at the catchment level and Sub-Catchment Water Resources User Associations (SCWRUAs).

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

200. Project level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy. The Project Manager and Monitoring Officer will ensure that all project activities are monitored on a regular basis to assess progress towards achieving the purposes of all project activities within the outcome-based indicators presented in the Logical Framework (Section E of this Funding Proposal). Any delays or challenges experienced during the implementation phase, as well as the responsible parties for undertaking these activities, shall be reported and corrective measures required can be achieved efficiently. The Project Manager will also ensure that all project staff maintain transparency.

201. Monitoring and evaluation of project activities will be undertaken on an annual basis. The type of monitoring activities to be conducted during the implementation phase, as well as the responsible parties for undertaking these activities, are detailed in Annex 11: Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan. Such


---
**Page 97**
---

activities include field observations, key informant interviews and focus group sessions. The costs associated with undertaking the necessary monitoring, assessment and reporting requirements will be informed in Annex 4: Detailed budget.

202. A project inception workshop shall be held once the proposed project has been approved to (i) orient project stakeholders to the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that may influence project implementation; (ii) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team, including reporting and communication protocols; (iii) review the project’s logical framework, work plan, monitoring and evaluation plan, and financial plan; (iv) define roles and responsibilities and finalise the M&E plan (Annex 11); v) review the financial reporting procedures and mandatory requirements, and agree on the arrangements for submitting the quarterly financial reports to UNDP; vi) discuss the project’s gender strategy and identify any gender-related issues. The results of that inception reception will be shared with the UNDP Country Office, UNDP Regional Technical Advisor and Project Board for clearance. Once the report has been reviewed by the Project Board, it will be sent to the GCF for approval. This will happen once the project is funded after the Funded Agreement (FA) becomes effective.

203. An annual progress report (APR) will be prepared by the Project Manager, UNDP Country Office, and UNDP Regional Technical Advisor for submission to the GCF. The APR will include a summary of the Project’s Management Support, Monitoring and Evaluation activities. Any changes or modifications included in the project Logical Framework are monitored annually and that the results from regular M&E activities are presented in the GCF APR. Any challenges encountered during implementation shall be reported on an ongoing basis.

204. During the implementation phase, specific means of verification will be used to assess: (i) progress of project interventions towards achieving GCF Fund level impacts and Fund level outcomes; and (ii) performance of interventions against the project performance indicators presented in Section E.4 of this Funding Proposal. The Project Manager shall ensure that all project activities are being implemented as per the approved work plan, budget and logical framework to meet and ensure that gender targets are met. The Monitoring and Gender Officer will design and operationalise a performance monitoring framework to track the progress of the project against its indicators and report on the same in the quarterly reports submitted to the National Steering Committee (NSC) and Project Management Unit (PMU). The NSC shall review these reports at each meeting, and provide feedback to the Project Manager. The PMU will also review these reports and provide feedback to evaluate the progress of the project. It reporting the project’s performance to the National Steering Committee (NSC) and Project Management Unit (PMU), and providing feedback on the same in the quarterly reports submitted to the NSC and PMU, respectively.

205. UNDP will be responsible for managing the Independent Interim mid-term Final Evaluation of the project, including the process for contracting these services with an independent evaluator. The evaluation shall be conducted within 18 months from the date of signing of this Funding Proposal (i.e., at the project’s mid-term end of Year 3). This will be an outcomes-based assessment and will include analysing whether the project is on track, what problems and challenges have been encountered during implementation, and how these were addressed. The evaluation shall also assess the extent to which the project has achieved its objectives in the most efficient and sustainable way. Following this evaluation, the NSC will develop a management response to the evaluation recommendations along with an implementation plan for undertaking the required actions. It is the responsibility of UNDP to assess whether the agreed recommendations are being described below.

### The project’s gender targets as well as the associated gender-disaggregated indicators are presented in Annexes 5: Gender Assessment and Action Plan.

## IMPLEMENTATION DURING THE REMAINDER OF THE PROJECT PERIOD

The completed Interim Evaluation report will be cleared by the UNDP Country Office as well as the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor and submitted to the Project Board for approval.

### INDEPENDENT CONSULTANT FOR FINAL EVALUATION REPORT

An independent consultant will be contracted by UNDP to conduct the Final Evaluation Report at the end of project implementation. UNDP will be responsible for overseeing the Final Evaluation Report, which is a summative evaluation. This evaluation will include an independent assessment of the project's overall performance and impact against the indicators presented in the Logical Framework. The evaluation will be based on inter alia documentations of evidence, field observation visits and interviews with key stakeholders. The report will be submitted to the UNDP Country Office for clearance as well as the UNDP Regional Technical Advisor for clearance. Once the report has been cleared, it will be submitted to the Project Board for approval.

### PLANNED PROJECT EVALUATIONS IN THE UNDP COUNTRY OFFICE

The UNDP Country Office will include the planned project evaluations in the UNDP Country Office evaluation plan. All evaluation reports, including responses to feedback received from the public, will be uploaded in English to the UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre (ERC). The UNDP Country Office will retain all MAE records for this project for up to eight years after project financial closure to support post-evaluations.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor: Technical and operational

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | High |

**Description:** Low survival rates of crops and trees planted during the project makes stabilisation of slopes ineffective, hampering improvements in soil conservation practices.

### Mitigation Measure(s):

- Specifically selecting and using robust (indigenous) species that are well adapted to local conditions, with high levels of fire resistance and good re-sprouting capacity.
- Prioritising community participation in planning and implementation for agroforestry.
- Involving the community in planning at the inception stage to foster ownership and promote long-term monitoring and protection activities.
- Participating practicily planning for reforestation activities; and
- Notifying local communities of the importance of monitoring and protection of ground cover for ecosystem service provision.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

### Category: Other

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | High |

**Description:** Weak land tenure, use -and management systems prevent or delay implementation if agreements with landowners or communities cannot be reached.

### Mitigation Measure(s):

- Ownership of project interventions will be secured by the Community Land Trust (Plan under Action 1.1), including assigning land tenure and prioritising areas for interventions based on community recommendations and agreements.
- Once implementing the legislative framework for climate-related aspects of land management is not within the scope of the project, other risk mitigation measures include:
    - Ensuring that local communities are involved in the TR watershed at the inception and planning stage to establish trust and support in the validation of land ownership;
    - Using a community process during the assessment phase as a platform to address concerns and increase buy-in; of communities;
    - Implementing an environmental health monitoring system, helping to work towards more sustainable agricultural and sub-catchment management practices; and
    - Capturing upon completion of ecosystem services assessments, the development of a sub-catchment Water Users' Associations (WURAs) applies to land and water resource management to ensure that local needs and experiences are considered during project implementation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 96

## Selected Risk Factor 3
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | High |

### Description
Limited uptake of project interventions by target communities because of a preference towards fulfilling the immediate needs for resilience (e.g., food security) over long-term climate action.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Risk mitigation resources will include:
  - building community interest in engaging Haitian civil society organisations (CSOs) and working with community governance systems and associations to raise awareness of the challenges and impacts of climate on change or climate change.
  - training and knowledge sharing initiatives to facilitate capacity building and ownership; and
  - ensuring that project interventions are designed to be sustainable (sub-) catchment management and WRM, as well as food security and nutrition interventions, which are designed to be inclusive.

- Working with community members in monitoring the project's indicators to establish a link between project action and impact.

## Selected Risk Factor 4
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description
Inadequate national focus on adaptation measures because of other development pressures affecting the sustainability of the proposed project beyond GCF involvement.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Building a strong watershed management is increasingly becoming a national priority and is expected to develop into a sustained focus on adaptation in Haiti; and
- An integrated approach will ensure sustainable climate change adaptation and ensure the sustainability of the integrated management approach.
- Aligning the project within national policy efforts including water management and the National Adaptation Plan process.

## Selected Risk Factor 5
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

### Description
Continued overexploitation and unregulated land use and tenure renders soil conservation measures redundant, causing increased degradation and permanent loss of productive capacity.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Through an integrated flood management planning approach, the project is intended to address environmental degradation, particularly in areas where there are high levels of land-use in areas directly impacted by climate change.
- The improved institutional capacity will also contribute to reducing degradative practices through better planning, policy decision making and natural resource-management capacities.
- The project will promote active involvement of the local community through consultations to promote active involvement in sub-catchment WRM participation on crop/tree planting activities and other land-use management activities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 97

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

## Description
Weak coordination between stakeholders, climate initiatives and implementation partners reduce the effectiveness of WRM approaches in Haiti.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The risk will be mitigated by promoting coordination through the strategic identification of established multi-stakeholder platforms (e.g., National Adaptation Strategy) to facilitate collaboration among relevant actors. Coordination between these groups will be strengthened through workshops and knowledge-sharing initiatives aimed at sharing lessons learned, identifying gaps in existing approaches, developing best practices to learn from, synchronizing efforts, and by replicating successes from each project.

Risk will further be reduced by involving multiple actors in supporting capacity (WRM, including NDC), private sector, civil society organizations, and other relevant stakeholders. This will ensure that the interests of all relevant stakeholders within MCD and other relevant sectoral ministers, contributing towards coordination and integration across sectors.

The project will report regularly to the Technical Secretariat of the National Committee on Climate Change to ensure alignment with the Govt own Climate Strategies including the country's National Adaptation Plan. The project will also provide regular updates to the various agencies through the various donor groups for environmental sector and for disaster risk reduction.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

## Description
Extreme weather event and/or natural disaster during project implementation or operations inhibits or delays progress.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Land-use plans taking these risks into account; incorporating drought-resistant crops during rainy wet weather periods; and choosing tree varieties and timing of planting to favour fast-growing species during the first planting cycle to should protect newly started seedlings from extreme weather events.

## Selected Risk Factor 8
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

## Description
Socio-political instability undermines project outputs because of disruptions and changes in national priorities and government operations.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
This risk will be mitigated by ensuring a continuity of process through: Empowering local social-political administration at both local and national levels based on technical results to allow for an easy transition; mainstreaming integrated food governance into the institutional framework at multiple levels and amongst different actors; defining roles and responsibilities and developing clear standard operating procedures (SOPs) for implementing stakeholders, including communities, in a participatory manner; and with clear knowledge on how to address instability at a territorial level when working with communities.


---
**Page 102**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 96

## Understanding that building initiatives at various levels looking to empower local government and mid-level technical officials that are less vulnerable to political disruptions. These actions allow technical work to continue even when the central government is not functioning.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

An initial conflict analysis has been prepared and included within the project's ESMP. The analysis and the project’s ESIF provides guidelines for the development of Security Plans and Conflict Assessments that are specific to each project site. These plans will be developed in consultation with local authorities and include specific management plans per grouped areas. To reduce the potential of project beneficiaries or staff being targeted, a security plan will be developed at each project site. This will include measures such as providing basic benefits (capacity building, agroforestry inputs, enhanced associative capacity, etc.). In terms of mobility, UNDP has established a mobile office in Abidjan and will continue to monitor the situation on the ground. It will be continuously monitored to allow for adaptive management in case it is required. Programme strategies will take into account the security situation at each project site and will be adapted accordingly as necessary.

In addition, a logistics and operations officer has been foreseen by the project to ensure that logistics take into account the security situation on the ground. The project will also work with local authorities to ensure that its community-based approach will allow for the project to ensure strong community buy-in and enhance social cohesion in the communities where it operates.

The project will also continue to monitor the security situation at each project site, and will adapt activities to avoid potential security risks while creating an opportunity to maximise the peacebuilding potential of the project.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

209. The ESMP will be prepared in line with the UNDP social and environmental screening procedure undertaken to ensure it complies with UNDP's Social and Environmental Standards. The social and environmental risk category for this project is considered as a low risk (see Section 3.4). The Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF), as well as the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), are presented in Annexes A and B.

209. UNDP will be responsible for the ESFM, ensuring it is adequate and followed. The PMU will be responsible for the revision or update of the ESMP document during project implementation and will ensure timely corrective actions are taken to address any non-conformances with the ESMP. Any non-conformances must be reported in writing and logged into the register. Depending on the severity of the non- conformity, the Site Supervisor may specify a corrective action plan (CAP) for the PMU to implement within 30 days or as required by the UNDP/GEF staff member year by year at project start-up and the MIE. The objective of the review is to update the document to reflect knowledge gained during implementation.

210. The progress of all corrective actions will be tracked using the register. Any non-conformances and the issue of corrective actions are to be advised to UNDP/UNICEF and the MIE and also release updates on the project or a regular newsletter. All information related to the ESMP, including any amendments, should be published in English for media, for example print, radio, social media or formal reports, and all material must be published in English and Haitian Creole. The ESMP will be available at the project office and on the website. The project office is intended to serve as a point of contact for enquiries, concerns, complaints and/or grievances. All enquiries, concerns, complaints and/or grievances should be directed to the project office.

211. The project has the potential to cause low to moderate environmental and social impacts. The Project's design has avoided all high-risk environmental and social impacts and favoured a wholly climate resilient infrastructure approach. The project will not require any land acquisition or resettlement, nor will it use pesticides and fertilizers in agriculture systems because of improved income; and is a low risk that there is a possibility of civil unrest due to the potential for conflict over water resources. These risks are discussed along with mitigation measures in the ESMP.

212. When undertaking the risk assessment, all activities were assessed with an emphasis on those with the potential for some adverse impact, specifically Output 1: Activity 1.2 which involves the project's physical interventions (depot construction and installation of water pumps). The project has avoided any high-risk environmental impacts such as reproduction disruptions against women based on gender, due to existing structural inequalities and roles in agriculture systems because of improved income; and is a low risk that there is a possibility of civil unrest due to the potential for conflict over water resources. These risks are discussed along with mitigation measures in the ESMP.

213. The preparation of the ESMP included public consultation as part of the Stakeholder Engagement Plan. The full stakeholders' engagement plan was prepared as part of the project in Annex 7: Stakeholder Engagement Plan, which includes the full list of stakeholders consulted and a summary of the consultations. The project has disseminated information to all stakeholders through meetings, workshops, seminars, presentations, newsletters, and individual community meetings (including marginalised groups) and was approved by the Government of Haiti. These measures were undertaken in order to ensure that the project is fully informed about overall project design and implementation. It is expected that conclusions with affected communities and beneficiaries will continue. It is anticipated that based on the community needs, the projects will be fully accepted.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

214. A Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) was developed for this project in order to provide an overview of the gender-related issues that are likely to arise as a result of climate change and how this information can be used to design a gender-sensitive adaptation intervention. The GAAP identifies gender issues related to access to water, land, and other resources; access to education and health services; and access to resources, gender-differentiated roles in employment and livelihoods, and roles in political decision


---
**Page 104**
---

making. The Gender Action Plan specifically describes opportunities for gender mainstreaming in the project design so that all proposed project interventions address gendered climate resilience across the household, community and institutional levels.

215. Haiti ranks third globally on the Long-Term Climate Risk Index (LRI), demonstrating its extreme vulnerability to climate change impacts which women are especially vulnerable. Widespread deforestation and soil degradation have made severe episodes of drought more frequent in recent years. Women can better provide critical ecosystem functions, such as seed dispersal and pollination, and they are often the first to notice changes in weather patterns. Episodes of heavy rain and flooding. This climate programming is expected to increase in frequency and intensity, while rainfall is expected to decrease, with tropical storms and hurricanes, are expected to increase in frequency and intensity, while rainfall is expected to decrease. Women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to land and water, and generate additional income through livelihoods which, in turn, diminishes their ability to advance out of poverty, particularly when they have to, example: lost their land due to the impacts of flooding during cyclone events.

17. Gender equality and women's empowerment are not addressed in most of Haiti's strategy or action plan documents. Women's vulnerability is often overlooked as a result of gender inequality and lack of access to resources and circumstances. In relative terms, women lack access to productive resources, and this has impacts on their health, food security, education, income generation and decision-making power. The impact of climate change on women barriers limiting participation and movement outside the household sphere is worsened by phenomena such as flooding during cyclone events. Women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to land and water, and generate additional income through livelihoods which, in turn, diminishes their ability to advance out of poverty, particularly when they have to, example: lost their land due to the impacts of flooding during cyclone events.

18. Over 40% of households in Haiti are headed by women, and this percentage has been on the rise since 2007 [14]. Today, there are more women living under extreme poverty than men, with a large proportion residing in rural areas. Women's vulnerability is often overlooked as a result of gender inequality and lack of access to resources and circumstances. The impact of climate change on women barriers limiting participation and movement outside the household sphere is worsened by phenomena such as flooding during cyclone events. Women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to land and water, and generate additional income through livelihoods which, in turn, diminishes their ability to advance out of poverty, particularly when they have to, example: lost their land due to the impacts of flooding during cyclone events.

19. To address gender dimensions within the project design and implementation, the Gender Action Plan will account for:
- specific strategies to target/identify female-headed households;
- differential vulnerability analysis;
- identification of gaps in gender equality through the use of sex-disaggregated data enabling the gender action plan to be more effective; and
- the need for a monitoring system to track progress of implementation, and holding individuals and institutions accountable for outcomes that promote gender equality.
- advocacy and awareness that is adjusted to most effectively reflect gender specific differences;
- and the inclusion of a Gender Specialized position/provision of advice within the project to implement gender-related activities.

G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

According to the current audit policies, UNDP will be appointing third-party auditors to conduct a scheduled audit of the implementing partner’s financial statements and internal controls based on the implementing partner's risk rating and UNDP's guidelines. A scheduled audit is used to determine whether the funds provided to the implementing partner were used for the appropriate purpose and in accordance with the terms of the Agreement. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit and/or an internal control audit.

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all documents submitted by accredited entities to the GCF. Accordingly, unless otherwise provided under applicable programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board. Additionally, the IDP states that the GCF may disclose any document or information that it deems necessary and/or in its sole discretion. With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed without prior approval of the GCF. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

- full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation of each confidential portion and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy; and
- redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP208

## Proposal name: Enhanced climate resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through Integrated Flood Management

Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/ies): Republic of Haiti

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Direct benefits to 292,600 people (2.7 per cent of the total population), targeting the most vulnerable communities; an 80 per cent reduction in peak runoff volume and a 135 ha tree cover (17.74 ha agroforestry) and 7,700 ha retention areas to reduce floodplain extent | Ongoing crises including gang-related insecurity could pose a risk that needs to be carefully managed |
| Shifting away from unsustainable land-use practices towards an ecosystem-resilient approach to land and water resources management, including community-based approaches. | |
| Increasing household incomes, community health outcomes, improved water and soil quality, and food security through building sustainable land use and guaranteeing income. | |
| Economically viable (economic internal rate of return: 22 per cent; net present value: USD 381 million) and aligned with USD 32/beneficiary (direct). | |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the annexes and addendum XVI, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 115**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Ecosystem-based flood management solutions implemented in 25,440 hectares of the Trois-Rivières watershed (total cost USD 18.40 million; GCF cost USD 18.29 million)

- **Component 1 aims to directly enhance the resilience of the TR catchment by improving water retention and reducing sedimentation in degraded mountainous slopes in the TR watershed**. An increase in concentrated and heavy rainfall,


---
**Page 116**
---

combined with the increasing frequency and intensity of hurricanes and tropical storms, has resulted in the rapid erosion of fertile soils throughout the TR watershed. This is exacerbated by the steep topography and highly deforested landscape of the watershed, which intensifies flow rates because of reduced soil infiltration capacity.

The combination of these factors often results in the destruction of crops, farmlands and agricultural infrastructure, as well as a loss of livestock and human lives during the flood events. The proposed intervention is an example of Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (EbA) climate change solutions expected to address these issues by significantly reducing peak out- and flooding extent in the TR watershed. This will be achieved through the implementation of agroforestry on the soil. More specifically, the agroforestry systems will be implemented in 17,740 ha of degraded agricultural land within the TR watershed, which will implement 2,793 ha of forest areas across the target areas.

The proposed activities include establishing nursery planting facilities (e.g. for fruit production and timber species) and providing ongoing technical assistance to farmers including on establishing and monitoring of nurseries, scheduling seedling planting, using technical packages for agroforestry systems, and developing a calendar for managing different growing times of many agroforestry species. The spaces between the agroforestry species will be filled with perennial crops (e.g. banana, pineapple) or annual crops (e.g. sweet potato, yams and other annuals).

This intervention, which is an example of EbA, will be supplemented by developing the capacity of communities to plan for climate change impacts through participatory processes regarding climate-resilient land-use planning. Thirty-three communities within the seven target communes will participate in this process with the aim of developing a community-based plan and the development of 13 community land-use plans, through community engagement in a consultative process. These plans will focus on identifying the impacts of climate change, potential adaptation strategies to reduce these impacts, and mitigation measures to address these impacts such as actions like the implementation of EbA solutions to reduce flood risk in the watershed. The plan will also identify the most appropriate agroforestry species and techniques to reduce the risk of increased land degradation under future climate change conditions.

Component 2: Climate-resilient agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets established through a participatory approach

The second component is an integrated approach that will be implemented in the Trans-Rhymo watershed (total cost USD 8.30 million; GCF cost: USD 6.97 million).

Component 2 will formulate a transformative shift in Haiti’s agricultural sector from unsustainable land use practices (e.g., slash-and-burn agriculture and clearing of forests for charcoal production) towards sustainable options that are resilient to the impacts of climate change, while also improving food security and livelihoods. This component will address the needs of the TR region, while reducing deforestation and land degradation and maintaining the integrity of its natural resources. The main objective is to create a more sustainable agricultural landscape where agroforestry systems will be created in the integrated flood management practices in this region. This component will be entirely funded and implemented by Heifer International for this project with co-finance funding from GCF. The specific objectives are to achieve the following institutional outcomes including: (1) strengthening the institutional capacity of the people who are involved in the technical assistance, extension services, and research and development at all levels for implementing sustainable and climate-resilient agricultural practices; (2) enhancing access to financing mechanisms that will support the implementation of agroforestry systems and implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices over the long-term; and (3) establishing a social protection system for households that face chronic food insecurity. The co-

**Page 117**

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A
- The proposed project will directly benefit 292,600 people (2.7 per cent of the total population), targeting the most vulnerable population.
- The project net present value (NPV) of interventions is estimated to be USD 207 million with an internal rate of return (IRR) of 15.8 per cent. This NPV represents a cost-effective spend for the Government of Haiti. The estimated health benefits under the economic and financial analysis [for waterborne diseases such as diarrhea and cholera] that would, without intervention, result in USD 20 million in direct costs to the government.
- The project is expected to cover (17,740 ha) agroforestry and (7,700 ha) restoration, expected to result in a 40 per cent reduction in peak run-off and reduce floodplain extent by 26 per cent in the areas with a 1 per cent annual exceedance probability (AEP) of floods.1

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: N/A
- The proposed project will catalyze a paradigm shift in Haiti’s approach to flood management in the TR watershed by initiating a shift away from uncoordinated decision-making.

1 A 1 per cent AEP implies a 1 in 100 chance of flood in any one year in the given area.


---
**Page 118**
---

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: N/A

Under the project, the technical and institutional capacity of relevant stakeholders at the national, provincial, and commune levels will be strengthened regarding implementation of an integrated approach to land and water resources management that is centred around sustainable watershed management.

Strengthening the Water Act will establish a legal foundation for optimizing the use of water resources without compromising the sustainability of vital ecosystems under future climate change scenarios. The Water Act will also provide a framework for the institutionalization of integrated watershed management, including the establishment of catchment-based water resources management committees and sub-catchment water resource users associations.

The country-driven project has been developed using a participatory and consultative process, enabling all relevant stakeholders to contribute to the design of interventions, ensuring buy-in at all levels. This will ensure that the project builds on the social capital within communities, the social protection system provided through the project, as well as the EDA interventions that will be installed, providing tangible benefits to communities linking the project outputs with their needs.

### Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: N/A

In Haiti a SDRF and an ACD (LH) by cascading and corresponding climate hazards (floods, hurricanes, tropical storms, eruptions, etc.) The target beneficiaries endure double the national average poverty rate. The watershed is particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change-induced disasters. In addition to the direct effects on crops and livestock in the catchment area and watersheds such flooding causes severe damage to crops and farmland, as well as agricultural infrastructure. This results in a significant reduction in food production for both human and livestock livelihood, resulting in an increased risk of loss of life and livestock. Addressing climate change-induced disasters through flood-prone vulnerable communities in the region requires the adoption of an integrated approach to flood management that responds to the impacts of climate change.

At present, the Government of Haiti does not have the capacity to fully finance integrated climate-resilient flood management interventions through domestic finance in both


---
**Page 119**
---

The private and public sector: Without GCF support, the Government of Haiti will continue to operate under the existing paradigm of reactive recovery from the impacts of climate change.

### 3.5 Country ownership

Scale: N/A

- The project is a direct contribution to Haiti's adaptation targets in relation to watershed management, land use protection, and reforestation, as reflected in the proposed nationally determined contributions submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in response to its climate change capacity identified in Haiti’s national adaptation plan submitted in early 2023. The project is also a priority intervention with GCF and is included in the advanced draft country programme which, at the time of writing, was under review by the Board. A revised version of the project document was issued and cleared in April 2022.

- Extensive stakeholder consultations were held during the project elaboration and feedback was integrated into the development of the proposal. Active stakeholder engagement, notably in the intervention areas, is planned to ensure optimal execution of the project and as part of the adaptive management strategy of the project.


### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: N/A

- The funding proposal package presents economic and financial analyses with reasonable assumptions for the project activities and their expected impacts on the economy.

- The economic analysis contains estimations of all the relevant economic values from the project activities, including the costs and benefits associated with each activity. For example, Haiti used a social discount rate of 10 per cent in line with the all other UNDP projects. In summary, the economic analysis shows that the project is economically viable. The total cost is USD 243 million with an IRR of 6 per cent and 43 per cent depending on the different flood reduction scenarios. The model shows that the proposed project is economically viable.


- Financial adequacy and appropriate concessionality are assessed to be high.

- The accredited entity (AE) has conducted a sensitivity analysis for the agroforestry model calculated in four cases: (1) when activities are fully financed by farmers on their own; (2) when activities are financed with bank loans at the highest interest rates (30 per cent); (3) when activities are financed with bank loans at the lowest interest rates (2.75 per cent); and (4) when activities are financed with a mix of loans and grants at an average interest rate 10 per cent. The analysis shows that the agroforestry activities cannot be financed with full bank loans (negative cash flow). Likewise, they can only be financed with a mix of loans and grants scenario.


- The need for grants in this project is also justified by the public nature of the project activities and the lack of access to credit or other financial instruments. Lack of access for farmers as one of the major barriers. Alternative sources of funding and credit are not available or are difficult to access, leading farmers to go for the option of short-term relief


---
**Page 120**

activities, such as the production of charcoal at unsustainable rates to meet pressing short-term financial needs.

iii. The project’s adaptation cost is USD 78/16 direct beneficiary and USD 31/5 total beneficiary.
Cost per direct beneficiary is in line with the portfolio average for Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) projects, which is USD 204/16. This is consistent with the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2020–2023. The proposal does not present a clear roadmap or prospect about the potential for leveraging or mobilizing additional financial resources.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards
##### Project background.
The project aims to take a sustainable land and water resource management (LWRM) approach as adaptive measures to build resilience in the Tris-Rivieres (TR) watershed. The project will be implemented through three components: i) implementing green infrastructure (agroforestry and restoration activities) to strengthen flood resilience, supported by capacity-building programmes for land-use management; ii) establishing a water governance framework that will improve local and national food security in the watershed; and iii) improving national and local governance frameworks for the implementation of LWRM. The project will also contribute to increasing the level of sub-catchment levels to contribute to creating an enabling environment for climate-resilient flood management, including through the development of a water resources management plan and planning mechanisms to be developed in seven common catchments targeted by the project in the TR watershed. These actions are expected to reduce flooding in the communities along the Rio Morne. The project will improve climate resilience and food security, decrease the impact of floods, and provide significant environmental and social co-benefits through ecosystem-based flood management.

##### Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments.
The AE has classified this project as a Category B project based on its screening criteria. The AE considers the project as equivalent to Category B per GCF’s E&S risk categorization. The project is expected to have potential limited adverse environmental and social risks, which can be mitigated with appropriate environmental and social management measures. Any high-risk activities will be excluded from the project scope through a detailed environmental and social risk management plan, and exchanging high-risk grey infrastructure for flood control. The Secretariat confirms that the project meets all of GCF’s E&S safeguard instruments as defined in the E&S risk accreditation level of the AE. The AE has prepared a screening report against its own social and environmental risk assessment and management framework (SERSM) to guide the E&S screening, assessment and management to be conducted during the project implementation phase, where the preparation of a strategic environmental assessment (SEA) and environmental and social management plans (ESMPs) will be required.

##### General description.
The paragraphs below describe the programme’s compliance with GCF’s E&S standards.

#### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks
and impacts.
ESS 1.1: Description of project. The AE has described the baseline socio-economic and environmental conditions of the project, and an overview of the laws and regulations that apply to the project area. The AE has also provided a description of the ESMP identified and briefly describes a total of 18 potential social and environmental risks associated with this project. Among these, three specific risks have been related to the


---
**Page 121**
---

project’s potential negative impacts on economic displacement, critical habitats and KBA, and social concerns. The rest are considered moderate risks.

### ii. In response to the security concerns, the ESMS includes a conflict and fragility assessment and identifies that the project will unlikely increase or exacerbate the national-level political or socio-economic instability in the project area. However, it is noted as an important destabilizing factor that makes the project’s operating context more prone to conflict and increases its risk on conflict sensitivity. The ESMS also includes a community engagement plan (CEP) to address existing factors of conflict in certain project sites where social tensions around land tenure have been observed. The CEP will include measures to reduce the likelihood of increased social tension or conflict because such tensions are not to be related to inter-community grievances or to issues of identity, ethnicity, or religion. As the project will promote an inclusive community approach and provide training on gender equality and women’s empowerment, it is expected that the social tensions by working with stakeholders to ensure that all land and water users in the project context have access to resources and services that meet their livelihoods and cultural own needs.

### iii. The ESMS will adopt a process-based approach to conflict sensitivity. This will begin with a detailed conflict analysis to be conducted at each project site as part of the site-specific ESIA. The ESMS provides a methodological guide for conducting a conflict and development analysis (CDA) that includes an assessment of existing social tensions, potential risks to human rights and conflict-related risks specific to the project site. These management measures identified in the CDA will be integrated into the project’s community engagement plan (CEP). The CDA will also provide recommendations to tailor the delivery of the project components to address any identified risks and ensure that they are not exacerbated by the project. The project will also take an environmental peacebuilding approach by bringing communities together with a common objective on natural resources management and livelihoods, which will enable them to work collaboratively towards shared goals and reduce social tensions for designing community land use plans. In addition, the AWE will develop a security plan, including measures to address any identified risks in the CDA. The ESMS will also identify the relevant protocols and structures to manage the identified risk in alignment with UN policy for work in conflict-affected areas. These will be integrated into the project-specific ESIA and ESMPs and will be updated as needed.

#### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions. The AWE has triggered a standard on this as the project activities are likely to involve child labour, which could potentially expose children to disrupted physical, mental, moral, and social development. Child labour is not permitted in any of the project activities that will be supported by the project on agriculture and agroforestry supply chain, land restoration, and erosion control. The ESMS includes an indicative outline for labour management procedures set out in accordance with international labour standards or safety standards. The Project will avoid hiring workers below the age of 18 and will not include activities or production of activities that involve child labour despite the fact that some project activities may be carried out by adults who are potentially exposed to occupational health and safety hazards. The project will exclude activities involving exposure to hazardous substances, such as pesticides, herbicides, and other agroforestry materials. Furthermore, the ESMS includes an indicative outline for labour management procedures set out in accordance with international labour standards or safety standards. These procedures set out the conditions in which project workers will be employed, engaged, and managed, and include measures to ensure that they are not exposed to occupational health and safety hazards and regulations. The procedures will be appropriate to the site, locations, and workforce of project activities.

#### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. The ESMS identifies that to a limited extent the project activity interventions on agroforestry and reforestation may generate waste and litter, such as human waste, food waste, plastic, solid material, and scrap


---
**Page 122**
---

### ESS 5: Community health, safety and security.

The AIF has broadly identified that the project could have significant impacts on the health and safety of affected communities. This considered the health impacts related to the flood risk and associated threat to the communities as well as the impact of water management activities (e.g., construction of new infrastructure). As each project site is yet to be identified, the ESME requires further assessment to be conducted as part of the project’s environmental baseline study. The AIF has also noted that, in addition, as part of the project's wider efforts of implementing regular monitoring and evaluation of water management activities (e.g., construction of new infrastructure), it will be necessary for activity 3.3 to support implementation of IWBM plans). As is likely that some of these new infrastructure projects may create noise, dust, or traffic risks for communities, and that if water supply systems are not designed properly, there could also be a risk of contamination.

The AIF is suggested to provide basic guidance on and include a list of standard mitigation measures for the above-mentioned wide-scale activities as well as those listed in activity 3.3 supporting project implementation.


### ESS 5: Land acquisition and resettlement.

The AIF has triggered its ES on displacement and resettlement, noting that the project's agroforestry or reforestation activities (under Activity 1.2, establishing agroforestry systems areas over 17,740 hectares and restoring forested land) could potentially lead to the acquisition of private lands for these purposes as well as on lands previously used by local communities and/or their livelihoods. This could then create displacement or resettlement risks in the context of the project implementation. The AIF also notes that some activities will require any physical displacements of people or changes of land tenure. The ESME addresses this issue, noting that there is a risk of displacement and resettlement as well as other issues to be addressed during the project implementation, such as those activities that will involve developing agricultural tracks, developing water infrastructure, and providing upgrades to water infrastructure. These activities could potentially lead to the acquisition of private lands for flood-induced flooding. The AIF also notes that in such case the project will only work with those who are willing to sell their land or provide access to their land, which is not always the case as public lands. The project will ensure that all legislation is followed. The ESME requires the AIF to develop a resettlement plan and to engage with communities on economic displacement, namely where the livelihoods of individuals or communities are restricted by the project implementation. This could include providing alternative land resources to support their economic well-being. To be developed as part of the ESME, the LWP will address beneficiary participation in economic displacement decision-making, adequate (half and fair) compensation for displaced beneficiaries, and the resettlement plan. The AIF is required to ensure that prior to the project implementation and that the livelihoods of poor and marginalized are improved through the provision of alternative land resources or other means to ensure long-term rights consistent with applicable law, and outlive capacity training, and development actions targeting these groups. In addition, in case of displacement due to the agroforestry activities (as part of the ESME), project will be required to justify why the land and/or resources to which access will be restricted, the necessity of economic displacement to achieve the project objectives, and the

# Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure

The EMF outlines the overall requirements for stakeholder engagement to be followed during project implementation. The details of the existing rights and interests of stakeholders in land use and resource management are provided separately as an Addendum, which includes a stakeholder analysis and provides an SEAR if possible at the site-level during project implementation. The SEAR outlines processes for engaging with stakeholders throughout the project lifecycle, including targeted communication methods for different stakeholders. The SEAR also identifies key groups to be engaged in the project, such as local communities, indigenous peoples, and other relevant stakeholder groups in the engagement plan. The stakeholders will be assured access to relevant project information and will have an opportunity to provide input on the potential impacts of the project at various stages, including during the detailed project design and implementation. The project will disclose information on the potential environmental and social risks associated with the project and will engage in a collaborative decision-making process whenever possible. As the project risk has been assessed as substantial, the project will follow the disclosure framework specified in the EMF to ensure that all E&S safeguards are adequately addressed and implemented prior to implementation of activities that may give rise to potential adverse E&S impacts.

### Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and thereafter updated it based on feedback from the project team and lessons learned informed by secondary data and outcomes from the consultations during mission visits by the project Client.

The climate change and disaster risk management legislative frameworks in Haiti do not adequately address the issues of gender equality and the empowerment of women. However, flooding and other natural disasters disproportionately affect women and exacerbate gender inequality. Evidence and impacts were drawn from the 2010 earthquake in Haiti, which caused significant damage to infrastructure and housing, particularly among the poor and there is a significant proportion of women-headed households, so impacts such as loss of income, food insecurity, and lack of access to basic services are more pronounced for women than men. The loss of life due to floods ultimately affects women first and foremost. Gender-based violence is another issue that normally goes unnoticed and unreported, including police violence targeting women who report gender-based violence. In the 2010 earthquake, 54% of all victims were women; the majority were physical in nature (59.7 per cent), followed by sexual violence (29.80 per cent), while rape accounted for only a small proportion (5.30 per cent) and murder was rare (4.58 per cent, respectively).

From this gender assessment, various gender-responsive approaches have been proposed to address the issue of women's empowerment in ensuring that the design and installation of water infrastructure facilities are based on gender assessments and participatory processes. These include strengthening the capacity for women’s participation in water management supporting local municipal authorities and communities to develop their own plans for water resources management. It is also recommended that partnerships with the private sector include a commitment to gender equality and that women receive fair compensation when they contribute to the development of water resources. Other gender recommendations have been made in the areas of agricultural and climate change resilience, reforestation and agroforestry systems, including promoting land ownership for women and providing them with access to credit and other financial institutions as part of their adaptation strategies.

## Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

52. The GEF is requested to provide a grant of USD 22.9 million, which will be invested to address climate change-induced flood risks in the TR watershed and the impacts on population displacement and livelihoods. This includes two components with an estimated cost of USD 8.3 million and USD 0.5 million, respectively. The AE is requesting full grant support for this project. As an LAC and SIDS, Haiti has been experiencing increases in public debt which is placing pressure on the country’s fiscal space.

### Accredited entity/executing entity capacity to execute the current programme (medium risk)

53. UNDP is the AE for the project. UNDP Haiti country office has been working in Haiti since 1972 and has experience implementing water and land use-related climate change projects through policy formulation and strategic planning. The country office has a track record of managing large-scale projects, including this one. UNDP Haiti has had a budget range of between USD 2 million and USD 15 million in recent years.

54. The Ministry of Environment (MoE) is the EE of this project. UNDP has carried out a capacity assessment for MoE to identify areas where technical assistance would reduce risk regarding monitoring and evaluation (M&E), and procurement and financial management. Requests for the strengthening of MoE will provide direct project services by assisting procurement processes, and making payments to the suppliers and responsible parties at the instruction of MoE.

### Programme-specific execution risks (high risk)

55. The AE has identified the weak land tenure and management system as one of the risks that could undermine the sustainability of the project. The feasibility study found that there are many constraints in terms of obtaining land titles, fragmented land holdings and legal provisions limiting the use of land as collateral, and so on. The current mitigation measure


---
**Page 127**
---

suggests that the AE is through the community buy-in and implementation of the Water Act. In case an agreement is not reached, the project will work with other communities and participants nearby who are willing to participate in the project activities. The non-participants may continue to use their land as they do now under the existing land tenure and governance mechanism. Therefore, the AE/EG's ability to engage communities and establish the participatory community land-use plans will be key to dealing with the land tenure issue.

The financial sustainability of the project is achieved through the Village Saving and Credit Association (VSCA) and the budgetary contribution from the Government of Haiti. The VSCA component in the project is fully financed by co-financing, which is subject to the completion of the integrated approach to climate-resilient food management in component 1. The Government of Haiti has committed to provide a budgetary contribution for the VSCA and VCA is expected to increase access to finance for smallerholder farmers. Given the budgetary constraints of the Government of Haiti, the financial sustainability is uncertain and there is no co-financing provided by the Government. As a soft measure, it is anticipated that the project will work with the Ministry of Agriculture, Water resources departments and communities in terms of implementing the land and water resource management component.

The AE carried out an economic analysis that identified an economic IRR of 41 per cent and 43 per cent, depending on the choice of flood reduction scenarios, over a 25 years. The social benefits include increased food security (food availability), improved health benefits, reduction of infrastructure damage, and increase in the livelihood income. With regard to the financial feasibility analysis, it was conducted from the project's and individual farmer's perspective for the agroforestry activity (see paragraph 28 above). The analysis shows that the agroforestry activity cannot be financed with loans and farmers will need to invest their own money or borrow from other sources. Therefore, the full grant support is justified. As noted above, the project viability will also depend on the capacity of the implementing partners to manage the financial resources of the project, and the analysis assumed that there would be no additional operation and management costs during implementation.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The UNDP, as the AE, confirmed that no individual or entity that is listed on any United Nations Security Council sanctions list, including the United Nations Consolidated List, will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer or beneficiary.

UNDP confirmed that the risks for the project have been assessed and are mitigated in accordance with UNDP's Operational Policies and Procedures (OPPs). The specific risks linked to engagement with implementing partners and responsible parties, where specific capacity assessments have been performed and mitigation measures are put in place. These measures include the following:

- UNDP confirmed that it conducts partner capacity assessments as part of project preparation and implementation. As a part of the project preparation phase, including the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) and its fraud and corruption practices (FCP) policy included within the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures (OPPs).

- In particular, as part of the project preparation phase, the Project Partner Capacity Assessment Tool included a question regarding the history of fraud, corruption, money laundering, financing terrorism and the like. For both partners, the assessment did not find


---
**Page 128**
---

evidence for such past practices, in the case of Heifer, the assessment identified specific policies for the prevention of these practices:
* The UNDP Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers (HACT) assessments for both partners also evaluated the existence of policies against fraud and corruption, policies for reporting and monitoring the use of donor funds, and policies on procurement and capacity for reporting and monitoring the use of donor and partner funds.
* Heifer was evaluated to have an overall low risk in all those factors and a moderate risk in the procurement factor. The evaluation is based on the information available currently being computed against the AGROSECO system (https://agrosec.heifer.org). However, the evaluation does not consider the specific policies of Heifer as it is not yet fully comparable, as it follows specific written policies and procedures for procurement. The evaluation highlights that Heifer has a very strong policy base that is up-to-date annually by Heifer and where background checks are done systematically.
* The MoI was evaluated with a moderate risk score in the area of governance and leadership, which includes the existence of policies on programme management, accountability and coordination. Its total overall risk rating was assessed as moderate to high. The assessment highlighted that there is no specific policy against corruption or bribery however; MoI did confirm that it is accountable under all public decrees and laws for governance and public service delivery in line with the Law on Public Service.
* In response to the HACT evaluation, the MoI has developed its Manual of Financial Accounting and Accounting Procedures in the last year, which includes a specific mention of anti-fraud measures.
* UNDP confirmed that the Government of Haiti has put in place a set of institutions to fight corruption. These include:
    * Central Unit of Financial Information, which provides information on the laundering of assets, money-laundering and other serious crimes
    * Unit for the Fight against Corruption, which has the mission of working to combat corruption and its manifestations in all forms within the public administration;
    * National Commission for Public Contracts
    * National Commission for the Fight against Drugs; and
    * Superior Court of Accounts and Administrative Litigation, which is a financial and administrative court that reviews the accounts of the State, the territorial collectives, the administration and public officials.
* UNDP informed the Secretariat that the HACT evaluation did identify challenges in the MoI procurement process. The MoI has already taken steps to address these issues by supporting the development of a new Procurement Policy. The MoI also demonstrated a positive history of working with partners on policy implementation. With this in mind, and at the request of the Government of Haiti, the project will work under support in the form of a national implementation modality in which UNDP will provide project

support services for procurement as well as in the payment of vendors on behalf of the MoF (as per the agreement prepared by the MoF and approved by the project board).

Such capacity assessments must be completed before any contract is signed. Any contracts determined to be contracted by or partner with UNDP against the United Nations Security Council Consolidated List (CPL) will be subject to a due diligence process.

* On the basis of the HACT assessment of the MoF, UNDP will process payments to vendors and third parties through the “direct payment module”. Payments will be made on the basis of invoices submitted by the vendor or third party and verified by the MoF and approved by the project board. The project will also work with Heifer as a responsible party for negotiable instruments (as per the agreement prepared by Heifer and approved by the MoF, signed from Heifer).
