### 4.4 Fiduciary

The UNDP will be the AE of the project. It will provide oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the accreditation matrix regarding fiduciary capacity.

The MoE will be the EE for the project (also known as "implementing partner" in UNDP terminology). The MoE will be accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including M&E of project results. The MoE will also be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with GCF rules and regulations.

It will implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, and its POPP guidelines. It will ensure that all project activities comply with relevant requirements on fiduciary, procurement, ESS and other performance standards. A sub-agreement will be signed between the MoE and UNDP as per article 3 of the UNDP Standard Basic Assistance Agreement, and all references in that agreement to "executing agency" shall be read as referring to the MoE.

At the request of the Government of Haiti, UNDP will provide direct project services for this project, including procurement of goods and services through UNDP processes, providing direct payments to service providers, and provision of technical assistance and/or training services. Under these terms UNDP will only make payments and provide related services on behalf of the MoE, which will be responsible for the preparation of terms of references and selection criteria.

UNDP will also provide implementation support in the delivery of activities included within the project scope as per the POPP guidelines. A standard letter of agreement will be signed between UNDP and the GCF and upon indication by the MoE, a responsible party agreement will be signed between UNDP and Heifer International Haiti for the provision of services for these outputs.

Heifer International Haiti will act as a responsible party for output 1. It will execute all activities included within this output in accordance with the POPP guidelines. A standard letter of agreement (the funded activity agreement) signed between the GCF and UNDP, as well as a responsible party agreement between UNDP and Heifer International Haiti, will be executed whereby transfers will be made directly from UNDP to the goods and service providers based on annual workplans submitted by the MoE. Heifer's co-financing resources, which are


---
**Page 132**
---

conditional on the approval of this project by the GCF, will be used for implementing the project interventions outlined in output 2.

### Project Board

a. A project board will ensure that activities under all three outputs of the project comply with the strategies, principles and overall approach outlined in this proposal. The project board will be comprised of representatives from the executing agency, its parent organization, recommending to UNDP and EE/Implementing partner approval of project plans, substantive revisions, and final project reports.

b. To ensure UNDP’s ultimate accountability, decisions taken by the project board will be made in accordance with standards that shall ensure management for development results, the best value for money, and the highest level of integrity and transparency possible. In the case where a consensus cannot be reached, the final decision shall rest with UNDP. Project Board meetings may be held as frequently as required or at any time when raised by the project manager or one of the members of the project board.

c. The project manager will provide strategic orientation to the proposed project and ensure that all activities are in line with the approved project plan, including the GCF providing training to the unit’s staff. Specifically, the project manager will undertake day-to-day management of the project and will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented as per the approved project plan. The project manager will be primarily responsible for guiding the implementation of adaptation interventions in Haiti and will ensure that all activities are aligned with the project proposal and ensuring that these interventions are implemented to the required standards, within the specified time periods and according to the project budget.

d. The overall financial management of this project will be guided by UNDP’s financial rules and regulations. The project will be implemented following the national implementing agency’s internal procedures. The project board will ensure that UNDP will ascertain the national capacities of the implementing partner by undertaking an evaluation of capacity following its Framework for Cash Transfers to Implementing Partners (part of HACT). All projects must comply with UNDP’s policies and guidelines on procurement, travel, and applicable audit guidelines and policies.

e. UNDP will be responsible for ensuring quality assurance in accordance with its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the AMA and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF. This may include, but is not limited to, monitoring missions, spot checks, facilitation of training workshops, review of project documents, conducting internal implementation reviews, and audits at project level or at implementing partner level on the resources provided by UNDP.

f. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA. According to the current GCF audit policy, an external audit is required for all projects that are implemented during the project cycle as per UNDP assurance/unit plan goals, based on the implementation of the project. The audit will be conducted at least once a year or more frequently if necessary by an independent auditor appointed by UNDP to determine whether the funds transferred to the implementing partner were used for the appropriate purposes and in accordance with the workplan. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit and/or an internal control audit.

### Results monitoring and reporting

#### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

a. As an adaptation initiative, the project aims at generating various adaptation results across three GCF result areas. It is expected that 292,600 direct beneficiaries and 411,272 indirect beneficiaries will receive adaptation benefits in Haiti, representing 3 per cent and 4 per cent of the total population respectively. The project will also aim at generating a range of numbers of direct and indirect adaptation beneficiaries based on expected adaptation benefits.


---
**Page 133**
---

The Secretariat provided further guidance of how to disaggregate the beneficiaries against GCF results areas, which can feed into the target setting of GCF core indicator 2 and its supplementary indicators in the logical framework.

### The theory of change diagram adequately captures various levels of expected changes and logically links them with corresponding statements to the proposed activities. There was an issue on the description of outcomes; however, it was discussed well with the AE and it was agreed that the project will contribute to (1) improved resilience of communities in ecosystems, (2) improvements to the livelihoods of beneficiaries and (3) strengthened governance and institutional capacity for sustainable development and enhanced carbon sequestration. The barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes are identified clearly and linked to pertinent activities.

### The project has been aligned with the GCF integrated results management framework. It shows how the project can contribute to three GCF adaptation results areas: (1) improved resilience of communities in ecosystems, (2) improvements to the livelihoods of beneficiaries and (3) strengthened governance and institutional capacity for sustainable development and enhanced carbon sequestration. The project-specific indicators have been chosen for monitoring and reporting the key performance of the project.

The project-specific indicators also seem to be adequate to measure the progress of project implementation against the GCF integrated results management framework.

### There have been discussions on the project M&E plan (annex 11) and project implementation plan (annex 9). The AE has confirmed that it is aware that all MidE-related budgets, including project-specific indicators, are identified in the M&E plan and these should be identical to the M&E budget in the project budget plan. The AE has been responsive and annexes 11 and 9 have been revised accordingly.

### Legal assessment

#### 4.6 Legal assessment

The environmental agreement was signed with the AE on 3 August 2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016. The AE’s first term of accreditation expired on 22 November 2021. The AE re-accredited by the Board pursuant to decision B.30/05. The first amendment and restatement of the environmental agreement (EAA) was signed with the AE on 16 March 2022 and it became effective on 3 May 2022.

The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all integral agreements required for the implementation of the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Haiti, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected from civil or criminal jurisdiction in Haiti unless such jurisdiction is further assessed. The GCF provided an updated draft agreement on privileges and immunities with a background note for the Board’s consideration.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigate any alleged breaches of the GCF rules in Haiti, unless such jurisdiction is further assessed. Privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after a legal assessment has been completed on whether GCF can provide GCF expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP208

## Proposal name:
Enhanced climate resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through Integrated Flood Management

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

## Country/[ies]:
Haiti

## Project/programme size:
Small

### I. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

This funding proposal for a small-sized public sector adaptation project in environmental management and climate change mitigation, covering a 5-year period and with benefits accruing over a 25-year project lifespan, is submitted by the Government of Haiti (GoH) through the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) as accredited entity (AE), with the financial support of the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The total value of the funding proposal (FP) is USD 31.29 million, of which USD 22.43 million is requested from GEF with co-financing from Haiti and USD 8.86 million is requested from UNDP.

The proposed project will contribute to GEF's paradigm shift objective of increased climate-resilient sustainable development by enhancing the resilience of Haiti’s Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed, which covers an area of approximately 250 km² in the northern part of the country. This shift will be achieved by implementing a transformative approach to the way in which the Gulf and former lake basin is managed, through the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA), and integrated land and water resource planning and management that is informed by climate change specifically tailored for this region.

The project will be implemented with co-financing from Heifer for use under:

- **Output 1:** Economic-based flood management solutions implemented in 25,440 hectares of the Trois-Rivières watershed. Activities include: (i) rehabilitation and restoration of degraded ecosystems; (ii) reforestation of degraded landscapes at priority intervention sites; (iii) establishment of agroforestry systems with local government and communities, and participatory land use planning.

- **Output 2:** Climate-resilient agricultural practices, optimized value chains and social safety nets established to promote sustainable land management and reduce digital divide. Activities include: (i) strengthening the institutional capacity for productive climate-resilient land management at the national and local levels; (ii) establishing credit associations, agri-processing cooperatives and village savings and credit associations (VSCAs).

- **Output 3:** Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated water resources management. Activities include: (i) strengthening the required governance framework to support the climate-resilient land and water management; (ii) amending the Water Act, and establishing of catchment committees and sub-catchment committees.

The third component involves capacity-building at the national level and at catchment level throughout the country, and the other two components will focus only on the TR

# Impact potential

## Climate change basics: The FP highlights that Haiti’s extreme vulnerability to climate change impacts, such as earthquakes (which are generally considered natural disasters). In the past two decades, Haiti has experienced 10 hurricanes, 12 tropical storms and hurricanes, several tornadoes, and numerous other tropical storm and earthquake events with severe damage and loss caused by climate change intensified flooding in particular. For example, in 2004, Hurricane Jeanne caused seven deaths and approximately USD 35 million in damages; in 2010, Hurricane Tomas caused approximately USD 41 million in damages. More recently, Hurricane Matthew struck Haiti in 2016, causing at least 78 deaths and approximately USD 90 million in damages across several areas.

## The FP and feasibility study (FS) use historical data and future projections to demonstrate convincingly that climate change is causing an increase in annual average temperature of about 0.5°C per decade since the mid-1960s, with more pronounced increases in the Caribbean region. Climate change is causing a decrease in total annual rainfall in Haiti, but also a higher frequency of heavy precipitation events and an increase in the intensity of rainfall events. These changing climatic conditions are contributing to an increase in the frequency and severity of flooding in many areas, including the north-western TR watershed of the Massif du Nord, with its steep topography.

## The FP also shows how climate change impacts are compounded by direct human pressures on the environment, such as deforestation, overgrazing, poor agricultural practices, and historical deforestation, that involved fellling of 99 per cent of the primary forest cover in Haiti. These factors have led to a significant loss of soil fertility, increased erosion in degraded areas, increased surface run-off, worsened by decreased infiltration of water into soil because of reduced vegetation cover. These factors, combined with steep slopes and the high rainfall intensity associated with climate change, have resulted in more frequent landslides, causing flooding of downstream plains. Increased water flow intensifies erosion and causes sedimentation in rivers and streams, which can lead to increased vulnerability to flooding, including in the downstream city of Port de Paix. Drinking water contaminated with sewage and other pollutants is a major public health concern that contributes to the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases.

## Adaptation impact: The project aims to deliver impacts against three GCF adaptation results for the targeted communes of the TR watershed:
(a) Increased resilience of most vulnerable people and communities;
(b) Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security; and
(c) Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services.

7 Although 99 per cent of Haiti’s original forest cover and its high biodiversity was lost, significant regreening has occurred in recent years thanks to the efforts of local communities. The most important species used as fuelwood and land use change are eucalyptus (Eucalyptus globulus) and casuarina (Casuarina equisetifolia), with up to 30 per cent of the country’s area now reforested, mostly in areas harvested for timber.


---
**Page 137**
---

6. Overall, the project will confer direct and indirect adaptation benefits to approximately 292,600 and 441,722 vulnerable people in Haiti; respectively by implementing 17,740 ha of agroforestry systems and rehabilitating 7,500 ha of forests in priority areas in the TR watershed to address water security. The project interventions will reduce peak surface run-off and floodplain extents, resulting in a 35 per cent reduction in the number of households affected by flooding and a 28 per cent reduction in the number of households affected by 100-year floods. This will be achieved through revegetation which restores the ecosystems’ capacity for water infiltration and reduces surface run-off. The project interventions will also reduce sedimentation, thereby silting of drainage structures; and, although this is less emphasised in the TR, it) improves groundwater recharge and availability, ii) enhances soil fertility and iii) increases carbon sequestration. This will enhance 15,000 vulnerable households’ resilience and improve food security, by diversifying and increasing agricultural production and improving access to water for domestic purposes.

7. Interventions to reduce the extent of flooding are expected to reduce the spread of water- and vector-borne diseases, including: inter alia, cholera, diarrhea and typhoid; with an expert team from the Ministry of Public Health and Population (MSP) providing technical assistance on this aspect. The agroforestry and reforestation interventions under this project are also expected to reduce soil erosion and sedimentation in water bodies.

8. The combined project measures will transform Haiti’s approach to land and water resources management, by promoting the adoption of sustainable land use practices and more sustainable flood management practices in the public sector and in farming communities. The project aims to create pathways to scale up and replicate these practices in catchments across Haiti through activities that promote knowledge sharing and exchange.

9. Adaptation options and impacts: The project proponents conducted an options analysis which compared agroforestry systems, green infrastructure solutions such as ditches, check dams, canals and discharge channels on the one hand; and ‘green’ ecosystems-based solutions, such as tree planting, natural regeneration, slope stabilization, riverbank vegetation, and contour farming on the other. The analysis concluded that agroforestry systems are less costly and require less complex and expensive maintenance, and also because of the wide range of co-benefits they provide.

10. The justification for the selection of an ABE approach is set out in the PS; though it is noted that less technical detail is provided on the green solutions in section 4 in the options analysis. It should be noted however that agroforestry systems are a form of catchment reforestation, in comparison with the technical guidance on agroforestry. In answer to a question about how the project’s actual results will be tracked, the AE highlighted the significant budget allocation (through Heredia and UNDP co-financing) for monitoring and evaluation activities. The AE also noted that if a flood does occur, conducting post-flood community surveys in the project intervention sites; and integrating these findings into the project’s M&E plan would provide an indication of risk reduction.

11. Water that seeps into the stream from the groundwater, as opposed to surface run-off.

# Conflict sensitivity analysis

In response to a query about the potential for project impacts to be limited by the country’s fragile security situation, with lawlessness in and around Port-au-Prince and the surrounding areas, it is noted that most of the project activities are located in relatively secure parts of Haiti, with the partial exception of Grose-Morne and Bouchon. However, the presence of armed groups in these areas is a destabilizing factor that makes the operating context more prone to conflict and increases risks. To mitigate this risk, it was decided at the inception phase of the project that, as part of the project, a proactive approach to conflict sensitivity will be adopted and will be promoted by UNDP. This will begin with a detailed conflict analysis of project sites—as outlined in the section on “Project Sites” above—and will continue throughout implementation.

A conflict assessment will be developed for each commune, as part of the community land-use planning process. The assessment will identify potential areas of conflict and assess how these might interact with the project. This will help to ensure that the project does not exacerbate or worsen existing conflicts, and that it can contribute positively to peacebuilding.

The TIPF appreciates the conflict sensitivity analysis conducted, and the process to continue conflict assessment and mitigation during project implementation. We note that work on the ground in Port-au-Prince is currently being supported by UNDP staff from the capital who will be supported with supplies from the region or airlifted by the United Nations Humanitarian Air Service (UNHAS). This has been a successful model for other projects, including those dealing with violence and mudlocks. Having a project management office in the local directorate of the MoH in Port-au-Prince will also mean that staff do not need to travel from the capital in the south, and that they can be more responsive to the needs of the communities being served. This is true for a number of other rural projects in the MoH-UNDP environment portfolio, which currently has 90% of its projects in this category.

We remain concerned, however, about the ability of the project to carry out the national level work of output 3.1 effectively, strengthening the national governance framework for rights and responsibilities of land users, and establishing a network of community structures in 31 communes across the country. This aspect important for maximizing adaptation impacts is challenging because it requires significant travel by UNDP staff, with travel by MoH officials from the capital restricted. Even without such restrictions, it would be challenging to establish a network of community structures in 31 communes, given the limited capacity development happening in the TR catchment. Under the current security situation, it would seem even more difficult.

Overall, the project’s adaptation impact in reducing flood risk is dependent on the success of the reforestation and agroforestry efforts, as well as the development of the governance framework for land rights and responsibilities. The latter will be challenging to take place in a changing socio-economic and security context. The work on the ground in the TR catchment is currently being carried out with limited potential, while the impact potential of output 3.1 may be low, given the anticipated challenges in implementation. The TIPF assesses the impact potential of the project as medium overall.

## Paradigm shift potential

**Score:** N/A

### 17. **Paradigm shift:** The proposed project intends to catalyze a paradigm shift in Haiti’s approach to managing climate change intensified flooding in the TR watershed and beyond – by initiating a shift away from the present fragmented, uncoordinated decision-making, making use of existing institutional structures (e.g., water resources management) and water resource management that takes into account the current and projected impacts of climate change. The project aims at increasing resilience to climate change through the implementation of reactive recovery from the impacts of climate change intensified disasters, including severe flooding, and failure to prepare for slow-onset events like drought. The project aims to incentivize and support the development of a new integrated water resources management strategy within strategic areas of the TR catchment (C1), while promoting sustainable incomes based on resource-efficient land use practices in other parts of C1 (C2). This will be achieved through rural committees to plan land use and manage catchments effectively for climate risk (C3).

#### The role of MEF in executing the project, and the strong emphasis on governance and capacity building, is critical. The project aims at strengthening existing institutional structures, help to embed the integrated land and water management approach to climate change adaptation into the national development planning process, and ensure that results output 3.1. Although the TAP does not likely challenge that it will be carried out in carrying out its mandate, it is important for MEF to consider how this fits with its security context. The intended scale-up involves amendments to Haiti’s draft Water Act to include, inter alia, provisions for integrated land and water management (ILWM) and the establishment of 31 new catchment IWRM committees, each with sub-catchment resource management plans. MEF will also provide technical assistance to the Government of Haiti to develop and sustain IWRM structures, and its letter to GCF, committing USD 7.5 million for post-project operations and maintenance (DAM), supporting the “maintenance of the cost of the green infrastructure” (C3).

#### **Challenges of reforestation:** The TAP posed a question to the AEF about whether the intended paradigm shift might be challenging, particularly the attempt to restore the TR catchment’s forest cover. In the context of climate change and land degradation, most past reforestation efforts have failed to sustain tree growth, and sub-threshold structures set up to protect trees from grazing or other forms of damage have not been effective. It is acknowledged the depth of the challenges, but pointed to the systematic analysis conducted by UNDP on the causes of failure in Haiti’s previous reforestation efforts, which identified the elements of success and failure, and led the design project accordingly. Based on the services provided by trees, the TAP has proposed a number of technical assistance (TA) elements built into the project design that improve the chances of achieving the desired shift, despite the challenges.

#### **Agriforestry business as an incentive:** Despite decades of support to agroforestry, many farming communities maintain a focus on annual crops, because of the short-term returns and lack of incentives. The TAP proposes to address this by providing additional funding to GCF investment in reforesting 7,700 water towers (USD 5 million), the largest single portion of the GCF investment (USD 11 million) will go to best practice agroforestry supported by a business model that provides incentives for farmers and communities. Technical assistance will focus on maximizing the use of native and exotic tree species with high value timber, fruit, or other products. The trees will be planted in rows to deep shade soil and root enrichment, as well support for beekeeping and small livestock production. This will be achieved by providing technical assistance through enabling commercial agroforestry as a long-term incentive for keeping trees standing – through the provision of inputs such as seeds, tools, and training; access to processing and agri-processing facilities; and access to appropriate micro-loans through VSCAs.

# Social capital approach to community engagement

While many past reforestation efforts have worked hard to engage communities in tree planting and sometimes also in sustainable land management (SLM) activities, these projects have necessarily been a top-down approach, driven primarily by communities' own needs and knowledge. As explained by the AC: "[p]lant projects have mainly been guided by top-down approaches, with little or no involvement of local communities, who have not made them co-participants in the design of interventions that will be made within their territories. This has often led to a lack of community ownership as well as success of the forestry investment themselves."

### Contrast

By contrast, the proposed project has been designed around a social capital approach, which is based on the premise that communities are more likely to build shared cohesion and shared value, as a sound basis for sustaining project interventions. Both reforestation and agroforestry projects will be implemented in areas where there is an existing presence of farming households' own lands, and will be guided by community land-use plans developed with the participation of local communities. This will lead to signed governing reforestation plantations and their long-term security. A diverse range of agroforestry systems will be introduced that are adapted to the socio-economic context as well as traditional usage and preferences. Intensive capacity development will focus on understanding the tangible benefits of the interventions to the communities – in terms

# Written responses to the TFP questions received from UNDP on 17 May 2023.

The AC has drawn upon the work of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in Kenya, which guided the project proponents, outlining a social capital approach to agroforestry.


---
**Page 141**
---

of soil fertility, crop yields, nutrition, water availability, health and sanitation, and slope stability, in addition to the less direct benefit of downstream flood prevention. The principles of the farmer field school approach will be built into the restoration work (as well as other approaches) so that farmers can learn from each other and are supported by locally based agronomists and field technicians, learning surveying and monitoring skills, and how to use the land-use plans. Farmers will also be designated by themselves through the land-use plans as having value through reforestation.

(n) In answer to the ITAP question about how restoration work outside people's own farms but within their communities can be supported, Heifer has developed a community system to mobilize communal labour for tree planting, irrigation and agri-processing, which is not only effective in reducing the environmental impact of large-scale projects impacts but also in enhancing social cohesion and ownership®. This is reinforced by Heifer’s proven ‘Planting of the past (pou koulé) approach’, in which beneficiaries are required to plant trees on their own land as a condition for receiving technical support for replication. This system has been used by Heifer successfully in various type of projects, including reforestation and agri-processing. The key is that it creates a sense of local ownership, community and appropriation and allowing for the implementation of community-based management systems. A recent evaluation of this approach demonstrated that this approach would be welcomed and would be seen as less of a top-down project and more of an opportunity to participate in a process where measures that have proven to be successful include competitions around environmental protection, which are organized by local communities and/or women’s associations that are involved in the protection of seedlings in public areas.

(f) Funding enabling conditions, not running costs: A range of past initiatives in Haiti have been funded on a project-by-project basis, with little or no support for ongoing operations, only to find that these structures have not survived long-term (see useful analysis on this in a May 2011 report at the UNDP Haiti Reforestation Project). The proposed project will be different. It is based on enabling conditions that are already made by governments. This includes Heifer’s support in output 2.1 for existing community-based management systems, and output 3.1 for supporting community land-use plans, and GCF support in outputs 3.1 and 3.2 for a new (but required) by law Trust Fund for the protection of forests. The project will also provide technical support to water resource user associations. Most importantly, the project will not provide for the running costs of these structures. Instead, it will provide enabling conditions that are kind by local government and by community organizations (e.g. producer associations, women’s groups) to ensure that they can operate independently under the necessary enabling conditions for these structures to operate and bring value to communities, such that their continued existence is enabled.

## Food coupon strategy: An interesting element of the project’s theory of change in bringing about a transition to more sustainable and resilient food systems, especially for produce in local markets to 934 highly food-insecure families in the project sites, using Heifer co-financing. The AE notes that this is an innovative approach to addressing food insecurity as a result of drought-related losses, through the selling of charcoal as a livelihood option. It also provides a market for charcoal producers and helps them diversify their income while allowing the space required for the agroindustry interventions to take hold. In response to concerns about sustainability and potential market distortions, the AE explained that these are addressed by the project’s theory of change, which includes a mechanism to work with local food producers, helping to create a market for the new diversified products.

### Excluding charcoal value chains: The ITAP notes that the proposed project provides agroforestry support for a wide range of benefits and income generation opportunities. Tables 3 and 4 in the ITAP provide an overview of these benefits and opportunities, which are designed to generate income and benefits, including food, shade, soil fertility, fodder, medicines, hedgerows and erosion control, among others.

The ITAP notes that inefficient charcoal production and consumption contributes to greenhouse gas emissions and air pollution, and suggests that it is preferable that GCF funds are not utilized for this purpose. The AE noted that the project includes a component on environmental awareness and capacity-building in activity 1.1 including training in the 118 target communities on “the importance of reducing charcoal production and consumption.” This could be seen as a strategy for increasing climate resilience in the watershed, which is welcomed. This could include information on the negative impacts of charcoal production and consumption, to which these can be mitigated through the use of improved low-emission charcoal kilns (for producers) or stoves as well as alternative energy sources (for consumers).

The ITAP notes that inclusion of a covenant in the funding agreement with the AE that the AE shall ensure that GCF Proceeds under Activity 12.2 of the project are not used for producing wood for sale into charcoal value chains. The project’s interventions in this activity should focus

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: N/A

The proposed project will contribute towards the achievement of at least 5 of the 17 United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 2 – Zero hunger; SDG 5 - Gender equality; SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 11 - Sustainable cities and communities. The project is also expected to contribute towards achieving several environmental, social, economic and gender-responsive co-benefits, as described below.

### Environmental co-benefits: 
The restoration of degraded forests through targeted reforestation and agroforestry activities will lead to the implementation of IWRM practices, which will generate several environmental co-benefits. Reduced surface run-off and soil erosion will result in improved water quality and increased groundwater recharge and storage capacity of the soil. Reduced erosion and flooding will also mean less contamination of food and water by heavy chemicals and/or pesticides transported by flooding. Water quality will improve as a result of reduced sedimentation, which will lead to better fish production.

### Social co-benefits: 
Implementing targeted landscape restoration and IWRM, as well as enhancing the technical capacity of smallholder farmers and communities in The TW watershed to utilize agroforestry practices, will contribute towards improved livelihoods, gender empowerment, and greater energy security, as well as health benefits. Training communities and individuals on sustainable land management techniques, combined with increased access to agricultural markets and credit facilities will improve household nutrition and increase income.

### Economic co-benefits: 
Food management interventions are expected to reduce peak surface runoff in the TW watershed, which will lead to reduced flooding, reducing economic damages to existing infrastructure, generating public savings and indirectly increasing the fiscal space available for other development priorities. A wide range of livelihood opportunities will be created through the project interventions in agroforestry and reforestation, such as the establishment of nurseries, tree planting activities, and income-generating activities. Farmers will participate to interrupting of cash crops, timber trees as well as a variety of food crops and tree fruits will provide them with an important economic safety net to reduce the economic impacts of climate change. The project interventions are expected to increase their resilience by expanding agricultural markets, which will increase the resilience of the agricultural


---
**Page 144**
---

sector to natural climate change shocks, such as flood and hurricane impacts. Increasing access to agricultural finance through VSCAs will allow beneficiaries to purchase necessary agricultural inputs and improve their yields.

### Gender co-benefits: Approximately 52 per cent of direct beneficiaries of the proposed project are women, with a high percentage of female-headed rural households (approximately 39 per cent), the majority of whom are smallholder farmers. These women will benefit from increased access to credit for agricultural inputs and improved livelihoods as well as the extension of agricultural finance and provision of climate-resilient agriculture technologies. The project also aims to improve water management in the TR watershed to reduce the rate of deforestation, and in turn reduce the amount of sediment, nutrients and pathogens entering the river system. This will result in a decline in the spread of waterborne diseases, as the contamination of water supplies will be reduced, with a disproportionately positive impact on women, who are most exposed to contaminated water sources.

The ITAP assesses the project's sustainable development potential as high.


## Needs of the recipient

### Vulnerability of Haiti: Ranked 163rd out of 191 countries on the 2021 United Nations Human Development Index. Haiti is both a small island developing state (SIDS) and a least developed country (LDC), and faces multiple development challenges related to food and economic security, poverty, and climate change. The country has one of the highest levels of poverty in endemic, with approximately 59 per cent of the population living below the World Bank poverty line of US$1.90/day. Haiti is also highly vulnerable to natural disasters: the frequency of natural and climate change intensified disasters over the past two decades, including severe storms, droughts, floods, landslides, and earthquakes, has increased significantly since 2010, with the category 4 Hurricane Matthew (which left 806,000 people in need of urgent food assistance) making landfall on October 4th 2016. The country was also hit by two hurricanes in August 2021. On the 2021 Climate Risk Index, Haiti is third among countries that were most affected by extreme weather events from 2000 to 2019.

The project aims to address the vulnerability of Haiti through a cost-effective disaster response and rebuilding that is preventing the adoption and implementation of sustained climate-resilient approaches. The country's food security situation is further exacerbated by the country's fiscal challenges and dependency on neighbouring countries for importing critical supplies. For example, approximately 50 per cent of the national budget comes from foreign aid, with agriculture being the primary economic activity (40 per cent of the population is dependent on the agricultural sector). The project will support the development of a climate-resilient agriculture sector.

The FP quotes a statistic from the World Food Programme that 35 per cent of the population faced food insecurity in Haiti at the end of 2019. This figure has increased to 48 per cent due to the already alarming situation worsened in 2020 due to global conflict and the resulting economic and supply chain disruptions, as well as the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic and the political power of Haitians, to the point that an estimated 4.5 million people are currently in need of food assistance*. The project addresses these challenges by employing cost-effective EBA and WRM


---
**Page 145**
---

### Vulnerability of the Trois Rivières watershed: Poverty is prevalent in the TR watershed, including parts of the Arthabaska, North and Northeastern departments. The northwest part of the watershed has a population density exceeding 100 per km² with an extreme poverty rate exceeding 40 per cent, and including the city of Port-de-Paix, which is the third most populous municipality in Haiti.

### Current socio-political and economic situation: The FP figures 2017 quotes on Haiti's high level of public debt. The ITAP notes that the country's public debt has nearly doubled since then and continues to rise rapidly. The government has been unable to meet its obligations, and with rising rates from zero after the 2010 earthquake saw the cancellation of all outstanding loans, the interest rate on new loans is now at 35 per cent. With projections for it to rise rapidly in the next decade, the country faces a high risk of external debt distress.

### Institutional capacity needs: Facing myriad environmental, climate change, security and development challenges, Haiti has limited institutional capacity to effectively implement rural development policies that improve the quality of life of its citizens, while more effectively managing natural resources. The MoF is responsible for overseeing the implementation by a number of government entities, including the Ministry of Agriculture, Natural Resources and Rural Development (MARNRD), the National Water Office (ONH), and the Environment Ministry.

### The MoF has committed USD 7.5 million in kind to cover the O&M costs of the restoration investments, on-site conservation of genetic resources, and the continued work of the watershed and sub-watershed committees.

## 1. Country ownership

### 30. Policy alignment: The proposed project is strongly aligned with Haiti’s national priorities as outlined in the National Agricultural Programme of action (NAPA) of 2017, focused on soils, agriculture, coastal zones and water resources. The NAPA itself was designed to align with the country's earlier national agricultural strategy plan, Strategic Development Plan for Agriculture (SDPA), which aimed at poverty reduction and food security, including their goals to ramp up agricultural production in a sustainable way, preventing land degradation and improving soil fertility.

The proposed project aims to build on recent results of the national agriculture plan (NAP) process, supported by UNDP’s previous projects. The project is also aligned with the SDPA, which seeks to strengthen institutional and technical capacities and existing frameworks and systems. In particular, the project will contribute to the achievement of the following strategic development priorities: a) evidence base for adaptation planning and that priorities are sub-sequentially integrated into the strategic development plan and disaster risk reduction plan; and d) strategy.

### 31. The proposed project is also aligned with the revised national land use and land cover (NULUC) and land use and land cover restoration activities of 137,500 ha land. The ITAP notes that the new NULUC includes its non-exhaustive list of core priority actions the “Afforestation” and “Reforestation” plans. Haiti’s recent participation in the UNFCCC on climate change to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change highlighted the importance of addressing climate change impacts, including land degradation and desertification and the need to: i) implement climate-resilient solutions that will address the impacts of climate change; ii) promote sustainable land management practices (e.g., agroforestry, silvopastoral agriculture); iii) promote the adoption of an integrated approach to land and water resource management in order to improve productivity and sustainability of agricultural systems. Planned initiatives focused on landscape and watershed management, informed by best practices generated from the IWRM experience, are also consistent with this national strategy region. It is noted that the project’s IWRM investments build well on this approach, as well as the framework first put out in the 1999 Action Plan for Water Resources Management in Haiti.

### 32. Since inception of the project, UNDP has been fully involved in the development of this project since inception, through its contribution to the formulation process and inputs during field missions. UNDP has facilitated dialogue between the MoE, the MANDIP, and other stakeholders on land use planning and national agricultural prioritization priorities of the country. Following technical assessments of flooding risk, stakeholder consultations were held with the Ministry of Environment and Energy (MEE), the National Directorate for Water Supply and Sanitation in the

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: N/A

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Of the project’s total proposed budget of USD 31.29 million, 72 per cent of funds are requested from GCF, with 25 per cent in co-finance from Briefer and 2 per cent from UNDP.

Although this is a significant amount of government commitment to financing long-term maintenance of green infrastructure as seen in previous projects, it should be noted that the project’s success will depend on the level of government commitment to financing long-term maintenance of green infrastructure. The TAP agrees with the proposal’s objectives, and the TAP agrees that a 100 per cent grant is the most appropriate level of co-finance for this project given: i) the limited availability of public resources and lack of financial resources; ii) Haiti’s status as a SIDS and LDC; iii) the low level of debt of the country, with its limited capacity to repay loans; and iv) by the fact that the project beneficiaries are poor rural communities.

An economic analysis was conducted, focused on determining costs-benefits associated with the project. The analysis identified two main areas for cost-saving opportunities: i) reducing administrative activities, to enhance the resilience of the TR watershed to climate change-induced flooding; ii) facilitating sustainable catchment management practices with the strengthening of agricultural value chains and the development of a market-based approach to water pricing within the policy framework to facilitate IWBM in Haiti. The economic analysis estimated the net present value of


---
**Page 148**
---

interventions will be US$378 million, with an internal rate of return of 40% per cent,
representing a highly cost-effective spend. Project benefits included in the economic analysis
were estimated on an annual basis, comparing with and without-project scenarios in the
present value (PV) framework. The project is expected to generate significant co-benefits for
rural livelihoods (agroforestry income) and infrastructure benefits (avoided flood damage costs). In addition, the project will contribute to climate change adaptation by building on
what have accrued, has a 'grey' infrastructure approach to flood mitigation chosen over a 'green'
ecosystem-based solution.

The proposed development project is not a climate information/early warning system
project; its efficiency is enhanced by the fact that its design complements ongoing initiatives to
build Haiti's capacity for climate change adaptation. In particular, the project will build on the past decade of support from the Government of Canada to Haiti to retool and modernize the national meteorological and hydrological service, implemented by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) in 2013. The project will also complement ongoing multi-hazard alerts, including for flooding, through: (i) the Climate Risk and Early Warning
Systems (CREWS) project; and (ii) the Least Developed Countries Fund project that will enhance capacity for the delivery of climate information services to vulnerable communities in Haiti. In addition, a national-level institutional government capacity for climate risk-informed DRRM is being built, hydrologists from the
Hydrology Department of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development are using a
hybrid approach in the recreation of the flood modelling that will be undertaken by the project to
gauge its impacts, equipping them to recreate the analysis in other areas.

The project's overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium to high.


### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP believes that this climate change adaptation project from the Government of Haiti supports the country’s national development priorities by addressing climate risks through an integrated design project, and represents an important initiative to build societal and community resilience in a challenging context.

The ITAP also notes that the project is subject to inclusion of the following covenant
in the funded-activity agreement:

(a) The Accredited Entity shall ensure that GCF Proceeds under Activity 12 of the project are not used for processing wood for sale into charcoal value chains.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP208)

## Proposal name:
Enhanced climate-resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through Integrated Flood Management

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Haiti

Small

### Impact potential
We appreciate the detailed analysis and considerations of the ITAPs in regards to the project’s impact assessment, interventions on the ground which have been assessed to have a medium to high impact potential. We also recognize ITAPs concerns regarding the replicability of the project’s governance component arising from the current national context.

To this end, we would like to highlight that the project is designed with the intention that project actions are streamlined within existing national and local structures and create a basis of replication for other projects in the future. The UNDP Country Office (CO) in Haiti has informed UNDP that the current project will be a cornerstone to its own National Adaptation Framework and will serve as a demonstrative experiment for adaptation action. While we acknowledge the concerns raised by ITAPs, we believe that the project aligns with the country’s priorities particularly Activity 3.1, the strong commitment from the Gobt and the above described measures will help deliver the adaptation benefits expected in the project.

### Paradigm shift potential
The level of analysis undertaken by the ITAP in its assessment of the project’s approach is highly appreciated. As has been highlighted in the assessment, the project has built lessons learned into the design of the project and will be a key learning tool for other projects in Haiti and will look to put these in action to enable the project’s theory of change focusing on sustainable integrated water and land management as an adaptation solution to climate induced floods.

We recognize ITAPs concerns in regard to the challenging implementation context. However, as it has been highlighted by the assessment, we believe that there is a potential for paradigm shift as a result of a community oriented approach as well as by integrating a strong sense of national ownership around the project interventions.

### Sustainable development potential
We appreciate and are in full agreement of ITAPs assessment regarding the HIGH sustainable development potential of the project that is in turn related to the project’s ecosystem based approach.


---
**Page 150**
---

Needs of the recipient

We appreciate ITAP’s assessment and are in agreement of the VERY HIGH needs of the project’s recipients that was evidenced during the project's preparation and has been reiterated by the independent Technical Advisory Panel. A further study will be conducted to ensure that the recipient needs are comprehensively assessed.

Country ownership

We appreciate ITAP’s assessment as it pertains to the project’s HIGH degree of country ownership. It is noted that throughout project implementation UNDP will work well with the MoE and other ministries to ensure that national capacities are enhanced as a result of this project.

The MoE is fully engaging with its local structures to already put in place an operational guidebook that will be finalized based on additional consultations as part of the initial baseline study. This guidebook will fix roles and responsibilities of each local counterparts under the MoE leadership to ensure proper monitoring and sustainability of the project activities on the ground.

Efficiency and effectiveness

We appreciate ITAP’s assessment in regards to MEDIUM-HIGH efficiency and effectiveness of project implementation. As has been noted by the project's economic and financial analysis and is highlighted by the TEAP, the project’s internal rate of return is calculated at 40.7% taking into account the multiple benefits that will arise through its ecosystem based adaptation strategy including a reduction of flood losses, improved livelihoods and health.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

ITAP's full endorsement of the project is appreciated as well as its comprehensive analysis of the project’s potential impacts on climate change. The successful implementation of this project will create an important foundation and model for community based resilience in Haiti and look forward to its implementation.

UNDP recognizes ITAP’s concern regarding charcoal value chains and agrees on the inclusion of the suggested covenant.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Objective of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan

This Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) is a supporting document for the design of the following project: Green Climate Fund (GCF) project: Enhanced climate resilience in the Trois-Rivières region of Haiti through integrated flood management. It is the Annex 8 of the Funding Proposal, which is being developed by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and has been developed in close coordination with the Government of Haiti.

The main objective of this Gender Assessment is to screen the gender content which will inform where the GCF funding can be most effectively used. The assessment will also identify opportunities through the project, to ensure that the benefits of the project accrue equitably to women, men, boys and girls in the targeted projects sites selected with country partners and stakeholders in Haiti.

In particular, this Gender Assessment aims at identifying gender equality issues related to climate change adaptation and women empowerment in Haiti, with a specific focus on the gender differentiated impacts of climate change and how they intersect with existing inequalities. The assessment will also identify gender responsive and (where possible) gender transformative actions. The objectives of the GAAP are as follows:

* To align the proposed project design with Haiti’s national priorities on gender as well as GCF’s Gender Policy and Action Plan and UNDP’s Gender Strategy 2012 – 2025.
* To incorporate lessons learned from the previous gender studies and evaluations in Haiti to both understand the context of the country, and what has worked and what has not worked with respect to gender and tailored
* To present project-related results from stakeholder consultations and surveys involving women, girls and boys living in the areas where the project will be implemented by drawing on an early stage in project design.
* To integrate gender considerations into project indicators, goals and activities, and identify ways in which women can act as leaders and decision-makers in community-based adaptation interventions.

## Overview of the Proposed Project

The Government of Haiti (GoH) is requesting US$25 million in GCF grant finance to implement climate-resilient integrated water resources management projects aimed at reducing the impacts of flooding on the Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed. The investment will be supported by a US$10 million co-financing from the GoH, which has committed US$8.3 million in co-financing for the implementation of project interventions.

The proposed project will seek to contribute to GCF’s paradigm shift objective of developing climate-resilient sustainable development by enhancing the resilience of Haiti's Trois-Rivières (TR) watershed to the impacts of climate change-induced flooding.


---
**Page 153**
---

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Haiti is particularly affected by the climate change impacts of increasing frequency and intensity of floods, tropical storms and hurricanes. Between 1975 and 2012, such climate change-related events resulted in damages and losses equivalent to 2% of the country’s GDP on average per year.

In 2004, one storm — Hurricane Jeanne — caused severe flooding in northern Haiti. The flooding caused by this storm resulted in approximately US$41 million in damages, more than 2,800 deaths and the need for emergency shelter for over 350,000 people. This event also destroyed or damaged infrastructure across the country. Since 2012, the impacts of climate change have continued to negatively affect Haiti’s economy. In 2016, Hurricane Matthew caused widespread damages and economic losses and negatively affected more than half of the population in the southern part of the country, including the capital city of Port-au-Prince.

The storm also inflicted economic damages and losses estimated at more than 22% of the country's GDP that year (2016), including the loss of up to 90% of crops and livestock in some areas of the country. In addition, it caused a sharp increase in the number of cholera cases experienced throughout Haiti.

The intense rainfall associated with extreme climate events such as Hurricane Matthew also leads to severe flooding and landslides. Such events have become more frequent over time. There has been a regular occurrence in Haiti in recent decades. During the period 1993–2012, for example, Haiti experienced 35 floods, causing the deaths of approximately 2,870 people and adversely affecting approximately 4 million people.

In addition to these extreme climate events, there have been a total of 60 tropical storms in Haiti since 1993. Of those, 10 were directly attributed to hurricanes and a further 16 attributed to extreme rainfall events.

Implementing a transformative approach by making adaptation investments is crucial to ensure that the GCF project contributes effectively towards reducing vulnerability and exposure for people living within climate impacted communities towards climate-resilience communities. Specifically, the funds will be used to:

- Implement agroforestry systems and rehabilitate "water towers" through reforestation of degraded landscapes at priority intervention sites;
- Enhance technical and institutional capacity for productive climate-resilient land management at the national and local levels; and
- Establish the required governance framework for integrated water resources management (IWRM) to ensure that the project contributes effectively towards improving water systems and facilitate sustainable use and management of water resources over the long term.

The combined effect of project interventions will result in the adoption and implementation of a climate-resilient, integrated approach to food management that can be readily scaled up and replicated. The project will also contribute to building resilience at household level through food resilience and watershed management, and food security as key elements of building resilience to climate change impacts.

The GCF project is aligned with other initiatives in Haiti such as the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) highlight the importance of managing watersheds to combat the extreme climate risks. The Risks Disaster Management National Framework (Phytila), the National Water Policy, and the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Rural Development (MARDIR), focuses on building resilience through participatory planning.

3. Overview of Gender Issues in Haiti

Haiti’s Human Development Index (HDI) value for 2021 is 0.535; which puts the country in the low human development category, as it is 163 out of 191 countries and territories.

The Gender Development Index (GDI)—measures gender gaps in achievements in three basic dimensions of human development: health (measured by female and male life expectancy at birth), knowledge (measured by female and male mean years of schooling for adults aged 25 years and older) and living standards (measured by female and male estimated adult total consumption per capita). The GDI value for Haiti is 0.564 in contrast with 0.564 for males, resulting in a GDI value of 0.898, placing it in Group B.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII)—measures gender inequalities (the loss in human development due to inequality between female and male achievements) in three key dimensions—reproductive health, empowerment, and labor market. Haiti has a GII value of 0.635; ranking it 163 out of 170 countries in 2021.

Empowerment is measured by the shares of parliamentary seats held by women and at least some secondary education attainment for women. Labor market participation is measured by the labor force participation rates for women and men.

Similarly, The Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI)—of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) has a value for Haiti of 0.146 in 2014, suggesting that discrimination against women is medium.

This implies that the country faces consistent challenges in the areas of health and education, equal participation in economic activities, and political empowerment, particularly among women. Persistent inequalities across the social, economic and political spheres of life are critically related to climate change vulnerability and risk. Addressing these gender inequalities will enhance the climate resilience of women and ultimately increase their chances for effective risk prevention and/or mitigation. The assessment and analysis herein focus on and highlight the

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Methodology

This document is based upon available data and information retrieved from a review of literature and key documentation from the Government of Haiti, reports from multi-lateral institutions, and discussions with stakeholders during the project development phase and mission by the project development team in Haiti. It includes insight from national gender experts, agroforestry, and focus group consultations with community members to understand potential impacts on women’s engagement in the design phase to understand potential impacts of the project, and builds on lessons learned from past studies and assessments.

To summarize, the gender analysis and stakeholder participation and consultation enabled the following:

- A strong participative process and accordingly commitment by both local government officials and community members in fulfilling the objectives of the project.
- Demonstration of the need for gender-disaggregated data and indicators to establish a baseline in which to measure improvements and identify areas of interest; and
- Establishment of recommendations to be incorporated into the Gender Action Plan.

Accordingly, this document presents the findings from the gender assessment, including key findings both on the comprehensive gender assessment presented, as well as the results of the consultations. It has been built to fit with the logical framework of the Project, and identifies maintaining actions for the activities planned, with indicative timelines, means of verification and indicators.

## 4. Facets of Gender Inequality in Haiti relevant for the Project:

### 4.1 Climate change

Haiti ranks third globally on the Long-Term Climate Risk Index\*, demonstrating its extreme vulnerability to climate change. In this context women are especially vulnerable. Widespread deforestation and soil degradation have led to severe erosion, landslides, flash flooding, and sedimentation of river channels. The agricultural sector is especially vulnerable to climate change, and both women and men are central actors in this sector\*\*. Climate change will exacerbate existing vulnerabilities for women and other socio-economically marginalized groups, given their specific livelihood circumstances. In relative terms, women lack access to productive resources, and reduced impacts are more severe for women, and other socio-economically marginalized groups.

# Annex VIII - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

This has impacts on their health, food security and safety in the context of flooding and other extreme climate events. The additional socio-cultural barriers limiting participation and movement outside the household sphere is worsened by phenomena such as flooding, drought and erratic rainfall. These constraints are particularly acute for women who, in addition to bearing the brunt of these disasters, also bear additional income through livelihoods which, in turn, diminishes their ability to advance out of poverty, particularly when they have, for example, lost their land due to the impacts of flooding during cyclone events.

Gender equality and women's empowerment are not addressed in most of Haiti’s strategy or action plan documents for climate change and disaster risk management.134 Flooding has cumulative livelihood effects on women who are disproportionately represented among the poor and there is a significant portion of women-headed households. Women are also more likely to be displaced by disasters, with 70% of those in displacement and loss of life due to floods ultimately affecting women first and foremost. And the evidence is clear: women’s vulnerability to climate change and disaster risk is increasing.135 Studies have shown surges in GBV and food insecurity. Yet, during these extreme events, the importance of grassroots organizing and mobilization women's groups during disaster relief efforts was unparalleled and critical, demonstrating the resilience of Haitian women in the face of extreme adversity.

### 4.2 Socio-cultural norms and economic sectors

Women have played a crucial role in Haitian society since the beginning of the nation’s history, communicating with French colonial authorities through their leaders, such as Marie-Josèphe Tasca, and activists mobilizing for the right to vote in the 1950s. Today they continue to play central although largely invisible roles in the economy, particularly in agriculture, artisanal production, domestic work, and grassroots disaster relief. While gender equality was enshrined into Haiti’s current constitution in 2006, discrimination against women remains a reality for women at all levels of public office; gender gaps are recurrent and found in all aspects of Haitian society.

Over 40% of households in Haiti are headed by women, and this has been the case since 2007.135 Today, women’s economic participation is still limited to agriculture, artisanal production, domestic work, and rural areas.136 In fact most of Haiti's population is rural, and two-thirds of this population is dependent on agriculture; among them, women make up the majority of labor force involved in all aspects of agricultural activities.

While Haiti has indeed made advances to close gender gaps, persistent challenges remain. Sexist attitudes and beliefs that permeate Haiti’s social fabric continue to limit women's job opportunities and meaningful participation in decision-making processes.137 The lack of access to land ownership by women within the household and the burden of this unpaid, mobilized labor is an additional obstacle. Specific legislation has been adopted to address these issues; however, there are still significant gaps between provisions against women within public institutions, notably regarding domestic and child labor laws, penal codes regarding sexual harassment, intimate partner violence, and rape. Additionally, legalities regarding abortion and common-law partnerships hinder women's ability to exercise and enjoy their


---

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## 4.3 Gender roles, power, and decision-making

In the household, women generally have decision-making independence, taking on the majority of household decisions related to budgeting and the allocation of financial resources (e.g., small expenses, food purchases). However, in many countries, women are still less likely than men to be involved in major decision-making for household purchases[21]. Nonetheless, prevailing sexist beliefs and social norms regarding men and women in Haitian society inform the "acceptable" gender roles at school, in the workplace, and within the family. These roles are not determined by economic factors nor the same economic and political influences[22][23]. Manifesting along geographic and class divides, the spatial distribution of women in society is an important barrier that reinforces existing gender inequalities in the country.

Traditionally, men hold senior positions of influence and importance, namely as politicians, spiritual

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

leaders, school headmasters**, while women and girls assume the responsibility of primary caretakers of the household and well-being**. Women spend twice as much time on domestic tasks compared to men (13.4 h/wk vs. 7h/wk)**, and this difference is more pronounced in rural areas**. Children are often responsible for fetching water and firewood, which can take from five hours per day collecting firewood and numerous hours per day collecting water**. In fact, over a third of households must travel 90 minutes or more to access potable water supplies**. Moreover, not only are women and girls burdened with these tasks but they also bear the responsibility for food preparation and cooking, which is often done up to do so at the community-level**. Although a man's thread in the social fabric and primordial to the daily functioning of Haitian life, this work is typically undervalued, invisibilized and non-remunerated.

With climate change and the threat natural disasters, women inevitably devote more time and effort on these tasks, which can have a negative impact on family income. This can exacerbate their psychological and physical health, and further limit their capacity to adapt and recover from these events**. For example, girls (and sometimes boys) are pulled from school to help with domestic tasks in climate crises.

Women also play a key role in the rural economy, being the main actors in the commercialization, processing, marketing, and distribution of agricultural products. They are involved in both cash crop activities and animal husbandry. In rural areas, where small-scale agricultural production is a livelihood pillar, both men and women engage in agricultural wage labor, but frequently at different stages of the value chain. Women are more likely to be employed as seasonal workers on large farms or as hired hands for the cultivation of export crops (coffee, mangoes, etc.), and large livestock husbandry; they also have wage jobs. Women, on the other hand, are more involved in small-scale agricultural production, such as subsistence food security, including the supply of drinking water. In times of recent climate crises and natural disasters, women often bear a disproportionate burden for their families' survival, which can compromise the security and health of women, girls and other vulnerable groups. This demonstrates the power and potential of grassroots women groups in the fight for gender equity, despite the aforementioned barriers.

## 4.4 Gender and cross-cutting issues: Poverty

Half of the poorest countries in the world, with 59% of the population living on less than 2.5 USD per day (equivalent to about 136.3 gallons)**. Over the last thirty years, there has been a "feminization" of poverty** and today are more women in extreme poverty than men, living on USD 1.23 per day**. However, this is not necessarily due to gender discrimination or inequality. This can be explained by factors such as age, number of children, education, and industry of employment, while the remaining two-thirds is unexplained, suggesting some gender discrimination in the labor market

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Health and Sanitation

In Haiti, access to sanitation services is precarious, especially for remote rural households, due to cost, distance, and quality of facilities. This was already an outstanding issue for the health of women, young children, and other vulnerable groups (Persons with disabilities, the elderly), but recent natural disasters have exacerbated this situation. The lack of access to clean water and sanitation infrastructure is evident: maternal mortality rates in Haiti are among the highest in Latin America and Caribbean region[47]. In 2015, 38% of Haitian women gave birth outside a health facility[48], and that unplanned and advanced births have long-term impacts on women’s lives.

The widespread occurrence of contaminated water sources, inadequate sanitation facilities, and low access to clean drinking water is a major public health concern in Haiti. Women and children are especially vulnerable to health risks as they are the primary providers and handlers of water in households[49]. Waterborne diseases such as cholera, typhoid fever, dysentery, and hepatitis A are common. Children are affected by diarrhea and, to-date, cholera has killed thousands of people – with severe outbreaks being triggered by recent natural disasters. This is particularly relevant to young and pregnant women’s health and the health of their children.

Severe climate events and flooding are associated with an increased risk of infection of waterborne diseases, including typhoid fever, leptospirosis, Hepatitis A as well as cholera. Moreover, increased sedimentation in rivers and lakes due to deforestation and soil erosion can lead to contamination that is especially high when there is significant population displacement and/or contamination of drinking-water sources (i.e., flooding of sanitation infrastructure, sewage treatment plants, latrines, etc)[50].

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Above and beyond the primary risks of injury or drowning, floods can also increase risks of vector-borne diseases, such as malaria, dengue, yellow fever, and West Nile Fever, as they may expand the number and range of vector habitats[46].

When cholera, or other illnesses, affect households and communities, women and girls disproportionately bear the burden in the work of caring for sick family members and uphold the responsibility of restoring household and collective well-being[47]. Consequently, health behaviors promoted by aid agencies to prevent disease transmission (such as handwashing) often end up placing more work on the shoulders of women and girls.

Previous reports on the gendered effects of natural disasters and disaster relief[48] highlight the important role that women play in providing care for their families during and after a flood, including women and girls as well as LBTQI and PWG. The increased incidence of gender-based violence during climate crises and population displacement is significant, and there are security risks of utilizing public sanitation facilities, especially for women and girls[49]. In addition to the physical damage caused by flooding (which engender stronger psychological burdens and potentially important psycho-social impacts), the economic costs of recovery efforts are often minimized or understood as secondary rather than central in projects and relief efforts[50].

## Education

Nearly half of all Haitian children do not attend school[51], highlighting the mixed challenges both girls and boys face in accessing free, quality education – especially those from poor and rural households.

The public education system faces shortages of qualified teachers, limited supplies and a lack of infrastructure. In 2015, for example, hurricane Matthew damaged 1,633 out of 1,991 schools in the most impacted regions[52]. The private sector has stepped up to fill this gap by providing education services; however, many of these are operated by Canada, France or the United States, with school fees that are prohibitive for many families. This is especially true when it comes to secondary and tertiary education, where youth remain out of primary and secondary schools[53], which has created and reinforces an educational divide between boys and girls.

Although the available data suggests that both boys and girls have equal access to education – with a near gender parity in terms of education levels and similar literacy rates among men and women[54] – some attention is paid to the fact that girls are more likely than boys to drop out before completing their secondary education, especially when it comes to the advancement of boys, while young girls face numerous obstacles[55]. Indeed, in post-secondary institutions, women students are underrepresented compared to men[56], and in the job market they remain marginalized.
