tend to play fewer paying jobs. In more "normalized" niches[61] gaps among rural and urban areas also highlight the geographic and class dimension of education, where only about 64% of women in rural areas are literate, compared to 84% in urban areas; and this is a most acute among poor households[62].

To counteract the gender stereotypes that shape woman's life options, the MCDF signed protocol discourse[63] on equal access to education and training for girls and women, which was adopted by the education system. Nonetheless, gender roles and the uneven division of unpaid labor continue to limit the opportunities of women in accessing a quality education, by compelling them to the household and burdening them with domestic chores.

Enforced child labor is a prevailing risk[64] in specific sectors, such as domestic work for girls and construction for boys. The lack of access to education and training also leads to early marriage, which may be said to hold more affluent families to work as "restituves"[65] (domestic workers) under regulated and often ajoit conditions[66]. Recurrent climate threats further exacerbate this gender risk, as impoverished women are forced to sell their children for school fees or to buy food. Children may drop out of school to cope with livelihood stress and, in extreme cases, sell children for labor as a survival strategy.

## Political participation

While women face no legal obstacles to vote and stand for election, in the 2015 presidential and legislative elections, over 90% of the candidates were men[67]. While a constitutional amendment in 1987 decreed that at least one-third of the seats should be reserved for women, gender inequalities continue to exist; and its especially evident among the positions of power that are dominated by men. In fact, only 25% of elected officials in Haiti are women[68], currently only six of women in the Haitian parliament (2017), while in 2003 there was 9% of women[69].

This gender gap is evident at all levels and branches of the government. At the local level, only 7% of CDD/CCD[70] presidents are women; and at the national level, only one out of ten mayors are women (data from 2013)[71]. Each municipality is governed by a board of 3 members. There is a limited number of cities that have a woman on the board at level and these women often do not occupy leadership roles.

In judiciary and prosecutorial positions, only 32 out of 583 judges are women (5.2%), while in the superior courts, only one out of ten is female[72]. In the National Assembly, there were no women among the current 92 Deputies and 24 Senators recently elected into office[73]. In Haitian Senate, on a staff of 30 (29 active) senators, there is only one woman. For the Chamber of Deputies, there are 15 women out of 168 members; and in the National Assembly, women represent less than 7% of the Legislative Power. Moreover, throughout the history of certain Ministries (e.g., Ministry of Agriculture), all of the important posts have been and are still held by men.

Rural women are often excluded from decision-making processes or included in the design of public policies

and, at the national level, few actions have been taken to create a space where women can fully and meaningfully participate in environmental management (1). This is even more pronounced in areas outside the capital and in the target regions of this project(2).

Watershed management is led by the Ministry of Agriculture and the Ministry of Environment – which are primarily male-dominated. Women who have been involved in engagement in management activities (e.g., association of irrigators) typically include very few women(3). With respect to climate change and disaster risk reduction, women’s voices are often not heard or taken into account during policy-making processes, despite recognition of the gendered dimensions of migration and adaptation.

MCDP's recent inquiry into women’s low rates of political participation(4) indicates that primary barriers to wider participation in public life include lack of education, financial resources, and access to information (5). These concerns are especially relevant for rural women. While women have historically been highly involved in social and political activities at the household level, they are often excluded from decision-making processes due to the fear of losing one’s life and leaving children behind(6). These obstacles are only compounded by climate change and disaster risk reduction (7), which can exacerbate existing vulnerabilities and further burden the roles of work and care demands on women.

Recognizing that women have historically been excluded from political participation and decisions on the direction of development projects, MCDP has identified gender aspects to their project to reinforce the capacity of women and their participation in development projects(8). And, their priority areas have been identified as: (i) Water Resources Management; (ii) Disaster Risk Management; and (iii) Health. However, policy decisions and practices are still being implemented, and women are still largely excluded from in policymaking and decision-making processes.

Labor force participation

The work force is segregated by gender, and women have considerably fewer employment opportunities than men across all age groups(9). The most recent available data shows that women make up only 38.4% of the active population (or 47.7% of the working population), although women earn less than men in all sectors except for agriculture(10). In terms of senior management and professional positions, women are underrepresented in the higher paying, more influential positions in this sector(11). In fact, this is echoed across all sectors, where women tend to have the lowest paying jobs with (12).

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

particularly high disparities in private sector salary*, and they are underrepresented among professional jobs such as engineering, agronomy, and law; with the project's region of application, the level of employment is especially low, with an unemployment rate of more than 42%. This, along other factors, makes women more vulnerable to poverty. Women who migrate for work are looking for opportunities in other countries, namely in Dominican Republic or North America – mainly working as undocumented immigrants.

Women predominate in the informal sector (75%), where women are often exploited and undocumented, so exploitation and income stability are reliably gender concerns*. This also means that, in socio-economic crises, women will be the primary victims and it will be largely undetectable or unreported. The 2016 Haiti Earthquake was a major disaster for women and their families. According to fact, post-earthquake data indicates that almost all Haitian babies between the ages of 3 and 9 work in the informal sector (UNICEF, 2017). In addition, according to UNICEF (2016), the prevalence of "restavèks" – children sold/adopted to wealthier families to perform chores or exchange for food and shelter – is also a major concern. According to the same source, based on recent estimates, there may be as many as 300,000 children working as restavèks in Haiti*. Although the minimum age is set to 15 for work in industrial, agricultural, and commercial enterprises, there is currently no law that prohibits child labor.

Women also hold a majority (70%) of jobs in trade in the primary sector, especially in agriculture where they are the primary actors working in commercialization as well as processing and transportation*. The work of women in this sector is often informal and precarious. This means that they have little access to stable income sources, that are dependent on the changing environment and climate change*, which makes them more vulnerable to poverty. This indicates that there is a lot of potential for projects to support women's role in agriculture and to benefit women as part of climate change resilience building initiatives.

Agriculture and food security

Agriculture has been the backbone of Haiti’s economy and an architect of Haitian society since the colonial area. Today, a majority of agricultural production occurs on small farms, primarily oriented towards subsistence (FAO, 2016; World Bank, 2017; UNICEF, 2018; The Future Project, 2018). Both women and men participate in the cultivation of subsistence crops (namely of tubers and beans) as well as commercial crops such as coffee, cocoa, sugar cane, rice, and bananas. Although these crops (Beans, rice, corn) are most vulnerable to crop loss from climate change*, demonstrating the disproportionate impacts incurred by women and rural, low-income households.

Women have traditionally been excluded from decision-making in agriculture and other sectors of the sector are skewed by gender. While women primarily work with domestic trade and commerce**, the lucrative export commodities, such as essential oils, mango, cacao and coffee, tend to be controlled by men*. Women face additional income generating obstacles in this sector: with limited access to private

# Annex VIII - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

transportation. They are more likely to use public services to undergo transitions[98]. This not only contributes to the time poverty women already face, but also highlights their livelihood vulnerability to extreme flooding, which decreases transportation options and limits their ability to access markets in order to sell their produce.

Not only do economically consumed crops generate less income than cash crops of coffee, cocoa, and mangoes[100]. Given the gender distribution of agricultural labor, women are more likely to be employed on small farms. This is especially true for women who are married or widowed, and access to development program benefits, indeed, export commodity value chains are often promoted a favorable pathway to generating more income in the country, however, over-investment in these cash crops has led to a decline in food security.

Furthermore, there has been minimal support and regulation from the state for women in agricultural value chains; nonetheless, women have innovated by establishing expensive information and trade networks. Women are also responsible for managing livestock, which is often done with limited access to machinery like tractors or machine labor, while women assist with tasks such as harvesting and weeding and tending to smaller livestock herds[102].

Over the last 50 years, agricultural production, processing, and marketing have decreased, and today it accounts for about 25% of gross national product[103], given women's central role in agriculture this has implications for their economic empowerment. Women are responsible for most of the food production; productivity is often low on farms; compounded by population growth, Haiti's agricultural sector is struggling to meet domestic demand and generate income.

For rural farming families, production does not often generate enough income to meet basic dietary household needs. In fact, only a quarter of their cash income originates from farming, so livelihoods must be diversified[104]. Women are responsible for most of the food production in Haiti; however, they feed half the population, while Haiti imports as much as 60% of its food, making it especially vulnerable to global economic and food crisis. Trade liberalization has exposed farmers to competition from subsidized agricultural products from the United States and Canada[105]. This has also disrupted local markets and agriculture (IGAD, 2013) from USAID (2016). As such, access to nutritious and stable diets is a major concern for women in Haiti. Women's role as producers of food is critical for production, and this is only exacerbated by climate change. In fact, among the communes most affected by climate change are those with the highest levels of poverty.

Food security is thus a central, cross-cutting component that is relevant to any climate resilience intervention for both men and women. Given women's role in the production, distribution, and provision of food to their communities, it is critical to consider gender equality when addressing food security issues.

### Land access

Notwithstanding their central role in agriculture and food security, women's access to and rights over productive land remain limiting factors that contribute to gender gaps and poverty[106]. Women often


---
**Page 165**
---

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

face difficulties obtaining legal titles and do not often gain equal land inheritance, as such, they rarely own land or rent land on land that belongs to their male relatives[108]. Exasperated by climate extremes and population growth, low-income families and women-led households are thus often relegated to degraded, overexploited, unproductive, infertile lands for subsistence production, namely denuded, steep-sloping terrain[109].

Article 36 of Haiti's Constitution recognizes and guarantees the right to private property. In practice, however, this right is not always respected by laws and courts. Customary rights are less evident in urban areas [Smucker et al., 2005; Howard 1998].

Rural land ownership is generally governed by customary law and traditional practice as opposed to formal law. Women's access to land varies widely across rural Haiti, depending on the type of communal land management although land held by families may remain undivided [Smucker et al., 2005].

Haiti's formal laws promote principles of nondiscrimination. The 1987 Constitution (Article 18) mandates the equality of all Haitians. Women achieved equality under the law in 1982, when a decree was passed that established the equality of the sexes, particularly within marriage [GON CONSTITUTION 1982; De Ferranti et al., 2003].

In practice, the formal law often does not impact the reality faced by Haitian women. A majority of couples in Haiti have common-law marriages that are not registered with the state. Common-law marriage is recognized as a legal relationship under customary law and allows spouses to inherit their inheritances from their husbands' land [De Ferranti 2002].

Under both formal and customary laws, married daughters do not automatically have equal rights to the land owned by their deceased parents, although female heirs tend to receive smaller shares of land. Customary law favors the possessor of land. Heirs who remain on the land assume control of absentee shares and consolidate them with their own land [Smucker et al., 2005]. This practice is often used as a means for the community of their access to inherited land [Smucker et al., 2005; Gardella, 2006].

Married out daughters usually lose their shares of land to relatives because unregistered customary practice does not recognize women's rights to inherit their intended share and they are not personally farm it. Instead, they give the land to a relative [Smucker et al., 2005].

In general, women's property rights derive from their status as wives, mothers, or wives and thus women often do not have equal rights in terms of inheritance under customary law. The customary laws that are prevalent in rural areas exclude women from land ownership. Archeological evidence suggests that women were the primary owners of land before the French colonial period [The rights of women in households, headed by men (De Ferranti et al., 2003)].

Even though a woman doesn't hold the title to property – it's rare to find one who has a title anyway usually only men have titles. Women are responsible for their children and supports, and collaborate with the (husband or father) of family to make sure that they and their children can eat, that the children can go to school, that they can pay for their health needs etc.

Comment: The gender assessment and action plan is a strategic plan for what will be the priorities and objectives for merging women's issues and land rights, and thus launch a program that all of the women's organizations in Haiti can participate. It is important to have a clear vision and goals so as to align their technical, political, social, and economic capacity What's most important, consciousness-raising, training and action. ## Forest resources and charcoal production

Charcoal is infamously known as one of the most deforested areas in the world, with population growth, and growing demand for energy, being the main drivers behind this. The FAO estimates that 20%–45% of forest loss and degradation over the last century. While the FAO states that 2%–4% forest cover are often seen as a good indicator of deforestation, the reality is more complex. The global charcoal production rate between 1955-32%, depending on the data and methods used[10]. This incites an e-revaluation of the predominant narrative about environmental degradation and the use of forest resources in the country. In Haiti, charcoal production is a major source of income for women (48%) and men (50% in the case of coffee)[11].

The majority (over 90%) of Haitian households meet their energy needs by charcoal and firewood, as only about 23% have access to electricity – primarily in urban areas[12]. In fact, about 10,000 sacks of charcoal are used a day[13]. Both men and women are actively involved in the production of charcoal, while women are more likely to be involved in the collection of firewood (for cooking, heating, lighting, and cleaning)[14]. As such, this is an issue of relevance to both gender and environmental management.

With a stable national demand for charcoal, it is often a supplementary or primary source of revenue for poor households. However, there are also negative impacts on the environment from charcoal production involving the cutting of fast-growing trees such as cassia and acacia (that coppice and regrow within 4 years) to be used in the production of charcoal[15]. This is often done by women who are considered nannies[16]. As such, interest is surging on how to integrate, rather than eliminate, charcoal production and use into programs and projects.

Women differ from men with respect to their roles within the household and the economy; the intended use and preferences of forest resources are often distinct between men and women. For example, recent reforestation initiatives in Haiti lead by the CCAF found that men tended to favor tree species for charcoal production (e.g., acacia, casuarina) while women preferred trees used as fruit or vegetable crops to integrate into home gardens[17]. In some regions of Haiti, there may also be taboos around the use of certain tree species by gender. Women are often excluded from decision-making and political leadership positions[18], their power and control over belief systems that shape these practices can be particularly strong[19]. Thus, it is a target for gender equality programming to include women in planning, implementing, and monitoring efforts that involve reforestation efforts. Indeed, evaluation of previous reforestation attempts in Haiti indicate that interventions are most successful when there is a strong

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Access to productive land

In Haiti, women make up a majority of the labor force in agriculture, contributing to all aspects of production and specifically commercialization and trade[123], yet ownership and rights over productive land resources is highly skewed by gender. The national statistics are at best mixed, with only 9% property titles held by women (UNFPA[124]). Land tenure is regulated by a civil code (enacted in 1962), which requires tenure registration for all land. However, the majority of rural households do not have formal title to their land; it is de facto declared by a sales receipt[125], indicating that the land tenure situation is uncertain and fragile across much of Haiti.

Most rural households (80%) have access to at least one hectare of land[126], with climate change and soil degradation however, the productivity of land is declining. Fertile land may have decreased by as much as 50% in some areas over the past decade due to erosion[127]. The average rural household has lost hectares per year due to erosion linked to a combination of land-use practices and climate change[128].

Haiti has a particular and highly informal land ownership scheme rooted in traditional family relations that are passed down through generations. Land is inherited by sons, who then divide the land through inheritance and distributed among children, which has contributed to the fragmentation of land into smaller and smaller parcels over time. With each generation, the inheritance situation is re-evaluated, such that women and girls are often excluded from inheriting land[129]. This means that women pass on their rights to the next generation.

For women and girls, these tenure customs are a serious obstacle regarding their access to and securing their rights over productive land. Although inheritance is bilateral, with both daughters and sons being included in the division of land and homes, daughters traditionally receive smaller inheritances[130]. Inheritance laws have been changed several times since independence (1987), but women continue to have little legal and intentional recourse to affirm these rights, as common law relationships and unofficial arrangements are still widely practiced. Women who work in the fields as hired laborers or "restavek" (child domestic workers, who are often young girls), they are not recognized in informal hereditary arrangements; moreover, they retain their original family names and consequently have no legal rights to land.

For landless households, sharecropping is common, particularly under informal agreements of the "mitié" system, whereby up to half of the agricultural production is collected by the property owner[131]. Most of these agreements are not formalized in writing or registered with the government. The lack of written contracts and the informal nature of the arrangements make it difficult for women to assert their rights over land.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Within certain agroclimatic practices—which account for up to one-third of agroforestry—secondary areas do not have the same family status; yet men may still collect yields and thus revenue from their farms or gardens[1].

Overall, the situation of women rights and land access is particularly precarious for women-led and poor-landholding small households, and this deeply interconnected to climate vulnerability. Being constrained to farming on small, unfertile plots and steep slopes, the livelihood security and well-being of these marginalized groups are further undermined by a lack of access to extension services, credit, markets, infrastructure, tropical storms, and natural disasters[2]. In fact, conflict over increasingly scarce productive resources has multiplied in recent years[3]. Further land conflicts may emerge from natural disasters, as post-earthquake reconstruction efforts often involve communities moving back to rural areas to farm or claim land[4].

## Access to extension services and finances

With the prevalence of new aid projects from donors, NGOs and even the private sector, agricultural extension services have been increasingly privatized. The number of public agricultural extension services has diminished in the last decades[5]. According to a World Bank (2013) study, both men and women are equally likely to receive agricultural extension services in certain regions of Haiti; although this is probably due to the fact that women are more likely than men to be involved in family agriculture because of gender equal access[6]. Moreover, the report found that there are fewer positive outcomes of agricultural extension for women than for men.

In terms of credit, the MDG highlights that eligibility criteria of microfinancing institutions create barriers for women to access sufficient funds to effectively implement agrofarm projects or transition to other economic activities. In addition, the lack of collateral and the high interest rates associated with commercialization and sale of produce, low access to credit is a missed opportunity to build their climate resilience and increase livelihood security.

## Gender-based Violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH)

In Haiti, gender-based violence (GBV) is a pervasive problem; and current legal and judiciary structures do not adequately address this vulnerability. It is a major obstacle for the empowerment of women and girls, as it reduces their ability to fully participate in the public and private spheres of life. In one three-month period in 2013, 54% of women experienced physical and/or sexual violence; and the rate of intimate partner violence has risen since 2005[7].

Climate and natural disasters only increase the incidence, as violence and sexual assault in displaced persons’ and IDP camps has been reported to be widespread[8][9]. The World Bank summarizes that residents indicate that women and girls are up to 14 times more likely to be harmed during a

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Disaster for those who survive, climate-induced disasters can amplify gender inequalities, making them more vulnerable to GBV, even when life for others may go back to normal.

### Solidarité Femmes Ayitiennes (Haitian Women’s Solidarity - SOFA) - Haitian feminist organization of approximately 10,000 members nationwide, 80% of whom are peasant women – along with the Global Justice Center, conducted a study in 2013 on how land grabbing is a source of violence against women and other human rights violations in this country.

A majority of GBV cases go unnoticed and unreported – as the most recent surveys indicate that only 43.1% of women never told anyone, while 65% percent of women did not seek help of any kind[20]. When they do seek help, it is often from a non-governmental organization (NGO) or a church, and this seeking of assistance accompanies a fear of revenge measure from abusers and the perception that official avenues are ineffective in terms[21]. Indeed, the prevalence of police violence targeting women and other marginalized groups has been documented by several organizations. The Haitian government has not responded regarding conflicts of any kind[21]. Among cases of violence reported in 2009–2011, the majority were physical (58%) followed by psychological (34%), with sexual violence constituting a minority (6%). Economic and economic violence constituted a minority (5.8% and 4.5% respectively)[22]. Although very few of these cases are reported or prosecuted[23]. See data below from UN Women’s Global Database on Violence against Women.

| **Physical Violence by Non-Intimate Partner** | 30% |
| **Psychological Violence by Non-Intimate Partner** | 48% |
| **Child Marriage** | 19% |

Even when cases that are reported, justice for victims of GBV is hindered by the significant gender imbalance in Haiti’s legal system. In 2016, there were only 32 women judges out of a total of 615[24] and today the percentages remain about the same. Moreover, with women disproportionately represented among the poor, those with low education and living in poverty, they generally have lower access to legal aid and justice systems[25].

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Currently, there is no specific legislation to deal with domestic violence or sexual harassment, and while rape was criminalized just over a decade ago, alarmingly the law still does not recognize spousal rape as a crime. Although the Government has made efforts to combat violence against women and girls, with the passage of the Criminal Code (27 March 1852) and the Criminal Code (9 May 1862), inspired by the French Napoleonic Code, were amended in 1930 to include provisions for spousal rape. On the other hand, the Code of Civil Instruction of 12 April 1852, which was based on the French Civil Code of 28 March 1806 (art. 4) a woman cannot have the status of trader without her husband’s consent. Only the Labour Code of 1961 affirms the equality of the sexes in terms of access to

Several constitutions have succeeded one after another and first establish a turning point for the situation of women in society. The Constitution, guaranteed by the Constitutional Court, was in force, adopted on 10 March 1987 by a Constituent Assembly, which was approved in a referendum on March 1987, as amended, unequivocally enshrines the principle of equality of men and women before the law (art. 24) and guarantees their rights to vote and stand for election (arts. 30, 28, 32.1 and 32.6).

Thus, at the level of civil legislation, the numerous laws published between 1825 and 1852 contained progressive provisions that were maintained until the 20th century: the Civil Code (27 March 1852) and the Criminal Code (9 May 1862), inspired by the French Napoleonic Code, were amended in 1930 to include provisions for spousal rape. On the other hand, the Code of Civil Instruction of 12 April 1852, which was based on the French Civil Code of 28 March 1806 (art. 4) a woman cannot have the status of trader without her husband’s consent. Only the Labour Code of 1961 affirms the equality of the sexes in terms of access to

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

The Ministry of Women and Women’s Rights (MCDFE) is tasked with ensuring that signed agreements and conventions (domestic and international) are implemented, as part of its broader mission to promote gender equality in all areas of life.

Recently, the adoption of the Gender Equality Bill as well as the implementation of the National Plan for Gender Equality 2015-2024 have been a major step forward. The Ministry is committed to women’s continued efforts to protect the rights of women[1].

The ministry's operations are divided across four areas: Women’s Rights Promotion Directorate (DPD), Gender Analysis Directorate (DAG), the Democratic Participation and Representation Directorate (DPRD) and the National Coordination Office (ONC).

MCDFE representatives for Gender Focus Points/ Point focal gender are in place across ministries and state structures to coordinate and collaborate on women's rights and gender equality in the public sphere. They also participate in national and international conferences, seminars, workshops, and awareness-raising and training activities. However, the MCDFE faces insufficient budget allocation, as well as a lack of resources for its implementation.

The Office for the Fight against Violence Against Women (Bureau de Lutte contre la Violence Faire sur les Femmes) was created in 2015 under the Ministry of Justice and the Ministry of Security and Justice. It is managed according to a Protocol signed by the three ministries. The office is a public service office offering support and quality care to women and girls who are victims of specific violence and ensuring access to information and quick response to cases.

## Criminal Code – Under Haitian law, rape is classified as a crime and therefore it is subject to a 10-year statute of limitations. Rape is not considered as being a “crime de sang”, meaning a crime which resulted in the death of the victim. The maximum penalty for rape is five years imprisonment or life prison sentence if the victim is an adult and 15 years maximum if the victim is a minor under the age of 20.

The decree changed the categorization of rape from a crime against morality to a crime against the person, which broadens its scope. The law now includes sexual violence as well as other forms of specific violence as it failed to accurately define the crime of rape and to list its elements. Judges and prosecutors are therefore required to resort to definitions of rape found outside of the code[15] thus leading to inconsistencies in the application of the law, which can lead to different treatment for similar cases.

Incorporating gender and gender responsive policy planning is an essential part of effectively and strategically addressing climate change impacts. Overall, Haiti’s mechanisms and reform plans for gender equality are based on a number of international agreements and conventions for women's rights. Some of these have been ratified in Haiti, including:

* The Convention for the Suppression of Human Trafficking and Exploitation of the Prostitution of Others September 2, 1952

* The Convention on the political rights of Women, July 31, 1957 (Please note that the Haitian women has the right to vote since 1950)
* The Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination with regard to Women (CEDAW), 1979
* The Convention on the fight against discrimination in the field of Education, October 15, 1984
* The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, 23 November 1990
* The Optional Protocol to CEDAW, 1999
* The Inter American Convention on the prevention, punishment and eradication of violence Against Women, April 1994

## Climate change governance and gender in Haiti – There are the links between Haiti’s climate change governance structures and the established frameworks for gender equality and women's empowerment (i.e., MCFD, National Gender Equality Policy2016). The National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) 2015-2030 is aligned with the UNFCCC Paris Agreement – promotes gender integration in all aspects of its implementation. In addition to the NAPA, Haiti has also developed a Climate Change Strategy, aligned with the UNCCCE Paris Agreement – promotes gender integration in all aspects of its implementation. The Ministry of Environment (MIDE) is responsible for national climate change policy and coordination. The National Committee on Climate Change (CNCCC), which is the Second National Communication on Climate Change (DNCCC), gender considerations were taken into account in four priority areas linked to climate change adaptation: agriculture and food security, water resources management, energy sector, and urban development.

## Changes Climatiques en Haïti. Le Ministère de l'Environnement (MIDE) est principalement responsable pour la politique nationale sur le changement climatique et la coordination. Le Comité national sur les changements climatiques (CNCCC), qui fait partie du deuxième communiqué national sur les changements climatiques (DNCCC), a pris en compte les considérations de genre dans quatre domaines prioritaires liés au changement climatique : agriculture et sécurité alimentaire, gestion des ressources en eau, secteur énergétique et urbanisation.

### A National Disaster Risk Reduction System is in place, with coordination across 10 ministries, and a Disaster Management Plan has been developed. While change adaptation plans do not disaster risks as a priority area, there is limited coordination and information sharing among the two167. Day-to-day disaster risk reduction activities are overseen by the Department of Civil Protection (DPC), which is managed by the Ministry of Interior and Public Security (MININT). Women are highly vulnerable to the livelihood impacts of extreme climate events and natural disasters, while the high incidence of GBV in emergency shelters further exacerbates their vulnerability. The current disaster risk reduction planning does give some consideration to the specific vulnerabilities of women (and other vulnerable groups) but the level of integration is inconsistent across all administrative levels168. Donor agencies are supporting the development of gender-inclusive practices at temporary emergency shelters and there are on-going capacity building and training activities to support gender-responsive disaster and emergency responses in the country.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Project-specific design consideration resulting from the Gender Assessment

Although Haiti faces challenges with respect to its capacities and resources for water-related governance and climate change adaptation and mitigation[1][2][3], the country is attempting to make advances in improved and integrated water management despite significant setbacks related to recent natural disasters. The gender assessment was conducted as part of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) design, governments, international NGOs, and researchers that aim to strengthen capacities and support Haiti in its commitment to building climate resilience and promoting gender equality within its policies and planning.

The gender assessment carried out highlights the complexities of gender relations in Haiti and identifies barriers to women’s empowerment. The analysis is a basis for supporting, strengthening, and opportunities to improve gender-responsive water governance and management risk reduction in the context of climate change adaptation.

Building on this, the following challenges and opportunities for gender-responsive integrated flood management and development of green and grey infrastructure in the context of climate change adaptation in Haiti are highlighted:

- Women are disproportionately vulnerable to flooding and climate change, with impacts on their livelihoods, health and safety, and of unpaid labor burdens.
  - A majority of women and women-led households depend on agriculture for their livelihood. Women are responsible for producing most of the food consumed at home and produce some of the most vulnerable crops and are more likely to face obstacles of unproductive land and meager land tenure.
  - Health risks associated with climate change are higher for women (especially pregnant and lactating) as well as other vulnerable populations, e.g., children and elderly). Women also undertake the primary care labour within the household; therefore, they are at a greater risk of exposure to waterborne diseases.
  - Women face high risks of violence during and in the aftermath of disasters: in rapid evolos post the 2012 earthquake conducted by humanitarian organizations, for example, 75% of women reported that they had experienced sexual violence. 70% of women and men surveyed said their fear of sexual violence had increased since the earthquake. Women are also more likely to be displaced from their homes since the earthquake. 70% of organizations say women and girls are most at risk of GBV.
- Climate changes such as flooding or drought, place an additional burden of unpaid labor on women, as they are primarily responsible for family/community water use/purification.

Through the Activity I.1, that aims to capacitate farmer’s groups as well as women’s cooperatives and associations, several transformative sub-activities have been designed to ensure the above challenges are addressed. Further, the food coupon distribution (Activity 2.3) will also target vulnerable households, with a specific focus on women-headed, female-led, widow-headed households.

**Page 174**

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

There is an institutional and policy making gender gap. Gender equality is not fully addressed in Haiti’s climate change, disaster risk, and water resource management strategies or action plans; as such, measures to promote gender-responsive adaptations and mitigate gender-specific vulnerabilities are lacking. Women leaders and women's associations do not fully participate in policymaking and decisions related to water resource management, disaster risks, and climate change across all levels.

Through the Activities 3.1 and 3.2, this challenge will be addressed. These activities aim to improve Haiti’s institutional and policy making framework as catchment-level governance framework, with a focus on improved gender and social inclusions.

## Recommendations from Haiti's existing on Climate Resilience and Gender Projects and Programmes

### Lessons Learnt

Lessons learnt from previous programmes and projects in the country and elsewhere can be drawn upon to support transformative potential of integrated flood management and effective flood control through soil conservation in the Trois Rivieres region and ensure equal participation and benefit sharing among all stakeholders.

A recent cross-review of policies, institutions, and interventions in water resource governance across several countries conducted by UNDP (2017) found that gender-responsive approaches should:

- Build strong collaboration with public water resources departments, ensuring that designing and implementing projects are inclusive and based on gender responsive assessments.
- Increase the capacity of local government representatives to ensure the effective participation and leadership of women in water management mechanisms.
- Support communities in updating principles of gender equity when establishing water-user groups.
- Support local municipal authorities and communities to develop and maintain social mechanisms for gender-equitable distribution of water resources.
- Ensure that all stakeholders, including women, have access to information related to gender equality and women receive equal access to technical or management training, peer-to-peer learning, and other resources.

With respect to the agricultural sector and climate change resilience, UNDP (2016) recommends that gender-responsive initiatives include:

- Close the gap in access to land: Eliminate discrimination under the law; Recognize the importance of women’s rights to land ownership; Educate women on their legal land tenure targets; Educate women regarding land rights; Ensure that women's voices are heard; Adjust bureaucratic procedures; Gather sex-disaggregated data for policy design and monitoring.
- Closing the gap in local labor markets: Target women’s multiple roles, reduce gender inequalities in human capital. Optimize on public work programmes; Strengthen women’s

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## rights and voice

Closing the financial services gap: Promote financial literacy; Design products that meet the needs of women; Promote a woman-friendly and empowering culture; Close the gap in social capital through women's groups; Use technology and innovative delivery channels.

Closing the technology gap: Develop technologies and environments that address women’s needs, including mobile phones, solar power, and other innovations.

With respect to the reforestation and agro-forestry systems in the context of climate change, IUCN (2008) recommended that gender-responsive initiatives:

- Foster a participatory approach by promoting the integration of women from local communities and promote systematic attention to the participation of women in forestry development in policies, strategies and capacity building efforts related to the conservation and sustainable development of forests.

Programmes should promote equal access of women to land ownership and other resources necessary for effective socio-economic participation in forest management and climate mitigation activities. This includes access to credit, markets, information, training (both formal and informal), extension services, market linkages, and technology.

Both men and women must be trained in methods, and forestry technologies, including nursery techniques, tree selection, and selection of species, land preparation, planting, weeding, and maintenance.

### Project Design and Implementation

Gender-based analysis within this project has been optimized by promoting the full involvement of women in decision-making regarding integrated flood management at the community and institutional levels, as well as the stewardship of agroforestry systems, playing key roles in the selection of species and transforming them into a source of income. However, there is still much to be done.

Strong efforts will continue to be made in order to ensure the equitable participation of women. Additionally, gender equality must be mainstreamed throughout all aspects of project design and implementation. This includes ensuring that gender equality is pursued and achieved in ongoing management and water governance especially on the Water Governance Unit (WGU).

Addressing gender dimensions within the project design and implementation, this proposal works to identify and integrate interventions to provide gender responsive and transformative results.

The project design and implementation will take into consideration the following gender implications:

- Specific strategies include / target female-headed households;
- Definition of gender equality;
- Identification of gaps in gender equality through the use of disaggregated data enabling development of a gender action plan to close those gaps, developing resources and expertise for implementing such plans;
- Addressing the lack of women’s representation on project boards and holding individuals and institutions accountable for outcomes that promote gender equality;
- Addressing the different needs and capacities of men and women in addressing gender differences. Strategies used in the project are then tailored, taking into account such differences;
- Inclusion of a Gender Specialist position / provision of advice within the project to implement gender-related activities.

# Annex VIII - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

During project implementation, qualitative assessments will be conducted on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated to the project. This will be incorporated in the annual Project Implementation Report, Mid-Term Report, and Terminal Evaluation. Indicators to quantify the achievement of these goals include: number of women who have access to affordable land; number of women who had access to affordable solutions; number of men and women employed from the jobs created by the project; training opportunities; knowledge management and information dissemination.

## Stakeholder engagement

The stakeholder analysis, participation and engagement of women's organizations promote gender equality at the local as well as the national level. The involvement of women's organizations in the project design, aided in identifying relevant gender issues within the country's social context, and implementing and monitoring the gender aspects of the project.

## Monitoring and evaluation

Through onset analysis, data will be collected and collated in the project's inception phase to establish a baseline. This data shall be monitored against throughout implementation and evaluation. In order to monitor and evaluate progress of the project, the following indicators can be measured:

### Quantitative outcomes
- Female-headed households as beneficiaries;
- Improvements in health and well-being;
- Improved access to credit;
- Business development services component targeting rural women entrepreneur groups.

### Qualitative outcomes
- Opportunities to generate additional income. Women are more likely to respond to incentives that meet their family's basic needs, such as better health and nutrition.
- Time-saving for women as a result of lower hours in labour required for agricultural and water management practices prior to the implementation of the project;
- Contribution of women to decision-making on household and community matters;
- Expanded engagement in public and project decision-making as a result of initiation of women peer-to-peer networks;
- Support for training and educational activities which may include activities related to climate change, agriculture, leadership, business, finance, entrepreneurship and decision-making; therefore, increasing the confidence of women (and/or increased involvement) of women to participate with confidence in community meetings;
- Effectiveness of awareness raising.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## 9. Gender Action Plan (GAP)

This Gender Action plan provides suggested entry points for gender-responsive actions to be taken under each of the Activity areas of the project, based on the Logical Framework of the Project (Section E – full Funding Proposal). In addition, specific indicators are also proposed to measure and track progress on these activities. The Action Plan is not a stand-alone document but rather an integral part of the overall project plan. It provides concrete recommendations on how to ensure gender (including disaggregated data) continues to be collected and measured throughout implementation.

UNDP – as the Accredited Entity – will be responsible for the implementation of this GAP, and the responsible parties column (see below) lays down the architecture at the activity-level. A Gender and Safeguards Expert will be part of the Project Management Unit (PMU) with the remit to deliver the GAF in collaboration with the Activity Leads. The PMU will also provide technical support to the Activity Leads on gender mainstreaming, as well as a focal point for gender-related issues. The Action Plan is not intended to replace the Gender Mainstreaming Strategy but rather to serve as a tool to supplement the available skill set and experience on the PMU – has been mentioned (indicative) where necessary in the Action Plan below.

The expected impact of this GAP is: Increased resilience to climate change of vulnerable communities, including women and girls; gender-equitable participation in territorial planning and household food and water security for increased climate change resilience

The expected results are: 100% of project direct beneficiaries (50-60%) of project direct beneficiaries for sustainable water and land management as a means to enhance their resilience to climate change.

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Appendix I: Glossary and List of Stakeholders

To ensure a common understanding, the following UNDP definitions apply to key terminology used throughout the document. It should be noted that UNDP operates in line with the principles of gender equality as an integral dimension of all operational activities for development call on all United Nations sites to improve interagency coordination and maximize impact. At the country level, UNDP supports the integration of gender equality in all common country analyses and United Nations Sustainable Development Cooperation Frameworks.

### Gender

* Refers to the social attributes and opportunities associated with being male and female and the relationship between women and men and girls.
* Sex refers to the biological characteristics that define males and females. These are determined at birth and are not learned through socialization processes. They are context-specific and changeable.
* Gender determines what is expected, allowed and valued in a given society or culture for people of different sexes. It is learned through socialization processes and is therefore changeable.
* Gender equality implies that the interests, needs and rights of both women and men are taken into consideration—recognizing the diversity of these interests, needs and rights within each group—and ensuring equal access to opportunities and benefits.
* Gender mainstreaming means maintaining a gender perspective in the process of designing implications for women and men of any planned action, including legislation, policies or programs, in all areas and at all levels. It is a strategy for making women’s as well as men’s concerns and experiences an integral dimension of the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of policies and programmes so that women and men benefit equally and inequality is not perpetuated.
* The ultimate goal is to achieve gender equality.

### Gender Equality

* Refers to the rights, responsibilities and opportunities of women and men and girls. Equality does not mean that women and men are identical but rather that they have equal status as human beings.
* Gender equality implies that the interests, needs and rights of both women and men are taken into consideration—recognizing the diversity of these interests, needs and rights within each group—and ensuring equal access to opportunities and benefits.

### Women’s Rights

* The human rights of women and/or the girl child are non-negotiable, integral and indivisible part of universal human rights.
* The full and equal participation of women in political, civil, economic, social and cultural life at the national, regional and international levels, and the eradication of all forms of discrimination on grounds of sex are priority objectives of the international community.


---
**Page 197**
---

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Women’s Empowerment

*Women’s empowerment has five components: Women’s sense of self-worth, their right to have and to determine choices, their right to have access to opportunities and resources, their right to have the power to control their own lives, both within and outside the home, and their ability to influence the direction of development.*

## Gender Parity

*equal numbers of men and women at all levels of the organization. It must include significant participation of both men and women, particularly at senior levels. Gender parity is one of several integrated mechanisms for improving organizational effectiveness*

## Gender-based Violence

*Gender-based violence is a form of discrimination that seriously inhibits women’s ability to enjoy rights and freedoms on a basis of equality with men. Discrimination against women in the field of employment, as well as in other areas of public or private life, under general international law or under human rights conventions, is discrimination within the meaning of Article 1 (2)CEDAW)*

*any act of violence that results in, or is likely to result in, physical, sexual or psychological harm or suffering to a woman, including threats of such acts, coercion or arbitrary deprivation of liberty, whether occurring in public or private life.*

## Sexual Abuse

*Actual or threatened physical intrusion of a sexual nature, whether by force or under unequal or coercive conditions.*


---
**Page 198**
---

# Annex VIII – Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Sexual Exploitation
Any actual or attempted abuse of power or position for sexual purposes, including but not limited to, profiting financially from the sexual exploitation of a victim.

## Sexual Harassment
Sexual harassment is not SEA. Sexual harassment refers to prohibited conduct in the work context and can be committed against UN staff and related personnel. In contact of the United Nations, sexual harassment primarily describes prohibited behaviour against another UN staff or related personnel, whereas SEA describes any unwelcome sexual advance, request for sexual favour, verbal or physical conduct or gesture of a sexual nature, or any other behaviour of a sexual nature that might reasonably be expected to cause offence or humiliation to another when such conduct interferes with work, is a condition of employment, or creates an intimidating, hostile or offensive environment.
